# PREFACE

THE MS. <note>abbreviation for Manuscript</note>, dating from the third century A.D., which is here edited for the first time in a single whole, has long been known to scholars. Its subject-matter—magic and medicine—is not destitute of interest. It is closely connected with the Greek magical papyri from Egypt of the same period, but, being written in demotic <note>a late stage of the ancient Egyptian language and script</note>, naturally does not reproduce the Greek hymns which are so important a feature of those papyri. The influence of purely Greek mythology also is here by comparison very slight—hardly greater than that of the Alexandrian Judaism which has supplied a number of names of Hellenistic <note>Greek-influenced</note> form to the demotic magician. Mithraism <note>a Roman mystery religion centered on the god Mithras</note> has apparently contributed nothing at all: Christianity probably only a deformed reference to the Father in Heaven. On the other hand, as might have been expected, Egyptian mythology has an overwhelmingly strong position, and whereas the Greek papyri scarcely go beyond Hermes, Anubis, and the Osiris legend, the demotic magician introduces Khons, Amon, and many other Egyptian gods. Also, whereas the former assume a knowledge of the *method of operation* <note>original: "modus operandi"</note> in divination by the lamp and bowl, the latter describes it in great detail.

But the papyrus is especially interesting for the language in which it is written. It is probably the

<summary>This preface introduces a 3rd-century Egyptian manuscript on magic and medicine, highlighting its unique use of the Demotic script and its deep roots in native Egyptian mythology compared to contemporary Greek sources.</summary>
<keywords>manuscript, Demotic, Egyptian mythology, magic, medicine, papyrology, divination, Hellenistic influence</keywords>

# PREFACE

the latest Egyptian manuscript which we possess written in the demotic script <note>Demotic is a highly cursive form of ancient Egyptian writing used for everyday documents and literature in the later periods of Egyptian history.</note>, and it presents us with the form of the language as written—almost as spoken—by the pagans at the time when the Greek alphabet was being adopted by the Christians. It must not be forgotten, too, that this is the document which contributed perhaps more than any other to the decipherment of demotic, partly through its numerous Greek glosses <note>A gloss is an explanatory note or translation of a difficult word written between lines or in the margins.</note>.

We have therefore thought that a complete edition, with special reference to its philological <note>Philology is the study of the history and development of languages.</note> importance, would be useful. The vocabulary is extensive, comprising about a thousand words. The present volume, containing the introduction, the transliteration, translation, and notes, will be followed by a complete glossary, with separate indices of Greek words, invocation names, names of animals, plants, and minerals, and a list of the glosses, etc., besides a chapter dealing with the principal grammatical forms met with in the manuscript, and a hand-copy of the text; the photographic reproduction by Hess of the pages in the British Museum and Leemans' facsimile of those at Leiden will of course preserve their independent value for reference, as, for instance, in judging the condition of the manuscript and the precise forms of the signs in particular passages.

There is considerable inconsistency in the spelling of words in the papyrus itself. So much having to be rendered more or less conventionally, while fresh light is thrown daily on the intricacies of demotic, it is probable that there are a good many inconsistencies in our transliterations, translations, and notes, in spite of the watchfulness of the excellent reader at the Clarendon Press.

# INTRODUCTION

## I. HISTORY OF THE <term>MS</term> <note>Abbreviation for manuscript.</note>

THE <term>demotic</term> <note>A late Egyptian script and language phase used primarily for legal and administrative documents, and later for literary and religious texts like this one.</note> magical papyrus of London and Leiden was discovered at Thebes with other papyri, principally Greek but dealing with subjects of a like nature, in the early part of the last century, and was bought by Anastasi, who was at that time Swedish consul at Alexandria, and made a large collection of Egyptian manuscripts. When Anastasi obtained the manuscript, it must already have been torn into two parts, and it is even probable that he obtained the two parts at different times, since he sold his Egyptian collections, including the Leiden manuscript, to the Dutch government in 1828, while the London portion was bought at the sale of his later collections at Paris in 1857 for the British Museum (No. 1072 in Lenormant’s Catalogue).

The Leiden fragment was made known to the world much earlier than that in the British Museum. Its importance for the deciphering of the demotic script by the help of the numerous <gloss>glosses</gloss> <note>Notes written between the lines or in the margins to explain or translate specific words.</note> in Graeco-Coptic characters <note>A transitional script using the Greek alphabet with a few additional characters derived from Egyptian to represent sounds unique to the Coptic language.</note> was at once perceived by the distinguished scholar Reuvens, at that time Director of the Leiden Museum of Antiquities, who proceeded to study it carefully, and in 1830 published an admirable essay ¹ in which he sketched the principal contents of the manuscript and indicated its value for the progress of demotic studies.

---

¹ <note>original: "Lettres à M. Letronne sur les papyrus bilingues et grecs, par C. J. C. REUVENS. Leide, 1830. (Première lettre, Papyrus bilingues.)"</note> *Letters to Mr. Letronne on the bilingual and Greek papyri, by C. J. C. REUVENS. Leiden, 1830. (First letter, Bilingual Papyri.)*

<summary>This page describes the 19th-century discovery and subsequent division of the Demotic Magical Papyrus between London and Leiden, highlighting the role of early scholars like Reuvens in its decipherment.</summary>
<keywords>Thebes, Anastasi, Leiden, British Museum, Reuvens, Demotic script, Graeco-Coptic glosses, manuscript history</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the history of the manuscript's study, picking up from the work of Reuvens and tracing the lineage of scholarship through Leemans, Brugsch, and the eventual reunification of the London and Leiden fragments by Dr. Pleyte and Professor Hess.</meta>

studies. He then took in hand its reproduction, and the <term>MS.</term> <note>Abbreviation for manuscript.</note> was lithographed in <term>facsimile</term> <note>An exact copy or reproduction of a document.</note> under his direction, and he had corrected the proofs of the first plate when he was cut off by a premature death in 1835; his work was carried to completion and published by his successor in the Directorship of the Museum, Leemans, in 1839¹. Heinrich Brugsch studied it closely, and drew from it most of the examples quoted in his *Demotic Grammar* published in 1855; but, although later scholars have frequently quoted from it and translated fragments of it, the manuscript has hitherto remained without complete translation, commentary, or <term>glossary</term> <note>A specialized list of terms and their meanings.</note>.

The London manuscript, however, lay from 1857 onwards almost unnoticed in the British Museum. To the late Dr. Pleyte, Leemans’ successor at Leiden, belongs the credit of discovering that the two manuscripts originally formed one. He had studied the Leiden portion, and at once recognized the handwriting of its fellow in London. Without publishing the fact, he communicated it to Professor Hess of Freiburg, when the latter was working in Leiden on the manuscript there. Professor Hess went on to London, and, having fully confirmed Dr. Pleyte’s statement, published in 1892 a reproduction of the British Museum manuscript with an introduction, including the translation of one column, and a glossary².

Reuvens in his essay dwelt at some length on the ‘<term>gnostic</term>’ <note>Relating to Gnosticism, a collection of ancient religious ideas that emphasized secret knowledge (gnosis) as the way to salvation.</note> character of the manuscript. He devoted his attention mainly to the parts which contain the <gloss>glosses</gloss> <note>Explanatory notes written between lines.</note>, and those are almost exclusively magical <term>invocations</term> <note>The act of calling upon a deity or spirit for aid or protection.</note>, among which occur the names of gods, spirits, and demons, Egyptian, Syrian, Jewish, etc., strung together in a manner similar

->CONDITION OF THE MS.<-
B 2

to those found in <term>gnostic</term> <note>Relating to Gnosticism, a collection of ancient religious movements that emphasized secret, mystical knowledge for the salvation of the soul.</note> writings and on <term>gnostic gems</term> <note>Small engraved stones or amulets used in antiquity as charms, often inscribed with powerful symbols and "barbarous" names of power.</note>. He even went so far as to associate them with the name of a particular gnostic leader, Marcus, of the second century, chiefly on the ground of his recorded use of Hebrew and Syriac names in his invocations and the combinations of vowels. In consequence the <term>MS.</term> <note>Abbreviation for manuscript.</note> has acquired the name of the 'Leiden Gnostic,' and the term 'Gnostic' has been passed on to the London MS. But as will be seen from the complete translation here published, there is nothing in the work relating to the gnostic systems—it deals with magic and medicine, and it seems a misnomer to call the MS. gnostic merely because part of the stock-in-trade of the magician and medicine-man were a number of invocation names which he either picked up from the gnostics or derived from sources common to him and them. Hence it has been thought desirable to abandon the epithet 'gnostic,' and to call the work the 'Magical Papyrus of London and Leiden' (<term>Pap. mag. LL.</term> <note>An abbreviation of the Latin *Papyrus magicus*, referring to the combined London and Leiden manuscript.</note>).

## II. CONDITION OF THE MS.

The London portion is in far better condition than the Leiden portion. The papyrus is pale in colour and the ink very black; consequently where the MS. has not suffered material damage it is easy to read, as the scribe wrote a beautiful and regular hand.

The Leiden papyrus, on the other hand, has unfortunately suffered much, as Leemans, with a view to protecting the surface, covered both <term>recto</term> <note>The "front" or primary side of a papyrus sheet, where the fibers typically run horizontally.</note> and <term>verso</term> <note>The "back" or secondary side of a papyrus sheet.</note> with <term>'vegetable' paper</term> <note>A 19th-century translucent tracing paper used for protection; however, the chemicals or adhesives often caused long-term staining.</note>, which probably could not be removed now without serious injury to the MS.; but either the paper or the adhesive matter employed with it has darkened and decayed, rendering the writing illegible in places.

<summary>This page explains why the manuscript should be called the 'Magical Papyrus of London and Leiden' rather than 'Gnostic,' and describes the physical condition of the two halves, noting that the Leiden portion has been damaged by early attempts at preservation.</summary>
<keywords>Gnosticism, Marcus the Gnostic, Magic and Medicine, Papyrology, Manuscript Conservation, Leemans, London and Leiden Papyrus</keywords>

# INTRODUCTION

In 1829, while the <term>MS.</term> <note>Abbreviation for manuscript.</note> was still in the care of <note>Caspar Reuvens (1793–1835), the first director of the Dutch National Museum of Antiquities and a pioneer in the study of Egyptian papyri.</note> Reuvens and before it had been subjected to the operation described above, he took a tracing of it which has been preserved. Although this tracing offers little help with very small details, it can be relied upon to fill in certain groups of text that are now completely lost in the original document.

The main body of the writing is on the *<term>recto</term>* <note>The "front" of the papyrus, where the fibers run horizontally.</note> of the papyrus, while on the *<term>verso</term>* <note>The "back" of the papyrus, where the fibers run vertically.</note> are written memoranda, medical prescriptions, and short invocations.

The London <term>MS.</term> is <term>Pap. No. 10070</term> of the British Museum (formerly <term>Anast. 1072</term> <note>Part of the collection sold by Giovanni Anastasi, a Swedish consul in Egypt who amassed a large collection of antiquities.</note>).

The Leiden <term>MS.</term> is known as <term>I. 383</term> (categorized among the <term>Anastasi</term> manuscripts as <term>A. 65</term>).

The London portion forms the beginning of the manuscript and connects to the Leiden portion without any gap; the tenth and last column of the London manuscript and the first of the Leiden manuscript actually form a single continuous column.

The first London column is damaged, and it is impossible to say for certain whether the manuscript began there or if there was an earlier part that is now lost. It is quite possible that it began at this point. On the other hand, it is certain that the manuscript is incomplete at the end, as the broken edge of the papyrus at Leiden shows traces of a column of writing that followed the current final column.

It is impossible to estimate how much has been lost, as the manuscript is not an original composition following a set plan, but rather a collection of <term>heterogeneous material</term> <note>Diverse or varied subjects gathered from different sources.</note> assembled without any logical order.

The two portions, if joined together, would measure roughly 5 meters (about 16½ feet) in length. In height, it averages nearly 25 centimeters (10 inches). The writing is arranged in columns, of which there are twenty-nine on the

the <term>recto</term> <note>The "front" side of the papyrus where the plant fibers run horizontally, typically the primary side used for writing.</note>, while on the <term>verso</term> <note>The "back" side of the papyrus where the fibers run vertically.</note> are thirty-three small columns or portions of columns; but these are not marked off, as are the <term>recto</term> columns, by vertical and horizontal framing lines <note>The horizontal lines on the <term>recto</term> are continuous for the whole length of the papyrus.</note>, nor are they written continuously, but they seem to have been jotted down there on account of their brevity and discontinuous character.

The <term>recto</term> columns vary somewhat in size, but average 20 x 20 cm. (8 in. square). The writing is frequently carried beyond the framing lines.

In each column of the <term>recto</term> the number of lines is on the average about thirty to thirty-three; but the number is very irregular, ranging from forty-three in one column to five in another.

### III. CONTENTS.

As has been stated above, the <term>MS.</term> <note>Abbreviation for manuscript.</note> is a compilation. An analysis of the contents will be found on page 14. From this it will be seen to consist mainly of directions for divination processes involving numerous invocations, together with erotica and medical prescriptions, in which, however, magic plays as large a part as medicine.

The <term>MS.</term> is far from being unique in regard to its contents. Fragments of similar works in <term>demotic</term> <note>A cursive Egyptian script used for legal, religious, and literary texts in the later periods of ancient Egypt.</note> exist at Paris (Louvre, No. 3229, published by Maspero, *Some Papyri of the Louvre* <note>original French: "Quelques papyrus du Louvre"</note>, 1875), and at Leiden (I. 384 <term>verso</term>, <term>Anast.</term> 75 <note>Part of the collection of Giovanni Anastasi, a Swedish consul who sold large quantities of Egyptian antiquities to European museums.</note>, published by Leemans, *Monuments of the Museum of Leiden* <note>original French: "Mons. du musée de Leide"</note>, 1842, plate 226-227) a <term>MS.</term> partly <term>demotic</term> and partly Greek, the latter portion being published by Leemans in *Greek Papyri of the Leiden Museum* <note>original Latin: "Pap. graeci mus. lugd. bat."</note> 1885, volume 2, Papyrus V, and re-edited by Dieterich, *Magical Papyri of the Leiden Museum* <note>original Latin: "Pap. Mag. Mus. Lugd. Bat."</note>. The Greek papyri containing similar texts are numerous, many examples having been pub-

# INTRODUCTION

published from the museums of Berlin, Leiden, London, and Paris by Goodwin, Parthey, Leemans, Wessely, and Kenyon.

The well-known codex of the National Library <note>original: *Bibliothèque Nationale*</note> published by Wessely in the *Memoirs of the Imperial Academy of Sciences in Vienna* <note>original: *Denkschr. Kais. Ak. Wiss. Wien*, xxxvi. 1888</note>, contains a few invocations in Old Coptic <note>An early form of the Coptic language using the Greek alphabet with additional signs, often used in pagan magical spells before the Christian era.</note> along with the Greek (compare Griffith, *Journal of Egyptian Language and Antiquity* <note>original: *A. Z.*, or *Zeitschrift für Ägyptische Sprache und Altertumskunde*</note> 1901, p. 85, and bibliography, *ibid.* p. 72).

Magic was from the earliest times largely developed by the Egyptians in relation both to the dead and the living. Under the former head fall both the pyramid texts <note>Ritual spells carved into the walls of Old Kingdom pyramids to assist the Pharaoh's soul.</note> and other texts found in the tombs, including most of the *Book of the Dead*, which consists mainly of magical invocations intended to make smooth the path of the deceased in the next world.

Magical texts for the use of the living are found in the Harris magical papyrus (edited by Chabas, 1860), the Metternich stela <note>A famous healing monument inscribed with spells to protect against venomous animals.</note> (edited by Golenischeff, 1877) and kindred stones, the Berlin papyrus edited by Erman (*Magical Spells for Mother and Child* <note>original: *Zauber-sprüche für Mutter u. Kind*, 1901</note>), etc. Reference may be made to the volume on Egyptian magic by Dr. Wallis Budge, 1899, and to a special study on vessel-divination <note>A form of scrying or "liquid-gazing" used to see visions or summon spirits.</note> by E. Lefébure, ‘The Divinatory Vase’ <note>original: ‘Le vase divinatoire’</note>, in *Sphinx*, 1902, VI. 61 sequence. Compare also Dieterich, ‘Abraxas’; Kenyon in *Catalogue of Greek Papyri in the British Museum* <note>original: *Cat. Greek Pap. in B. M.*</note>, I. 62 sequence; Miss Macdonald in *Proceedings of the Society of Biblical Archaeology* <note>original: *P. S. B. A.*</note>, xiii. 160 sequence; Wünsch, *Sethian Curse Tablets from Rome* <note>original: *Sethianische Verfluchungstafeln aus Rom*</note>, etc.

In the closely allied department of medicine, it is sufficient to refer to the Ebers papyrus, the Kahun papyri, and the Berlin medical papyrus (edited by Brugsch, *Collection of Monuments* <note>original: *Rec. Mon.* or *Recueil de monuments*</note> plates 87–107), which offer many parallels. Among the Greek medical writers it is noticeable that Alexander of Tralles <note>A 6th-century physician whose work blended traditional medicine with folk-magic and amulets.</note> seems much more closely allied to the Egyptian school, if that be represented by our manuscript <note>original: MS.</note>, than Galen <note>A famous Roman-era physician who prioritized rationalized anatomy and theory over the ritualistic or "magical" cures often found in Egyptian medicine.</note>.

<summary>This page discusses the historical and academic context of the manuscript, citing comparative magical and medical texts like the Book of the Dead and the Ebers papyrus. It notes the manuscript's relationship to both Egyptian tradition and later Greek medical thinkers like Alexander of Tralles.</summary>
<keywords>Egyptian magic, funerary texts, medical papyri, vessel-divination, scrying, Old Coptic, scholarly bibliography</keywords>

# PREVIOUS WORK ON THE MANUSCRIPT

While the content of this manuscript is certainly interesting for the history of magic and medicine, its primary importance for being published lies in its value to philology <note>The study of the history and development of languages.</note>. From the beginning, its numerous glosses <note>Interlinear translations or explanations of words.</note> have captured the interest of scholars, as they have helped determine the meaning of many demotic groups <note>Demotic is a highly cursive form of ancient Egyptian script used for everyday documents; "groups" refers to clusters of signs forming specific words.</note>. Furthermore, this is likely the latest known papyrus written in the demotic script. Most of the glosses are actually transcriptions into Coptic <note>The final stage of the Egyptian language, written using the Greek alphabet.</note>, and this also includes all the Egyptian words written in cipher <note>Secret or coded writing used to hide magical names or formulas.</note>. Consequently, this manuscript provides us with a series of very early Coptic words, including several grammatical forms of great interest. It is also possible that the text will be important for studying the different dialects used in non-Christian <note>original: "pagan"</note> Egypt, though that subject is currently too underdeveloped for us to make certain claims. The vocabulary is quite broad, featuring a number of Greek words and the names of over 100 plants, in addition to many animals and minerals.

### IV. PREVIOUS WORK ON THE MANUSCRIPT

It may be useful to list here the names of the scholars who have studied this manuscript in detail, rather than just mentioning it in passing, and to whom we are grateful for many insights:

**REUVENS.** (See above, page 1).
**LEEMANS.** Monuments, etc., text; *Egyptian Papyrus in Demotic Script, etc.* <note>original: *Aegyptische Papyrus in demotischer Schrift*</note>, 1839.
**MASPERO.** *Collection of Works* <note>original: *Rec. trav.* or *Recueil de travaux relatifs à la philologie et à l'archéologie égyptiennes et assyriennes*</note>, i. 18–40 (1870).
**REVILLOUT.** *Setna, introduction* pp. 3–48 (1877); *Egyptological Review* <note>original: *Rev. Égypt.* or *Revue Égyptologique*</note>, i. 163–172 (1880), ii. 10–15, 270–2 (1881); *Satyric Poem* <note>original: *Poème satyrique*</note> (1885).
**PLEYTE.** *Proceedings of the Society of Biblical Archaeology* <note>original: *P. S. B. A.*</note>, 1883, 149.

**MAX MÜLLER.** *Collection of Works* <note>original: *Rec. tr.*</note>, viii. 172 (1886), xiii. 149 (1890).

# INTRODUCTION

HESS. *The story of Setna* <note>original: "Setna pass."; likely referring to the Demotic Tale of Setne Khamwas.</note> (1888), *On the Pronunciation of Greek* <note>original: "Zur Aussprache des Griechischen"</note>, in *Indo-Germanic Research* <note>original: "Indo-germanische Forschungen"</note>, volume 6, page 123; *The Gnostic Papyrus of London: Introduction, Text, and Demotic-German Glossary* <note>original: "Der gnostische Papyrus von London, Einleitung, Text u. Demotisch-deutsches Glossar"</note>, 1892.
GROFF. *Memoirs of the Egyptian Institute* <note>original: "Mém. de l'Institut Égypt."</note> volume 3, page 337 and following (1897), and *Bulletin of the same institute* <note>original: "Bulletin du même"</note>, 1897, 1898.

As the London portion of the manuscript—which is actually the first part of the text—was published fifty years later than the second part held in Leiden, each publication originally had its own independent numbering for the columns, both starting from Column I. Given that many academic references already exist using the column numbers established by the earlier scholars Leemans and Hess, we would have preferred to keep the old system. However, as you will see by comparing our new hand-drawn copy of the entire manuscript with those older publications, we were forced to introduce a new, continuous numbering system. This was necessary because we realized that some sections previously thought to be separate were actually parts of the same column. For example, what Hess labeled as Column **X** and Leemans labeled as Column **I** are actually a single column. The same is true for Leemans columns **II** and **III**, and columns **IV** and **V**, as well as columns **XVI**, **XVII**, **XXII**, and **XXIII** on the verso <note>The "verso" is the back side of the papyrus scroll.</note>. A table comparing these old and new numbers can be found at the end of this volume.

## V. THE GLOSSES.

There are approximately 640 words accompanied by transcriptions in Coptic characters <note>Coptic is the final form of the ancient Egyptian language, written primarily using the Greek alphabet; these "glosses" provide a vital guide to how the Egyptian words were pronounced at the time.</note>, in addition to a few others inserted directly into the main body of the text.
Besides the standard letters of the Greek alphabet, we find the following additional characters being used:

The glosses <note>small explanatory notes or translations written between lines of text</note> were undoubtedly written by the same scribe who wrote the demotic text. It appears that he wrote these glosses before he filled in the rubrics <term>rubrics: headings or specific words written in red ink for emphasis or organization</term>. We know this because the handwriting of the demotic text and the red rubrics is unquestionably the same. While filling in the empty spaces he had left for the red ink, the scribe took the opportunity to use that same red ink to correct occasional mistakes he had made in the main black text. 

In the text, this correction process can be seen in several places. For example, in column 24, line 1, a missing <image-desc>small curved horizontal stroke</image-desc> in the second instance of the word *str* (?) was added in red. Similarly, he used red ink to add a missing determinative <note>a symbol at the end of a word that indicates its general meaning, such as 'motion' or 'animal'</note> in the last word of 28/8, a missing letter in the word *phr* (29/11), and a missing word *šn* <note>meaning 'to ask' or 'inquire'</note> inserted between the lines in 29/12. He also added a plural sign in 25/26 and the gloss **eōe** <note>original Coptic: ⲉⲱⲉ; an affirmative particle meaning 'verily' or 'yes'</note> in 28/8, which he had overlooked when first inserting the glosses in black ink.

It is true that there is often a significant difference between the Greek letters found in full Greek passages and those found in the glosses (for example, the name "papipetou" <note>original Greek: παπιπετου</note> in 15/25 and 15/29). However, this difference is likely because the full passages are written in a cursive hand where letters are joined together <note>known as ligatures</note>, whereas the glosses are written carefully with each letter formed separately. This was done to ensure they remained legible within the very narrow spaces between the lines.

These observations show that the main text and the glosses in our surviving manuscript were written by the same hand. However, this does not necessarily mean they were composed by the original author. Max Müller has argued <note>original: "Rec. tr.", referring to the journal *Collection of Works Relating to Egyptian and Assyrian Philology and Archaeology*</note> (volume 8, page 175) that the glosses must have been created by a different person because they are primarily written in the Fayumic dialect <note>A regional dialect of the Coptic language from the Faiyum Oasis, distinct from the more common Sahidic or Bohairic dialects</note>.

<summary>This page analyzes the paleography of the manuscript, concluding that the same scribe wrote both the Demotic text and the interlinear Coptic glosses, though the content of the glosses may have originated from an earlier source in the Fayumic dialect.</summary>

dialect, while the dialect of the demotic text is 'Low Sahidic' <note>original: "Untersahidisch," a term for the Achmimic dialect of Coptic used in Middle Egypt</note> (i.e. Achmimic, so called by the scholar Stern). In the *Collection of Works* <note>original: "Rec. tr.", referring to the journal Recueil de travaux relatifs à la philologie et à l'archéologie égyptiennes et assyriennes</note>, volume 13, page 152, note, he replaces the latter term with a more precise definition: "The dialect stands between Fayumic <note>a dialect from the Faiyum Oasis</note> and the Middle-Egyptian of Akhmîm, though closer to the latter." <note>original German: "Die Mundart steht zwischen Fayumisch u. dem Mittel-ägyptischen von Akhmîm, letzterem näher."</note> But it is very doubtful whether this distinction between the main text and the glosses <note>small explanatory notes written between lines</note> can be maintained. The only example quoted by Max Müller that distinctly suggests the Fayumic dialect is the note <note>original: "gloss"</note> **lo** and **l** <note>original: Greek letters λω and λ</note> over a group of characters in column 16, line 5 and column 25, line 34, which he reads as *er-w* <note>original: "(ε)ρ ο ο γ"</note>, regarding the interchange of **r** and **l** as evidence of the Fayumic dialect. But the demotic group in question does not read *er-w*, but *mr* as in *mr-’ḥ* <note>meaning "overseer of the fields" or "lesonis" priest</note> (1/17, 2/7, 14/6, 28), and the note **lo** <note>original: λω</note> represents the absolute form of the late Egyptian word which we see in its construct form in Sahidic Coptic as *leoneoe* <note>original: λεωνηωε</note> and in *lesonis* <note>original: λεσωνις, a high-ranking temple administrator</note>. From the detailed examination of the dialect (found in volume 2) it appears probable that the dialect of the text does not show any distinction from that of the glosses, and it is not necessary to look for a different author behind the scribe of the present manuscript and place the compiler at an earlier date. He may well have been one and the same person.

---

### VI. DATE.

Reuvens (in the work cited above, page 151) placed the date of the manuscript in the first half of the third century <term>A. D.</term> <note>Anno Domini / Common Era</note>, and this was repeated by Leemans.

Groff and Hess attributed it on palaeographical <term>palaeography: the study of ancient handwriting styles to determine age and origin</term> grounds to the second century; but in the light of recent additions to the knowledge of Greek <term>palaeography</term>, and the opinions based on them by scholars such as Kenyon, Grenfell, and Hunt (see the *Journal of Egyptian Language* <note>original: "A. Z.", short for Zeitschrift für Ägyptische Sprache</note>, volume 39 (1901) page 78), the third century must be accepted as the date of the manuscript. But this, of course,

<header>DATE</header>
<page-type>text</page-type>

is the date at which the papyrus was written, and merely furnishes a latest possible date <note>original Latin: *terminus ad quem*</note> for deciding as to the date of the contents.

That the whole of the papyrus, in its present state, was written by one and the same scribe—with the possible exception of the back of the 28th column <note>original: "verso XXVIII"</note>—can scarcely be a matter of doubt to any one who has studied closely the handwriting of the original manuscripts <note>original: "MSS"</note>. It must be stated, however, that the scholars Reuvens <note>Caspar Reuvens (1793–1835), an early Dutch archaeologist</note> and Leemans <note>Conrad Leemans (1809–1893), who published the first descriptions of these papyri</note> were of opinion that the explanatory notes <note>original: "glosses"</note> were written by a later hand than that of the body of the text: but this question has been discussed above (p. 9), and apart from the identity of ink, and the material proof given there, it may be added that the cursive Egyptian <term>hieratic: an older, more formal cursive form of Egyptian hieroglyphs</term> notes in column 27, line 8 are certainly written by the same hand as the numerous cursive passages scattered through the text.

The date of the contents is a much more complicated question. Written partly in <term>hieratic</term>, partly in <term>demotic: the "everyday" Egyptian script of the period</term>, and partly in Greek, they wear the aspect of a collection of various sources <note>original: "compilation"</note>, which is borne out by the varied and disconnected nature of the subject-matter.

It has been suggested that the work is a translation into <term>demotic</term> of a Greek original, and perhaps this is the first question demanding discussion. At first glance <note>original Latin: *Prima facie*</note> it may be said to be likely, as so many similar works exist in Greek. The introduction of three prayers or calls to the divine <note>original: "invocations"</note> of considerable length written in Greek characters almost compels us to accept that origin for those particular sections, namely column 4, lines 1-19; column 15, lines 24-31; and column 23, lines 7-20. It seems probable that the translator felt he could transfer to Egyptian the medical recipes <note>original: "prescriptions"</note> and preparations, while the formula of the magic spell <note>original: "incantation"</note> had to be left in the original language. Had these sections been written in Egyptian originally, it is not likely that a spell in a foreign tongue would be inserted in the place presumably of an Egyptian one.

<page-num>12</page-num>
# INTRODUCTION

And in the first named instance there is the additional evidence of two true Greek explanatory notes <note>original: "glosses"</note>, that is, not *Coptic transcriptions* of the demotic words, but Greek equivalents of the two words ‘table’ and ‘goose,’ which seem to be inserted clearly to prevent a misunderstanding of the original terms. In the second instance (column 15, lines 24-31), the original Greek lines 25-28 are immediately followed by a demotic translation of the same passage (lines 29-31), which points in the same direction. Translation from the Greek is rendered probable, outside the passages already referred to, by the transcription of Greek prescriptions and substances in column 24, lines 1-25, and on the back of the scroll <note>original: "verso"</note> in sections I, II, VIII, and IX. According to an ingenious suggestion of Max Müller <note>Wilhelm Max Müller (1862–1919), a noted German-American Egyptologist</note>, in the second section on the back, the otherwise unintelligible phrase *mᶜnes n rm* is almost certainly a mistranslation of masculine magnesia <note>original Greek: μαγνησία ἀνδρεία; likely referring to a specific mineral grade or "loadstone"</note>. Max Müller has also (in the *Collection of Works*, volume 8, pages 175-6) given strong reasons for regarding the passage in column 25, lines 23-37 as being translated from a Greek original. However, even where there are reasons for believing that the <term>demotic: the everyday Egyptian script and language of this period</term> is a translation from the Greek, the original source, in relation to magic at any rate, was probably Egyptian—certainly so in the case of the Greek passage in column 15, lines 25-28, which has itself clearly an Egyptian origin.

On the other hand, some of the chief sections of the manuscript show no traces of Greek influence, such as columns VI and XV, lines 1-20; but it would be rash to say that they are older; they may well represent only a purer Egyptian source. Max Müller has suggested that some of the magic formulas go back to the period from the Eighteenth to the Twentieth Dynasty <note>approximately 1550–1070 BCE, the era of the New Kingdom</note>. This cannot be true of more than a few phrases. The language indeed is not entirely uniform, but throughout the papyrus the vocabulary and grammar are distinctly not <term>Late Egyptian: the language phase of the New Kingdom</term>; they are ‘demotic,’ and that too

<summary>This page discusses the linguistic origins of the papyrus, arguing that while some parts are translated from Greek, the underlying magical traditions remain fundamentally Egyptian. It also notes that the grammar belongs to the Demotic period rather than the much older Late Egyptian phase.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of the manuscript's origins, moving from the Greek influences discussed on page 12 to a linguistic examination of the Egyptian scripts—Demotic and Hieratic—and what they reveal about the date the text was composed.</meta>

<page-num>13</page-num>

# DATE

The language used here is of a kind which approaches Coptic <note>The final stage of the Egyptian language, written using the Greek alphabet</note> much more closely than in any other known papyrus. Certain passages, such as the spell in column 13, lines 1–10, show some old-fashioned qualities <note>original: "archaism"</note>, but in all cases, these are mixed with more modern forms.

The use of <term>hieratic: a cursive form of hieroglyphs used primarily by priests</term> might be thought to indicate that the text is quite ancient wherever it appears. However, the writing is a strange jumble; the hieratic is inextricably, though sparingly, mixed with the <term>demotic: the common, everyday Egyptian script of this period</term>, and a single word is often written partly in hieratic and partly in demotic. Where hieratic signs occur, the language is generally no more ancient than when the demotic is used alone. In column 23, line 24, the word **Abrasax** is written in hieratic. Now, **Abrasax** is usually regarded as a typical Gnostic invocation name; the early Christian writer Irenaeus stated that it was invented by Basilides (who flourished around 125 AD). This statement is now generally regarded as an error, and the name may be older; but there is no authority for placing it in pre-Christian times <note>See Hort, under the entry "Abrasax," in Smith's *Dictionary of Christian Biography*; Dieterich, *Abrasax*, p. 46; C. Schmidt, *Gnostic Writings in the Coptic Language*, 1892, p. 562</note>.

Not many documents written in hieratic have been confirmed to be later than the first century AD; but they were plentiful at Tanis among the burnt papyri found by Professor Petrie in the house of ‘Bakakhuiu’ (also known as Asychis), the destruction of which Mr. Petrie was inclined to date to 174 AD; and Clement of Alexandria <note>original: "Clemens Alexandrinus"</note> mentions in his *Miscellanies* <note>original: "Strom. v. 237"</note> that hieratic was still being taught in the schools (around 160–220 AD). Hieroglyphic inscriptions, featuring the name of the Emperor **Decius** (reigned 249–251 AD), are found in the temple of Esneh, and the continued existence of hieroglyphics almost certainly implies that hieratic was still in use as well.

Judging by the language, it is difficult to believe that any part of the work in its present final version <note>original: "redaction"</note> is more than a century or two older than the papyrus scroll itself.

# INTRODUCTION

The contents of the papyrus may be classified as follows:—

1. **Divination**—
    (a) by the vessel of oil <note>A practice known as lecanomancy, where the practitioner gazes into a liquid to see visions</note> I-III, IX-X. 22, XIV, XVIII. 7-33, XXI. 1-9, XXII (?), XXVIII, *back of the sheet* XXII, *back of the sheet* XXVI.
    (b) by a lamp V, VI-VII, VIII. 1-12, XVI, XVII-XVIII. 6, XXV. 1-22, XXVII. 13-36, *back of the sheet* XVIII, *back of the sheet* XXIV, *back of the sheet* XXXI.
    (c) by the sun X. 22-end, XXVII. 1-12, XXIX.
    (d) by moon XXIII. 21-31.
    (e) by the Foreleg constellation <note>The Egyptian name for the Great Bear or Ursa Major</note> *back of the sheet* XVIII.
    (f) by stars? IV. 23-4.
    (g) through the priest Psash (?) VIII. 12-end.
    (h) through Imuthes <note>The deified historical figure Imhotep, worshipped as a god of healing and wisdom</note> IV. 1-22.
    (i) by dreams *back of the sheet* XVII, eye-paint XXI, invocation XXVII? XXVIII.
    (k) for thief-catching III. 29, or shipwreck (?) *back of the sheet* XV.

2. **To obtain favour and respect** XI, *back of the sheet* XXXII.
    to avert anger of a superior XV. 24-31.

3. **Erotica <note>Spells and rituals related to love, lust, and attraction</note>**—
    by potions XV. 1-21, XXI. 10-43, XXV. 23-XXVI.
    by salves XII, *back of the sheet* III. 14-16, XII-XIII. 9, XIII. 10-11, XIV, XXIII, XXV, XXX, XXXII.
    <term>attraction spell: a ritual intended to "fetch" or compel a person to come to the practitioner</term> <note>original: "αγωγιμον"</note> *back of the sheet* XVI, XVII, XIX.
    <term>separation spell: a ritual intended to break up a relationship or cause a divorce</term> <note>original: "διακοπη"</note> XIII. 1-10.

4. **Poisons, etc.**—
    blinding XIII. 11, XXIV. 30.
    soporifics <note>substances used to induce sleep</note> XXIII. 1-20, XXIV, *back of the sheet* II. 16-III. 3.
    maddening (magic) *back of the sheet* XXIX.
    slaying XXIII. 7, XXIV. 28, *back of the sheet* XXXII.
    uses of the shrew-mouse, etc. (chiefly in love magic) XIII. 11-end and *back of the sheet* XXXII.

5. **Healing**—
    poison XIX. 10-21.
    sting XX. 1-27.
    dog's bite XIX. 9, 32-40.
    bone in throat XIX. 21-32, XX. 27-33.

--- Page 31 ---
<meta>This page serves as an editorial guide for the reader, explaining the specific symbols and stylistic choices used throughout the translation and study of the Demotic papyrus.</meta>

## EXPLANATION OF SIGNS

### REGARDING THE TRANSLATION

**Restorations** are placed in *square brackets* [ ]. These represent parts of the text that were damaged or missing but have been reconstructed by the editors based on context or similar passages. **Lacunae** <note>Gaps or holes in the original papyrus material.</note> in the original, for which no restoration is suggested, are represented by dots. 

Words in *round brackets* ( ) are not in the original text but have been added by the translators to make the English sense clearer; those between **angular brackets** < > are intended to be omitted, as they represent errors made by the ancient scribe.

The **second person singular** has been rendered by 'thou' and 'thee' specifically in invocations <note>Formal prayers or spells calling upon a deity or spirit.</note>, but by 'you' in all other contexts. In the very few instances in which the **second person plural** occurs, it is indicated by the use of 'ye' or 'you' (plur.). 

The notation (*bis*) <note>Latin for "twice."</note> following a word indicates that the word is followed in the original by the sign *sp sn* <note>original: "sp sn"; an Egyptian scribal shorthand meaning "two times," instructing the reader to repeat the preceding phrase.</note>, implying that the word or phrase is to be repeated.

An accurate transcription of the **magic names** is given in the technical transliteration; in this translation, we have rendered the sounds approximately without sticking strictly to one academic system. We have generally followed the **glosses** <note>Short explanatory notes or pronunciations written above the main text, often in a different script.</note> where they exist, as we believe this is the most helpful approach for readers who are not professional Egyptian scholars.

### REGARDING THE TRANSLITERATION

For the technical system used, see the note preceding the Demotic glossary. Words transliterated with Coptic letters *between asterisks* (e.g., \*abc\*) are written in **cipher** <note>A secret code used by the ancient scribe to hide specific ingredients or names from the uninitiated.</note> in the original manuscript.

### HOW TO USE THE REFERENCES

In referring to the plates of the papyrus found in Volume II, Column I, line 1 is quoted as **1/1**. References to the **verso** <note>The back side of the papyrus sheet.</note> are marked with a V, such as **V. 2/3** (Verso, Column II, line 3), and so on.

<summary>This page provides a guide to the editorial symbols and formatting used in the translation, explaining how restorations, missing text, and secret ciphers are identified. It serves as a necessary legend for interpreting the technical layout of the magical recipes and spells.</summary>
<keywords>editorial conventions, translation signs, lacunae, restorations, magic names, Coptic cipher, papyrus references, scribal notation</keywords>

--- Page 32 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the general editorial principles of the manuscript to the specific translation and philological analysis of the first column of the papyrus. The focus shifts to a ritual invocation involving a vessel, likely for a scrying ceremony.</meta>

# TRANSLATION

### COLUMN I.

1. . . . . . and the district of Oxyrhynchus <note>original: "Pr-mze"; the capital of the nineteenth province of Upper Egypt, known in Coptic as Pemdje.</note>, saying:
2. . . . . . a chapel on his side, resting in Pelusium <note>original: "Pr-'r-'mn"; a major city in the eastern Nile Delta, known in Coptic as Peremoun.</note> . . . upon it, water . . .
3. . . . . . of . . . his toes <note>The Demotic "sḥ" likely refers to toes or fingers, compared here to serpents.</note> as living rearing serpents <note>original: "'nḫ·t"; describing a serpent poised to strike or darting forward.</note>.
4. . . . . . quickly! Let the light and the freedom <note>original: "wstn"; meaning breadth or spaciousness, here referring to the clarity of a magical vision.</note> be with this vessel.
5. . . . . . open for me the earth, open for me the underworld <note>original: "ty·t"; the Duat or realm of the dead.</note>, open for me the abyss <note>original: "nwn"; the primordial waters of Nun.</note>.
6. . . . . . great one of bronze of eternal age <note>original: "'rq-ḥḥ"; literally "finishing millions" or eternity.</note>, the gods who are in the sky who are high, come to me!
7. . . . . . light and the freedom with this my vessel.
8. . . . . . a youth <note>original: "ḥm-ḥl"; likely the young medium or assistant used in the scrying ritual.</note> whose face is bowed over this vessel (of oil), let it be successful.

---

### COLUMN I. (NOTES)

Line 1. *Restore from page 18, line 7.* The parallel text to lines 13-17 shows that more than half of the page is lost, but the heading line was probably not of full length. *Pr-mze* is **Oxyrhynchus**, capital of the nineteenth province of Upper Egypt.

Line 2. *Pr-. . . e (?).* The group suggests a reading *Pr-'r-'mn* for **Pelusium**, but the *'r (?)* sign is perhaps too upright, and it is more likely that the two signs following *Pr* are a special group for some divine name. Nothing is known of the religious importance of Pelusium, or even of its name in Egyptian, but the city is mentioned in a Greek invocation quoted below at line 12.

Line 3. *sḥ (?).* This group, here in the plural, may represent <note>Egyptian hieroglyphs representing two fingers or toes</note> *sꜥḥ*, 'toes' (Lange, *Journal of Egyptian Language*, 1896, 76). Compare Lepsius, *Book of the Dead*, chapter 42, line 9: 'my fingers and toes are as rearing serpents.' But the same group recurs in 7/2 as a masculine singular substance where the context rather suggests the meaning 'testicles.'

*'nḫ·t*, of a serpent, compare 12/17; in Egyptian Pleyte, *Turin Papyrus*, 132. 4 and (very late) Mariette, *Boulaq Papyrus*, I. Plate 9, lines 5-6. It seems to mean 'darting forward for attack.' Similar meanings, 'rise,' etc., are common, especially in late texts (Brugsch, *Dictionary*, 198-9); compare also 9/16, 10/7. But in Griffith, *Tell el Yahudiyeh*, xxv. 15, an enraged serpent *'nḫ nf·w-f* 'breathed its vapor' at a god, *'nḫ* being there a transitive verb surviving in Sahidic Coptic **ansh-te** 'breathe' (Bsciai, *Collection of Works*, vii. 25; Job ix. 18).

<summary>This page contains the opening lines of a ritual invocation, likely for scrying, calling upon the gods to open the underworld and bring light to a vessel. --- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page provides the primary translation and philological commentary for the opening column of the ritual. It continues the scrying (vessel-divination) instructions introduced previously, specifically detailing the invocation used to open the spiritual realms and prepare the medium—a young boy—to see into the vessel.</meta>

# TRANSLATION

## COLUMN I.

(1) [A vessel-divination <note>A method of scrying or fortune-telling by gazing into a bowl of liquid.</note> which a physician?] of the province of Oxyrhynchus <note>original: "Pemze"; a major city in Upper Egypt, known in Coptic as Pemdje.</note> [gave to me]. Formula: (2) ‘[O god N.] . . . . . the border of whose girdle (?) rests in Pelusium <note>original: "Peremoun"; a strategic city on Egypt's eastern border.</note>, whose face is like a spark (3) . . . . . . . . of (?) an obscene (?) cat, whose toes (?) are a rearing cobra <note>original: "uraeus"; the sacred serpent symbol of divine authority.</note> (4) . . . . . . . . . quick[-ly ?]; put light and spaciousness <note>original: "wstn"; referring to the clarity and "breadth" of the vision within the vessel.</note> in my vessel (5) . . . . . . . Open to me the earth, open to me the Underworld <note>original: "Tei"; the realm of the dead or Duat.</note>, open to me the abyss <note>original: "Nun"; the primordial waters from which the world was created.</note>, (6) . . . . . . . great . . . . . of bronze of Alkhah <note>The sacred necropolis at Abydos where the head of the god Osiris was said to be kept.</note>, ye gods that are in heaven, that are exalted, come ye (7) . . . . . . . . . . [put ?] light and spaciousness in my vessel, my (8) . . . . . . . . [this] boy <note>Ancient scrying rituals often employed a young, "pure" boy as a medium to look into the oil or water.</note>, whose face is bent over this vessel.

---

### PHILOLOGICAL NOTES

**Line 4.** *The light and the freedom (spaciousness)*; compare the Greek Leiden Papyrus V, column 5, line 17 (edited by Dieterich): "let there be depth, breadth, length, [and] light" <note>original Greek: "genestho ba(thys) pla(tos) me(kos) auge"</note> in a dream request <note>original Greek: "oneirou aitesis"</note>.

**Line 5.** Or perhaps "[Open to me, O heaven!]", "Open to me, O earth!" etc. Compare the Paris National Library Papyrus, line 1180: "Open, O heaven!" <note>original Greek: "anoigeti ourane"</note>.

*The Underworld (Tei)*: This term is transcribed as **THI** or **TH** in various ancient glossaries. In the Second Tale of Khamuas (ii. 10), the judgment of the dead takes place in the *Tei*, a term used interchangeably with *Amenti* (the West/the Land of the Dead).

*The Abyss (Nun)*: There is a question as to whether this is represented by the hieroglyphic sign of three jars in a rack above water. This sign appears only twice in this papyrus, but in one instance, it clearly runs parallel to the word *Nun*. Compare column 21, line 33 ("...and you are this great black spirit... coming forth from the abyss <note>original: hieroglyphic sign of jars over water</note>") with column 9, line 15 ("I am the serpent... coming forth from the abyss <note>original: "p nwn"</note>").

**Line 6.** *Alkhah*: A term for the necropolis at Abydos where the head of Osiris was preserved. It is spelled with an 'l' (*'lg-hh*) only once in column 15, line 30; this reflects a common phonetic swap, similar to how the Greek word for "character" <note>original: "charakter"</note> is written elsewhere in the text.

*Come ye*: The scholar Müller suggests this might be a collective command "let us come," or "come ye to me," where the "to me" has become a shortened, unaccented suffix. However, compare column 11, line 16, which explicitly writes "come ye to me."

**Line 8.** The words for "oil" <note>original: "nehe"</note> and "vessel" <note>original: "hene"</note> are confused in the writing here. Parallel passages in other manuscripts would support the use of either word in this context.

The accompanying notes provide scholarly comparisons to contemporary Greek magical papyri and clarify the geography of sacred sites like Abydos.</summary>

# TRANSLATION

## COLUMN I. (Continued)

(9) . . . . . saying: "This vessel, it is the vessel of Isis, she who goes around <note>Likely referring to the goddess Isis searching for the scattered pieces of her husband Osiris.</note> (10) . . . . . come to me within . . . my Compeller <term>ẖtr; a title for a deity—here Anubis—who 'compels' other spirits to appear for the magician.</term> saying every word . . . (11) . . . [that] <gloss>one</gloss> may open the eyes of this boy to all of them <note>The spirits or visions appearing in the liquid.</note> (12) . . . . . saying: 'I am the King My-sr, Sr-my-srpt <note>Magical names that blend Egyptian and solar attributes.</note> is my name (13) . . . . . [to] you today; for I am Syt-t'-k, Stm is my name, Stm (14) . . . . . Hrenwte L<gloss>p</gloss>pt-t-th<gloss>e</gloss> L<gloss>o</gloss>ksnth<gloss>e</gloss> Se (15) . . . . . L-Bwel <note>Perhaps a corruption of the Greek 'elthe,' meaning 'come.'</note> twice, Lwtery G<gloss>e</gloss>s<gloss>e</gloss>ntr<gloss>e</gloss> Y<gloss>e</gloss>h-<gloss>e</gloss>o (16) . . . . . [the] awe of the heaven, Ablenethnelbe <note>A famous 'voces magicae' or power name often found on magical gems.</note>, the Griffin <note>original: 'srrf'; a composite creature often associated with solar protection and vengeance.</note> (17) . . . . . [You] should say it, drawing in your voice: "O Beautiful-of-Face <note>An epithet likely referring to the sun god or Anubis in his glorified form.</note>, my Compeller."

---

### PHILOLOGICAL NOTES

**Line 10.** *Compeller* (*ẖtr*): Here and elsewhere perhaps 'compeller,' meaning him (here Anubis) who compels the gods to do the magician's will.

**Line 11.** *One* (*ua*): Note this interlinear gloss as a variant or correction of 'that you cause,' also found in line 18.

**Line 12.** *Saying "I am"*: For the essentially Egyptian practice of the magician identifying themselves with a god to give their words power, see Dieterich, *Abraxas*, page 136, and compare Iamblichus, *On the Mysteries* <note>original Latin: "de Myst."</note>, book 6, chapter 6.

*The King My-sr*: compare the corrupted Greek phrase "the King Mouisdro" <note>original Greek: "τον μουισδρω τον αναξ"</note> in the Leiden Greek Papyrus V, column 9.

*Sr-my-srpt*: These same signs recur in varying order as a divine name elsewhere in this papyrus. The knife <note>hieroglyphic sign for a knife blade</note> and hide <note>hieroglyphic sign for an animal skin with a tail</note> are the zodiacal signs of Leo (the Lion) and Aries (the Ram); the flower or seed-head is a specific marker for the 'sapnot' plant throughout this manuscript. The same divine name—composed of a lotus bud (?) <note>hieroglyphic sign for a closed lotus bud on a stem</note> with a lion and the word for a ram—appears in the *Book of the Dead*, chapter 162. A group featuring a lotus leaf, lion, and ram is depicted on several 'hypocephali' <note>rounded amulets placed under the heads of mummies to provide heat and light in the afterlife</note>. Outside our papyrus, the standard order is lotus-lion-ram, and Greek versions agree: British Museum Papyrus 121, line 499 reads: "I am the one established in Pelusium, Serphouth: Mouisro" <note>original Greek: "εγω ειμι ο εν τω Πηλουσιω καθιδρυμενος Σερφουθ: μουϊσρω"</note>. A Gnostic gem in Aberdeen contains the prayer: "Serphouth Mouisro Lailam, give me grace, success, victory" <note>original Greek: "σερφουθ μουισρω λαιλαμ δος μοι χαριν πραξιν νεικην"</note>. In this combination, the lotus, the lion, and the ram likely all represent solar attributes.

*rn-yt*: This abnormal spelling of 'name' apparently arises from a combination of older and newer forms.

# COLUMN I

> (oil); cause to succeed (9) . . . . . . . . . for this vessel-divination is the vessel-divination of Isis, when she sought <note>Likely referring to the goddess Isis searching for the body of her husband, Osiris.</note> (10) . . . . . come in to me, O my Compeller <term>ẖtr; a title for a deity (often Anubis) who "compels" or forces spirits to appear for the magician.</term> (?), for everything (11) . . . . . . . . and cause the eyes of this child to be opened to them all, (12) . . . . . . for I am the Pharaoh Lion-ram; Ram-lion-lotus is my name (13) . . . . . . . to you here today, for I am Sitta-ko, Setem <note>Meaning "Hearing" or "the Hearer."</note> is my name, Setem (14) [is my true name, etc.] Hrenoute, Lapptotha, Laxantha, Sa-(15)[risa, etc.] . . . . . Bolbouel <note>original: 'bis'; repeat the name twice.</note>, Louteri, (Klo-)Kasantra <note>Likely a reference to the Greek prophetess Cassandra.</note>, Iaho <note>A common magical invocation of the Hebrew name for God, Yahweh.</note> (16) [is my name, etc., Balkam the] dread (?) one of heaven, Ablanathanalba <note>A famous magical palindrome meaning "Thou art our Father" in Hebrew/Aramaic.</note>, the Griffin <term>srrf; a mythical beast of protection and vengeance.</term> (17) [of the shrine of God, etc.].' [You] say it, drawling (?) <note>original: 'sq'; indicates a specific ritual vocalization, perhaps a rhythmic chant or a low muttering.</note> with your voice.

---

### PHILOLOGICAL NOTES

**Line 13.** The name *Syt-t-k*: The first part is written as the "serpent" (*syt* in Coptic). The *k* element means "bull" (*ko*). While the *t* is obscure, it might be part of a phrase meaning "the bull that impregnates the cows" <note>original Egyptian: 'p' k' sty m k'-wt'</note>. In other magical papyri, this phrase is sometimes substituted with a Coptic sentence meaning "I am Osiris whom Set destroyed" <note>original Coptic: 'ⲡⲉⲛⲧⲁⲥϩⲧ ⲧⲁⲕⲟ'</note>.

**Line 15.** *Kasantra*: This name appears in the Demotic of both related texts, suggesting a cultural memory of the Greek prophetess Cassandra being used in an Egyptian magical context.

**Line 16.** *Gryphon* (*srrf*): This refers to a winged quadruped with a predatory beak found in Egyptian art. Ancient texts describe it as "the image of god, the king of all that is in the world, the avenger that cannot himself be punished." Its physical form is a composite: the beak of a falcon, eyes of a human, limbs of a lion, and the tail of a serpent. It is considered the mightiest being next to God and serves as an instrument of divine vengeance, possessing authority over everything on earth, much like Death itself.

**Line 17.** *Drawl* (*sq*): This refers to an artificial way of speaking required for the spell, such as whining, chanting, or muttering. Similar instructions in Greek magical papyri call for a "compelling voice" <note>original Greek: 'ⲫⲑⲟⲅⲅⲟⲥ ⲁⲛⲁⲅⲕⲁⲥⲧⲓⲕⲟⲥ'</note> to ensure the spirits obey.

## COL. II.

1. You say to the child <term>ḥm-ḥl; a young boy chosen for his ritual purity to act as a medium.</term>: 'Open your eyes!' If his eyes open and he sees the light, you shall make him recite:
2. ->'Come, come, O Light! Shine, shine, O Light! Rise, rise, O Light! High, high, O Light! You who are outside!'<-

*The Overseer of the Cattle-pen* <note>original: 'p-mr-ʾḥ'</note> pronounced *p-le-ehe*, produces the common Ptolemaic proper name *Pelaias* <note>Greek original: Πελαῖας</note>, as is proved by a bilingual text <note>referencing Spiegelberg, Strasbourg Papyrus No. 21; the reading 'P-ers' is corrected to 'p-mr-ʾḥ'; the Greek nominative is shown in Grenfell, Amh. Pap. LI. 5</note>. The religious significance of this title, ‘the good oxherd,’ is not clear, nor has it been traced in early texts; from Column 2, line 7, it is clearly applied to Anubis, and perhaps dogs were used for herding cattle in Ancient Egypt? It is probably equivalent to the Greek word for 'shepherd' <note>original: 'ποιμήν'</note>, for which see Goodwin, Cambridge Essays, 1852. If 'the Good Shepherd' is the meaning, we may note the title 'Anubis-Christ' <note>original: 'Χριστος Ανουβις'</note> of the Leiden Greek Papyrus V. vi. 17.

line 19. *protect* <note>original: 'nḥm'</note> written with the lotus bud symbol.

line 20. <term>ret</term>. In the writing of the phonetic annotations (glosses), the breathy 'h' sound (aspirate) is suppressed before 'p'. For example, in Column 19, line 19, <term>prat</term> is written for *p-hert* ('the child'). Similar patterns appear in:
* Column 16, line 7: <term>karre</term>
* Column 28, line 9: <term>ropor</term> 
* Column 29, line 14: <term>pahot</term>
* Vol. 33, line 3: <term>araei</term>

->## COL. II.<-

(1) You say to the boy ‘Open your eyes’; when he opens his eyes and sees the light, you make him cry out, (2) saying ‘Grow (twice), O light; come forth (twice), O light; rise (twice), O light; ascend (twice), O light; you who are without, (3) come in.’ If he opens his eyes and does [not see the light, you must repeat the spell].

*Note on proper names:* Ptolemaic proper names beginning with *Phri-* mean ‘the master’ or ‘the sheikh’ (*p-ḥry-*). The pronunciation here would be *hri-to* rather than the Coptic *ret*, unless the spelling is meant to be decorative.

*Line 21:* Horus-Amon is a figure known from glazed pottery statues (see Lanzone, *Dictionary of Mythology*, 601).

*Line 23:* *sp-sn* <note>The Egyptian notation for repeating a word.</note>. It is likely that this group is used simply for emphasis—for example, after the phrase ‘exceedingly’—and here after the command ‘say to them!’ It may not literally mean to say it twice, but rather to speak with intensity.

*Line 26:* *hr r*, meaning ‘at the mouth’ or ‘opposite,’ as found in the Coptic word *hiren-*.

*Line 27:* *’bye* (honey); compare with section 9/16: or perhaps the phrase reads, ‘The truth of my mouth is the honey of my lips.’

->Col. II.<-

l. 4. ***a-l-k.*** The *a* is an addition above the line. *'l-k* would be the Coptic word for "to cease" or "to end," but the *a* prefixed suggests a different form <note>original: 'alk'; comparing the Demotic to Coptic forms to determine if it means 'to depart' or 'to cease'.</note>. The gloss may of course be incomplete, like some others.

*a-'ny*, the same formula in the Old Coptic Paris Papyrus: <note>original: "ει̅ι̅ cah̅a̅ωθ̅ και εζοτη"; translate: "Come, Sabaoth, and [reveal]..."</note>.

l. 5. *p š'y* (The Fortune-god). Occurs in the Old Coptic Paris Papyrus as "the Shai." A god whose name often occurs in Graeco-Egyptian names, like Senpsais, etc., and in the titles of the Emperor Antoninus Pius, it was translated as "Agathodaimon" <note>original: 'ἀγαθοδαίμων'; Greek for 'Good Spirit'.</note>. In the older texts, Shai seems to be mainly a god of destiny.

*p š'y nt hn p nwn* (The Fortune-god who is in the abyss) = <note>original: "ο μεγας δαιμων ο φνουνοχθονιος"; translate: "The great spirit of the subterranean abyss," from British Museum Papyrus 46.</note>.

*nšme.t*, the sacred bark of Osiris.

l. 6. *šp.* The meaning is very uncertain. It might be "the ruling star," compare the Leiden Greek Papyrus: <note>original: "ἐπικάλου τὸν τῆς ὥρας καὶ τὸν τῆς ἡμέρας θεόν"; translate: "Invoke the god of the hour and the god of the day."</note>.

...not see the light, you make him close his eyes, (4) you call to him again; the formula: "O darkness, remove yourself from before him! O light, bring the light in to me!

(5) Pshoi <term>Pshoi; the personification of Fate or Destiny, often associated with the 'Good Spirit' or Agathodaimon.</term> who is in the abyss, bring the light in to me! (8) shall give protection <note>original: 'so'; possibly referring to a protective amulet or a specific magical defense.</note> to me here today. For I am Horus, son of Isis, the good son of Osiris; you shall bring the gods of the place

(9) of judgment <term>The Hall of Judgment; the mythological space where the dead are judged by Osiris and his forty-two divine assessors.</term>, and you shall cause them to do my business, and they shall make my affair proceed; Netbeou <note>A mysterious deity or group of spirits, possibly associated with the sixteen keepers of the underworld.</note>, you shall cause them to do it.

(10) For I am Touramnei, Amnei, A-a, Mes, Mes, Ornouorf, Ornouorf, Ornouorf, Ornouorf, Pahorof,

(11) .... Pahrof, Io, a little king <note>original: 'qwy n stn'; possibly a phonetic sequence intended to be spoken as 'kouiens'.</note>, Touhor; let this child prosper, whose face is bent down to this

(12) oil <insert>and you shall</insert> escort Souchos <term>Souchos; the Greek name for Sobek, the crocodile god. Here, he is invoked to appear within the ritual oil.</term> to me until he comes forth. Setem is my name, Setem is my correct name <note>original: 'rn-yt'; equivalent to the Greek phrase meaning 'the true name' found in contemporary magical papyri.</note>. For I am

(13) Lot, Moulot, Toulot, Tat,"

### COL. II

14. ...manifest yourself fearlessly <note>original: "aph-ʿo-b-ʿo-s" (Greek: ἀφόβως)</note>, without falsehood <note>original: "ʿpsewst-ʿo-s" (Greek: ἀψεύστως)</note>, and in truth <note>original: "epʿletsyʿ" (Greek: ἐπ' ἀληθείᾳ)</note>! You shall say to me...

15. Write these words seven times. You should make him <note>The child acting as the medium.</note> open his eyes so that the light may enter. He should say: "O Anubis, come within!" and you should recite the formula after him:

16. The spell: "Ai! Ryz, Mu, Ryz! <note>These are magical vocables, or 'words of power', often used for their phonetic resonance in ritual settings.</note> Ai! O Great One! Ai! O fair youth born of Heryew, daughter of the Nemet-goddess <term>Nemet-goddess; a deity possibly associated with the forces of destruction or the execution of divine judgment.</term>,

17. come to me! For you are this lotus that bloomed in the sacred lake of the temple <term>Sacred lake; most Egyptian temples featured a lake representing the primeval waters of creation from which the sun-god first rose as a lotus flower.</term>, which gives light to the entire world.

18. Hail, Anubis! Come to me, the noble, the strong, the lord of secrets of the underworld, the Pharaoh of those in the West <note>The "West" refers to the realm of the dead, where the sun sets.</note>, the great physician,

19. [...the] beauty of Osiris, whose face is firm among the gods! You shall stand in the underworld before the hand of Osiris, and you shall serve

20. [the] soul in Abydos <term>Abydos; the primary cult center of Osiris and one of the most sacred pilgrimage sites in Egypt, believed to house the god's tomb.</term>, for they all live through you! These spirits of the sacred underworld, come to the earth and reveal yourself to me!"

---

<margin>
The word "fearlessly" and others in line 14 are transcriptions of Greek words. The scribe used a specific foreign marker at the end of each. It is notable that the Demotic character <note>a vertical stroke with a left-curving tail</note> represents the Greek letter Alpha, showing its pronunciation in this period.
</margin>

<margin>
The word "aʿny" in line 15 is a rare spelling for the word "to bring." In many texts, a "false ne-" is written before certain words like "good" (nfr) or "beautiful" (nanoy=), likely due to how the sounds were perceived by the scribe.
</margin>

<margin>
The word "Ai" (ʾy) is an interjection. While similar to other calls like "Hy," this specific character of a seated man with raised arms is used distinctly here as a ritual greeting or exclamation.
</margin>

<margin>
The phrase "Ryz, Mu, Ryz" belongs to a family of magical strings found elsewhere in the papyrus, such as "Lot, Mu, Lot" or "Lyl, Mu, Lyl." These rhythmic chants were intended to bridge the gap between the human and divine.
</margin>

### COL. II

Peintat is my correct name. O great god whose name is great, (14) appear to this child without alarming or deceiving, truthfully <note>The text emphasizes the need for a "true" vision, free from the trickery of malevolent spirits or illusions.</note>.' You utter these (15) charms seven times, you make him <note>The child serving as the medium for the vision.</note> open his eyes. If the light is good and he says 'Anubis is coming in,' you call before him (Anubis). (16) Formula: 'O Riz Muriz, O To-ur-to <note>These are magical "names of power" (voces magicae) used for their ritual sound rather than literal meaning.</note>, O this beautiful male born of Herieou, the daughter of the Neme, (17) Come to me, for thou art this lotus-flower that came forth in the lotus of Pnastor <note>Likely a reference to a specific sacred location or temple pool.</note>, and that illuminates the whole earth; (18) hail! Anubis, come to me, the High, the Mighty, the Chief over the mysteries of those in the Underworld, the Pharaoh of those in the West <note>original: "Amenti"</note>, the Chief Physician, (19) the fair [son?] of Osiris, he whose face is strong among the gods, thou manifestest thyself in the Underworld before the hand of Osiris. Thou servest (20) the souls of Abydos <term>Abydos; the primary cult center of the god Osiris and a major pilgrimage site believed to be the gateway to the afterlife.</term>, for they all live by thee, these souls (namely) those of the sacred Underworld. Come to the earth, show thyself to me—

---

The name is attached to the execution-block, but here it has the determinative <term>Determinative; a non-spoken hieroglyphic symbol placed at the end of a word to clarify its meaning (e.g., a flame symbol for words related to heat).</term> of fire.

Line 17. Compare the image of the god Horus on the lotus at Erment; and in Greek papyri: "you have the form of an infant child sitting on a lotus" <note>original: "εχεις μορφην νηπιου παιδος επι λωτω καθημενος"</note>; and "the one who sits upon the lotus and illuminates the whole inhabited world" <note>original: "ο επι του λωτου καθημενος και λαμπυριδων την ολην οικουμενην"</note>.

*ssn*, *srpt*: see the discussion for the Egyptian names of the lotus. *srpt* (see column 1, line 12) is a name apparently of late introduction; *ssn* is very ancient, and both words have parallels with varied meanings in Semitic languages. From this passage one may conjecture that *srpt* is the lotus bud and *ssn* the flower.

Line 18. *wr syn*, "chief physician," an old Egyptian official title, but amongst the gods it is most applicable to Thoth. Apparently Thoth and Anubis are here united; compare the name Hermanubis <term>Hermanubis; a composite deity merging the Greek Hermes with the Egyptian Anubis, often serving as a conductor of souls.</term> and line 21. *hry-sst*, "chief over the mysteries," is another old title appropriate enough for either Thoth or Anubis.

Line 20. "For they all live by thee." Apparently Anubis was held responsible for the provision of food and care for the souls of the deceased.

### COL. II

> 21. Give [the answer] today! You are Thoth, you are he who came forth from the heart of the Great Sea. O father of fathers of all the gods <note>original: "p yt yt·w". This title is equivalent to the Greek "propator" (forefather) often found in magical papyri, and appears in early Egyptian hymns to Amon-Re.</note>! Come to the door
> 22. of my vessel <note>The bowl or jar used for divination.</note> today, and give me a true answer <note>original: "wḥ". Similar to the Greek term "chrematison," meaning to provide a divine response or oracle.</note> concerning everything I ask about, without any lies; for I am Isis
> 23. the wise woman, to whom the secret has been told. 
> 
> Formula: (say seven times). 
> 
> You say to the boy: "Tell Anubis: 
> 24. 'Go out and bring the gods in.'" When he [the boy] goes after them and brings them in, you ask the boy: "Do the gods appear?"
> 25. If he says "They have come," <insert>and you see them</insert> <note>This addition above the line is unusual; typically, the child serves as the medium because the adult magician cannot see the spirits directly.</note>, you recite before them this formula: "Waken for me, waken for me <note>original: "nhe-k". A variant of the word "nhse," meaning to wake or rouse.</note>, O Sea! Waken, O Meria!
> 26. O Five-times-Great <term>Five-times-Great; originally the title of the high priest of Thoth at Hermopolis Magna (Khmun), this became a standard epithet for the ibis-headed god Thoth himself.</term>, [magic names]. Speak with me, Thoth! Let creation fill the earth with light, O Ibis!
> 27. O you whose face is noble, noble! Enter into the heart, make the truth happen, O great god whose names are [these].' (say seven times).
> 28. Then you say to the boy: "Tell Anubis: 'Bring a table in for the gods and let them sit down.'"

---

<note>original: "p yt·w sp-sn". This is intended to be read "p yt yt·w" (Father of the fathers), a common appellation in Greek papyri as "propator." This title for the creator occurs as early as the New Kingdom in hymns to Amon-Re.</note>

<note>original: "nte-k z n-y wḥ". This formula for asking for a divine answer mirrors Old Coptic and Greek magical phrases like "enter and give an oracle" (eiselthe kai chrematison). The word "šn" (inquire) is used here much like the Greek "chrematizein," referring to both the act of asking and the divine response.</note>

<note>original: "nhe-k ... nhs·t-k". The spelling here confirms the word is "nhse" (to waken/raise). The text shows some grammatical uncertainty in how the verb endings are written compared to earlier forms of the language.</note>

### COLUMN II

(21) ...here today. You are Thoth; you are he who came forth from the heart of the great Good Spirit <note>original: "Agathodaemon". In this context, it refers to the Egyptian deity Shai, the personification of fate and destiny, often depicted as a serpent.</note>, the father of the fathers of all the gods. Come to the opening (22) of my vessel today and tell me a truthful answer to everything I shall ask about, without any falsehood. For I am Isis (23) the Wise <note>The magician identifies themselves as Isis to command authority over the spirits.</note>, the words of whose mouth come to pass.

**Formula:** (Repeat) seven times. 

You say to the boy: "Speak to Anubis, saying: (24) 'Go forth, bring in the gods!'" 

When he goes after them and brings them in, you ask the boy, saying: "Have the gods (25) come in?" 

If he says "They have come" and you see them, you cry out before them. 

**Formula:** "Rise up for me, rise up for me, O Fortune <note>original: "Pshoi" (Shai). The god of luck and destiny.</note>; rise up, Mera (26), the Great of Five <term>Great of Five: Originally a title for the high priest of Thoth at Hermopolis, it here serves as a powerful epithet for Thoth himself.</term>, [magic names: Didiou, Tenziou], do justice to me. O Thoth, let creation fill the earth with light. O you who are an ibis in (27) his noble countenance, you noble one who enters the heart <note>original: "‘q r ht". While literally "entering the heart," in early Demotic this phrase often meant "to please" or "to satisfy."</note>, let truth be brought forth, O great god whose name is great." 

Say (this) seven times. (28) You say to the boy: "Speak to Anubis, saying: 'Bring in a table for the [gods]...'"

---

### Translator's Notes on the Original Text

The name **Portis** <note>original Greek: "Πορτις"</note> mentioned in the scholarly references is a common name from the Ptolemaic period <note>The era when Greek kings ruled Egypt (305–30 BCE).</note>. It is derived from the Egyptian name *P-wer-tiu*. 

Regarding the phrase *my 're qme* in line 26: the meaning is very uncertain. It may be interpreted as "let a creation be made" or it could potentially be a magical name, *Mareqom*. 

In line 27, the phrase "enter the heart" <note>Demotic: "‘q a p ht"; Coptic: "ak-het"</note> is a standard idiom in early Demotic literature used to describe something that is pleasing or satisfying to the mind.

The text emphasizes the importance of truthfulness in the divine oracle and identifies the practitioner with the goddess Isis.</summary>

<keywords>Thoth, Anubis, Isis, vessel-divination, boy-medium, Shai, Agathodaemon, Hermopolis, ritual magic, Demotic papyrus</keywords>

### COL. III.

(1) "let them eat, let them drink, let them celebrate a festival <note>original: "ir heru nefer". You cry out (3) before him <note>The deity.</note> instantly, saying: "O Good Spirit <note>original: "Agathodaemon". A Greek term for a protective divinity of fortune or destiny, often associated with the Egyptian god Shai.</note> of today, lord of today, O you who possesses these moments!" You make the boy say (4) to Anubis: "The god who will inquire for me today, let him tell me his name." When he stands and tells his name, you ask him (5) about every matter that you wish.

**Its spirit-gathering** <term>spirit-gathering: (original: "swḥ-ʾyḥ") The technical term for the preparation of the ritual space and the physical assembly of materials to attract and house the divine presence.</term>. You take seven new bricks, before they have been moved or turned (6) over to the other side; you take them while you are ritually pure, without letting them touch the ground, and you place them in the same manner in which they were (7) originally placed; you place three tiles under the oil; and the other four tiles, you arrange them around the child without (8) letting any part of him touch the ground; or you can use seven palm-sticks and treat them in this same fashion. You also take seven clean loaves (9) and arrange them around the oil, along with seven lumps <note>original: "tyk". A term for a cake or lump, here referring to salt.</note> of salt.

(10) Oasis oil <note>original: "nḥe n whe". This refers to high-quality oil imported from the Western Oases, often used in ritual and medicinal contexts.</note> that is pure; you put it into a small vessel <note>original: "ḥmḥm". (13) You cover him with a clean tunic of fine linen <term>fine linen: (original: "šnt·t n ᶜyw·t") Corresponding to the Greek word "sindon," this refers to high-quality Egyptian flax cloth used for priestly garments and mummification.</term>.
