(16) The invocation that you recite down into his head at first, to test him by his ears, to see again if he will be profitable to proceed (17) to the vessel, saying: "Noble Ibis <note>The ibis represents Thoth, the god of wisdom and writing.</note>, Noble Hawk <note>The hawk or falcon represents Horus, the god of kingship and protection.</note>, Noble Falcon, <unclear>Aphtete</unclear>, let me be pure! Water, another Noble Ibis, Noble Hawk, (18) Noble Falcon, <unclear>Aphtete</unclear>!" You recite to me down into his head seven times. You give to me [a sign] through (19) the matter of his ears. If his two ears speak <note>Likely referring to a ringing or a sensation the boy reports.</note>, it is very greatly good. If it happens that his (20) right <unclear>ear</unclear> speaks, it is good. If it happens that his [left ear] speaks, it is bad. <note>Signs appearing on the right were traditionally considered auspicious, while the left was inauspicious.</note> A spell for the vessel quickly, so that the gods come in and they speak.

When you have finished, you make him open his eyes and you ask him about what you desire; you do this until the ninth hour of the day <note>The text originally mentions the 'seventh hour', but scholarly comparison with Coptic linguistic patterns suggests this refers to the ninth hour, or roughly 3:00 PM.</note>.

> "Noble ibis, falcon, hawk, noble and mighty, let me be purified in the manner of the noble ibis, falcon, (18) hawk, noble and mighty."

You utter this down into his head for seven times; when you utter this, then (19) his ears speak <note>This likely refers to a ringing or popping sensation in the ears, interpreted as a spiritual sign.</note>. If his two ears speak, he is very good; if it be his right ear, (20) he is good; if it be his left ear, he is bad <note>In many ancient traditions, the left side was considered "sinister" or unfavorable for receiving divine messages.</note>.

---

**Translator's Notes on the Manuscript:**

1. 14. The word "looking" is omitted from the main reading as it was a correction added above the line in the original script.
1. 15. The phrase regarding the "seventh hour" is linked to the Coptic idiom for the "ninth hour." The terms used here are feminine in the original grammar.
1. 19. A unique spelling is used here for the word "if" or "it happens," differing from the standard Demotic spelling.
1. 20. The specific word for "left" cannot yet be fully transcribed into modern letters. In older Egyptian, the words were *iby* and *smh*; in various Coptic dialects, they appear as *hbour*, *jache*, and *sbir*. The latter appears in ancient translations of the Book of Zechariah, chapter 12, verse 6.

### COL. III

21. If you wish for a true word, you put the shell of a hoopoe’s egg <note>original: "мϲεϥ" (Old Coptic: hoopoe)</note> 
or what is inside it upon the fire; then it acts immediately. A prescription to make them

### COL. III

21. ...the gods enter and tell you the answer truthfully. You put the shell of a crocodile's egg <note>original: "мϲεϥ". Though the main text says crocodile, the Old Coptic term used here often refers to a hoopoe.</note>, or that which is inside it, on the flame; it will be enchanted instantly.

**Prescription to make them** 
22. **speak:** you put a frog's head <note>original: "tp". In this context, it likely refers to the skull or the dried head of the animal.</note> on the brazier, then they speak.

**Prescription for bringing the gods in by force:** 
you put the bile 23. of a crocodile with pounded frankincense <note>original: "ꜥnte sq"</note> on the brazier.

If you wish to make them come in quickly again, you put stalks <note>original: "ẖ", literally "wood," likely referring to the dry stalks of the plant rather than seeds.</note> of anise 24. on the brazier together with the egg-shell mentioned above, then the charm works at once.

If you wish to bring in a living man, you put sulphate of copper <note>original: "gꞏlgꞏntsy", from the Greek "chalcanthos." Also known as blue vitriol, used here for its chemical properties in ritual smoke.</note> on the brazier, then he comes in.

25. If you wish to bring in a spirit <term>spirit: (original: "ʾyḥ") A ghost or supernatural entity, corresponding to the Greek "daimon," which could be helpful or harmful depending on the ritual context.</term>, you put *sa-wr* stone with *ilkh* stone <note>original: "ylḥ". The letter "h" here is written with a rare sign used only a few times in this manuscript to denote specific mineral or textile terms.</note> on the brazier, then the spirit comes in. You put the heart 26. of a hyaena or a hare, excellent, excellent.

If you wish to bring in a drowned man <term>drowned man: (original: "ḥsy") Literally meaning "favored" or "blessed." In ancient Egypt, those who died by drowning or crocodile attack were considered deified and reached a special status in the afterlife.</term>, you put a "sea-crab" stone <note>original: "grˤb n yˤm". Likely a fossilized crab or a mineral named after the sea-crayfish (Greek: "karabos").</note> on the brazier.

27. If you wish to bring in a murdered man <note>original: "rm e-f mwt". While it literally means "a man who is dead," the specific ritual context implies a victim of violence.</note>, you put ass's dung with an amulet of Nephthys <note>Nephthys is the protective goddess of the dead and the sister of Isis; her presence helps manage the spirits of the deceased.</note> on the brazier, then he comes in.

28. If you wish to make them all depart, you put ape's dung on the brazier, then they all depart to their place, and you utter their spell of dismissal also.

29. If you wish to perform a dismissal <note>original: "ny ꜥze a ẖn". The commentary suggests this phrase, literally "to bring a thief in," corresponds to Greek spells for catching a thief, but here it functions as the formula for "dismissal" (sending the gods away).</note>, you put stalks of anise on the brazier of the scribe; while you recite, you ask [them to leave].
30. Let them go(?) to their place in a good way, in a joyful way.
31. If you wish to make the gods come in to you so that the lamp-ritual <term>lamp-ritual: (original: "p ḥn pḥr") The magical operation or "enchantment" centered on the lamp or vessel.</term> works quickly: you take a shrew-mouse <note>original: "mḥrr"</note> and you drown it <note>original: "t še-f n ḥsy", literally "cause it to go as one praised." This is a ritual euphemism for drowning an animal to deify it.</note> in the milk of a black cow.
32. You put it on the brazier and it works in that very hour, and the light appears.
33. A way to make the one who is over the bowl <note>referring to the medium or the spirit within the bowl</note> work very quickly: you take a scarab beetle <note>original: "swt"</note> made of gold, sixteen [parts], four of silver, four of [green stone?],
34. four of *seshet*-stone, four of *atme*-stone. You make them into one scarab, and you smear them with the blood of a hoopoe bird. You tie it with a thread of [fine linen] at the time of the sun
35. as a consecrated thing <note>original: "ḥsy"</note>, which is wrapped in fine royal linen <note>original: "š-stn"</note>. You tie it to the forehead of the young boy who is at the vessel, and it will work quickly, so that [no word(?) of the world(?)] is hidden from him.

### COL. IV.

1. A tested treatment <note>or "prescription"</note> which the great god Imhotep <note>A deified high official and architect from the Old Kingdom, revered in later periods as a god of medicine and wisdom.</note> performed. For its ritual-vessel: you bring a bowl of olive wood...

---

The term used here refers to a withdrawal into oneself, an idea naturally associated with sleeping or lying down and best seen with the verb "to go." By itself, this verb means "to go," while "to go for oneself" <note>Coptic: "she naf"</note> means "to go away," "to go home," or "to withdraw."
line 29. "To bring a thief in" probably corresponds to the Greek phrase "to catch a thief" in British Museum Papyrus 46, line 172.
The phrase "if you ask" <note>original: "e-r-k š'ne"</note> is an isolated instance of a specific grammatical form <note>Coptic: "ekshan-"</note>. The following section describes the "dismissal" <note>Greek: "apolysis"</note> found in the Greek papyri, such as British Museum Papyrus 121, line 333.
line 31. "Cause him to go as one praised" <note>Coptic: "jou pasie"</note>—compare the Sahidic Coptic version of 1 Timothy 1:19 regarding "shipwreck" and the phrase "to go as one praised" meaning "to be drowned." The literal meaning is "thou shalt cause him to go as one praised (or pleasing)."
Similarly, in the Story of Setne Khamwas (i Kham. iv. 9), drowning is expressed as "He did that which pleased Re (the sun god)." The word "praised" <note>original: "ḥsy"</note> is written with signs for the sun or divinity.
In Greek, our expression is rendered by "deify" <note>Greek: "ektheoson"</note> or possibly "consecrate," which proves that the meaning "blessed dead"—that is, "divinized"—was not yet forgotten: "Taking a shrew-mouse, deify it in spring water, and taking two lunar beetles, deify them in river water" (Paris National Library Papyrus, line 2455); "Leave a lizard in lily [oil] until it is deified" (B. M. Pap. 121, line 629); "Deify it in [milk, etc.]" (Berlin Papyrus I, line 5).
The word "praised"—as a proper name Harsiesis—literally means "praised" or "blessed" and is a polite way of saying "drowned." No other meaning is certain in Demotic for the word when used as a noun; and that it...

<keywords>dismissal, deification, drowning, shrew-mouse, scarab, Imhotep, ritual deification, magic papyri, black cow milk, hoopoe blood</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the specific ritual instructions for vessel divination. It transitions from the conclusion of a sequence on summoning and dismissing entities—notably including a method for identifying a thief—to a new set of "spirit-gathering" techniques attributed to the deified sage Imhotep (Imuthes). (29) If you wish to bring in a thief <note>The text literally says "bring in a thief," which may refer to a spell for identifying a criminal, though some scholars suggest this section functions as a "dismissal" ritual for sending spirits away.</note>, you put crocus powder <note>Saffron</note> with alum on the brazier.

The charm which you pronounce when you (30) dismiss them to their place: "Good dispatch, joyful dispatch!"

(31) If you wish to make the gods come in to you and ensure that the vessel work its magic quickly, you take a scarab beetle and drown it <note>original: "ḥsy". Ritual drowning was believed to deify an animal, turning its spirit into a powerful intermediary.</note> in the milk of a black cow (32) and put it on the brazier; then it works magic in the moment named and the light comes.

(33) An amulet to be bound to the body of him who has the vessel, to cause it to work magic quickly. You take a band of linen of sixteen threads: four of white, four of [green], (34) four of blue, four of red, and make them into one band and stain them with the blood of a hoopoe bird, and you bind it with a scarab in its "attitude of the sun-god" <note>original: "n ʿḥ-f n p rʿ". This refers to the "solar scarab having the twelve rays" (Greek: κανθαρον ηλιακον τον τας ιβ ακτινας εχοντα). This is a biological scarab whose leg spines were interpreted as representing the sun's rays.</note>, (35) drowned, being wrapped in byssus <note>A fine, high-quality royal linen.</note>, and you bind it to the body of the boy who has the vessel and it will work magic quickly; there being nothing [in the world better] than it.

## COL. IV.

(1) A scout-spreader <note>Perhaps a "spirit-messenger" or "gatherer."</note>, which the great god Imuthes <note>Imhotep, the famous Old Kingdom architect and healer who was later deified and identified with the Greek god of medicine, Asclepius.</note> makes. Its spirit-gathering method: You bring a table made of olive-wood...

---

The use of the term "drowned" <note>original: "ḥsy"</note> implies that the animal's condition is shown by a water determinative <note>A small symbol in the script indicating the category of the word</note> added to the name on mummy tickets. We may thus be sure of its meaning in line 26, line 35, and numerous parallel passages. Applied to the god Osiris, also, the word "drowned" is quite appropriate.

l. 33. See the same list of the colors in the dictionary supplements.

l. 34. <term>n ʿḥ-f n p rʿ</term>: This refers to the "solar scarab having the twelve rays" <note>original Greek: "κανθαρον ηλιακον τον τας ιβ ακτινας εχοντα"</note>. It is a true scarab with its front legs positioned against the edge of its thorax, displaying twelve spines (four on the head and four on each leg), which were fancifully compared to the sun's rays; compare this to the hieroglyph of the sun's glory: <image-desc>a circle with radiating lines</image-desc>.

l. 35. The reading at the end is very uncertain: perhaps <term>ar-f</term>.

### COL. IV.

l. 1. Imuthes: compare historical studies on Imhotep. In British Museum Papyrus 121, line 630, he appears as "the Asclepius who is in Memphis" <note>original Greek: "τον εν Μεμφει Ασκληπιον"</note>; he also appears in the Demotic texts of Leiden.

### COL. IV

(2) [made of] wood, having four feet, upon which no man on earth has ever sat; and put it—it being clean—beside you. When you wish (3) to perform a <term>divination</term> <note>original: "inquiry-of-god," a ritual to seek divine answers</note> with it truthfully and without falsehood, behold, this is the manner of it. You put the table in a clean room (4) in the middle of the place, setting it near your head; you cover it with a tunic <note>likely a ritual cloth or garment</note> from its head to its feet, and you put four bricks (5) under the table before it, one above another(?), with a censer of clay placed before the table. You then put charcoal (6) of olive-wood on the censer and add wild-goose <note>original: "sr," referring to a domesticated duck or goose known since the Old Kingdom</note> fat that has been pounded with myrrh and "bone-of-life" <note>original: "qs-ankh"; this refers to a specific mineral or symbolic plant resin used in Egyptian magic</note>. Make these into balls (7) and put one on the brazier, laying the remainder at your side; then pronounce this spell in the <term>Greek language</term> <note>original: "wynn," literally "Ionian"</note> to it—this is the Formula—and you must spend the night without speaking (8) to anyone on earth. ### COL. IV

12. O great god, Barzan, Boubarzan, Narzazouzan, Barzabouzath,
13. O Sun, send to me in this night your archangel
14. Zebourthannen; give me a revelation in truth, truly, without falsehood,
15. without ambiguity, concerning this matter, for I adjure you by the one
16. who sits in the fiery cloak upon the silver <note>original: "aroureas," which scholar Mr. Kenyon suggests is a corruption of "argyreas" (silver)</note> head of the
17. Good Spirit <note>original: "Agathos Daimon," a protector deity in Hellenistic Egyptian religion</note>, the Almighty, the four-faced spirit, the Highest,
18. Dark and Soul-guiding <note>original: "psychaougeou," likely a corruption of "psychagogou," a guide of souls</note> Light <note>original: "phox," likely a corruption of "phos"</note>, do not disregard me, but send
19. [a messenger] quickly this night at the command <note>original: "epitagain"</note> of the god. Say this three times.
20. He will speak with you mouth to mouth <note>original: "r-f wbe r-k," an idiom for direct, intimate communication with a deity</note> in truth regarding every matter that you ask him. When he finishes, he departs again.
21. You should place a tablet <note>original: "pyngs," from the Greek "pinax"</note> of cedar wood... and you should place stars upon it and write your request on a new papyrus roll.
22. You place it on the tablet. It makes your stars come to you <note>this likely refers to the "stars" or planetary symbols on the tablet appearing to glow or move</note>, they being set upon your request.
23. To see a shadow <note>original: "hyb," referring to a spectral manifestation or phantom</note> when it is tested: pound the egg of a <term>thes-bird</term> <note>a species of bird, possibly a waterfowl, used in magical preparations</note> with <term>hl-oil</term> <note>a type of aromatic or medicinal oil</note>. Apply it to your eye with it; you will see the shadow.
24. Another again: [the] head and blood of a <term>hoopoe-bird</term> <note>original: "koukoupet"</note>. You should make them into a dry medicine; apply it to your eye; you will see them again.

### COL. IV

(12) ...the great god Barzan, Boubarzan, Narzazouzan, Barzabouzath, (13) the Sun; send up to me this night your archangel (14) Zebourthaunen; answer with truth, truthfully, without falsehood, without (15) ambiguity concerning this matter, for I conjure you by him (16) who is seated in the flaming vesture on the silver (?) head of the (17) <term>Agathodaemon</term> <note>original: "Agathodaemon," the "Good Spirit," a protector deity often depicted as a serpent</note>, the almighty four-faced spirit, the highest (18) darkling and soul-bringing (?) <note>original: "psychaougeou," likely a guide for the soul</note> Light <note>original: "Phox," likely a variant of the Greek "Phos" meaning light</note>; do not disregard me, but send up (19) speedily in this night a command of the god.' Say this three times.

(20) Then he speaks with you with his mouth opposite your mouth <note>original: "r-f wbe r-k," an idiom for direct, intimate communication</note> in truth concerning everything that you wish. When he has finished, and goes away again, (21) you place a tablet for reading the hours <note>original: "pyngs," a wooden tablet or "pinax" used for astronomical calculations</note> upon the bricks and you place the stars upon it and write your purpose on a new papyrus roll (22) and place it on the tablet; then he makes your stars appear which are favorable for your purpose.

(23) [A method] for "lucky-shadows" <note>this refers to seeing the shadow of the god within the lamp flame</note> that has been tested: a hawk's egg with myrrh, pound them, put some of it on your eyes, then it creates "lucky-shadows." (24) Another again: the head and blood of a hoopoe bird; cook them and make them into a dry ointment and paint your eyes with it; then you will see them again.

---

<note>original: "φωξ" (Phox)</note> "Phox" may be a magical name that does not change its form, though the customary line usually drawn over such names is missing here. Compare "the great and powerful god Phous" in the British Museum Papyrus 124, line 20, and "Phuxe" below in column 7, line 22.

Line 20. "His mouth opposite yours": similar to the phrase "mouth to mouth" found in the Berlin Papyrus I, line 39.

Line 23. "Lucky-shadows." The shadow is probably that of the god appearing in the lamp. Compare with column 6, line 6.

Line 24. "th-h-w" is probably the command for a verb meaning "cook them," also found in the Louvre Demotic Magical Papyrus, column 6, line 18.

<summary>This page concludes a summoning ritual where the practitioner communicates directly with a deity "mouth to mouth" and uses an astronomical tablet to determine favorable conditions. It also provides recipes for eye-salves made from hawk eggs or hoopoe remains to grant the ability to see divine shadows in a lamp.</summary>

<keywords>Barzan, Sun god, Zebourthaunen, Agathodaemon, Phox, direct divination, astronomical tablet, lucky-shadows, hoopoe, eye-salve, Egyptian magic</keywords>

# COL. V.

1. You should make your [...] stand, and you strike the ground with your foot seven times, and you recite these writings to the <term>Foreleg</term> <note>original: "hpš," the constellation of the Great Bear, often associated with the god Set or the destination of the soul</note> while your face is turned to the north seven times.
2. Then you turn yourself back and go to a dark room <note>original: "ry·t n kke," literally a niche or chamber of darkness, used for seclusion</note>.
3. [A method] which is tested: you go to a dark room that is clean, its face being open to the south, and you purify it with water...
4. ...and <term>natron</term> <note>original: "hsm," a natural salt used by the Egyptians for ritual purification and mummification</note>. You bring a new lamp that is white, not having had <term>red lead</term> <note>original: "prš," a red pigment; many rituals required "white" or "unpainted" lamps to ensure purity</note> or gum-water put on it, and you provide a clean wick...
5. ...into it, and you fill it with genuine oil. Then you write this name and these <term>magical characters</term> <note>original: "gh'lgter," from the Greek "charakteres," specialized symbols believed to hold cosmic power</note> on the wick with ink made of burnt myrrh.
6. And you place it on a new brick, your head being towards it, spread with sand; and you recite these writings to the lamp again seven times, while you offer <term>frankincense</term> <note>original: "lbwnt," a costly resin used to attract the divine</note> before...
7. ...the lamp. You look behind the lamp, and you will see the god in the vicinity of the lamp; then you lie down on a mat of rushes without speaking...
8. ...to anyone on earth. Then he gives you an answer in a dream. This is its recitation: (Behold, the writings which you write on the wick of the lamp: <note>original: "ϯαχυχϯυϯαχ," a magical string of sounds or power-words</note>).

---

## Col. V. Commentary

**Line 1.** There seem to be traces of writing above this line, at least towards the left end: compare the top line in Column VIII. Lines 1 and 2 are probably to be read in Line 3, before "thou goest to a dark niche," the phrase with which Line 2 ends. Read "your face" (or "your head") "turned to the north."
<term>North</term> <note>original: "mhty"</note>: a word occurring as early as the time of Darius, possibly arising from a confusion of the words for "north" and "to go north." An instance of careless confusion of sounds by our scribe (specifically the 'h' sounds) occurs elsewhere in the text, though it is of a different nature.
<term>Foreleg</term> <note>original: "hpš"</note>: literally the "foreleg," referring to the Great Bear constellation. This corresponds to "Arcturus" in the Book of Job 9:9 in certain translations. However, in Egyptian astronomical texts, the "Foreleg" consisting of seven stars is clearly the constellation of the <term>Bear</term> <note>original: "arktos"</note> itself.

**Line 3.** The grammar here suggests either a continuation of directions or a general rule for the practitioner.

**Line 4.** <term>Red lead</term> <note>original: "prš"</note>: either red earth or red lead (minium). The requirement that the lamp used for divination shall be free from red color (Greek: <note>original: "amiltotos," meaning 'not marked with red'</note>) is found in other Greek magical papyri, such as the Leiden Papyrus.

<summary>This page outlines a ritual for obtaining a divine revelation through a dream. It involves rhythmic foot-stomping, invocations to the Great Bear constellation, and the preparation of a ritually pure white lamp and wick inscribed with magical characters.</summary>
<keywords>lamp divination, Great Bear, Foreleg constellation, ritual purity, dream revelation, natron, magical characters, lychnomancy</keywords>

It involves astronomical alignment with the Great Bear constellation and strict ritual purity.</meta>

# COL. V.

(1) And you set up your <term>star-chart</term> <note>original: "planisphere," a device used to map the stars</note> and you stamp on the ground with your foot seven times and recite these charms to the <term>Foreleg</term> <note>original: "hpš," the Egyptian name for the constellation Ursa Major or the Great Bear</note>, turning towards the North seven times (2) and you return down and go to a dark recess.

(3) A method for inquiry, proven by testing. You go to a dark, clean recess with its opening facing the south and you purify it with (4) <term>natron-water</term> <note>water mixed with natural salt, used for ritual cleansing</note>, and you take a new white lamp—one that has not been treated with <term>red earth</term> <note>original: "prš," a red pigment often used in pottery</note> or gum-water—and place a clean wick (5) in it. Fill it with genuine oil after writing this name and these <term>mystic symbols</term> <note>original: "charakteres," specialized magical signs</note> on the wick with <term>ink of myrrh</term> <note>original: "smyrnomelan," a ritual ink made from burnt myrrh</note> beforehand; (6) and you lay it on a new brick before you, its underside being spread with sand; and you pronounce these spells over the lamp again another seven times. You offer <term>frankincense</term> <note>original: "libanotos," a precious resin burned as an offering</note> in front of (7) the lamp and you look at the lamp; then you see the god near the lamp and you lie down on a rush mat without speaking (8) to anyone on earth. Then he gives you an answer by dream. Behold its invocation. 

**Formula:** 
<margin>Behold the spells which you write on the wick: **Bakhukhsikhukh**, and the accompanying symbols.</margin>

<note>Scholar’s Note: This ritual corresponds to the Greek Magical Papyri, Book I, line 277. Regarding the prohibition of red earth and gum in the pottery lamp, compare the references in Sacy’s works regarding Egyptian ceramic techniques.</note>

<note>Scholar’s Note on line 5: "Myrrh ink" likely refers to the specific recipe for "smyrnomelan" found in historical Greek magical texts.</note>

<note>Scholar’s Note on line 5: The term for "magical symbols" used here is a technical term for mystic characters, common in Gnostic and ritual writings of the time.</note>

<note>Scholar’s Note on line 6: The Demotic characters for "underside" or "topside" are identified here by a specific combination of strokes and a bird-like glyph.</note>

<note>Scholar’s Note on line 8: The name **Bakhukhsikhukh** likely translates to "Soul of Khukh, son of Khukh." The word "Khukh" or "Kuk" refers to the ancient Egyptian elemental god of primordial darkness. This name is frequently found in other ritual texts like the *Pistis Sophia*.</note>

9. Hail! I am <term>Murai, Muribi, Babel, Baoth, Bami</term> <note>These are "Voces Magicae," or magical names believed to hold power through their sound rather than their literal meaning</note>, the <term>Fate</term> <note>original: "Shai," the Egyptian personification of destiny</note>

(twice), <gloss>Lahy</gloss> (twice), <gloss>Bolbuely</gloss> (twice), <gloss>Att</gloss> (twice), <gloss>Buel</gloss> (twice), <gloss>Yohel</gloss> (twice), the <term>Follower of the Flame</term> <note>original: "p šmsy ḥyt"</note>
12. of the great god, he who gives light exceedingly (twice); the companion of the flame, he in whose mouth is the flame, which does not diminish; the great god who sits
13. in the flame; he who is in the nature of the flame, who is in the fate of the heaven, in whose hand is the glory and the power of the god; **reveal yourself to me**
14. here today! Just as you revealed yourself to **Moses** <note>The text uses the Greek form "Moses." In late Egyptian magic, Moses was revered as a powerful sorcerer who received direct divine revelation</note> which you did upon the mountain, before whom you created darkness and light.
15. I pray to you that you reveal yourself to me in this night and speak with me and give me an answer in truth, without falsehood, for I will praise you

19. down into the midst of the matter of this flame which is here before you—<gloss>Pabuel</gloss> (twice)—and show me the business about which I am praying

21. in over my head and give me an answer to that which I ask about, in truth, without falsehood, O great god who is upon the mountain.

22. "O Atugi! <gloss>original: Gabaon</gloss> <note>These are magical names of power, 'Gabaon' likely being a variant of the biblical Gibeon</note> Gh^c^bh-'o! Come to me into [this place], may my eyes be opened to the light this night regarding the matter

26. into a glass vial. You shall seal its mouth very securely for twenty days in a

27. bring it out and open it. You will find that some small worms have been produced

28. out again. You must then put it into a glass container and place that glass container inside a 
29. pottery vessel in a hidden place at all times. Whenever you wish to perform [a divination] 
by the lamp with it, you shall anoint your eye with this 
30. aforementioned blood. When you go in to recite the spell to the lamp, you will 
see a **divine vision** <note>original: "sšt n ntr," literally a 'form of a god' or a 'manifestation'</note> standing outside the lamp, and he will speak 
31. with you regarding the inquiry you desire. Or [alternatively], you may lie down and he will come to you. 
If he does not come to you, you must wake up and recite his **summons**.

<note>The following notes are philological observations by the original editor regarding the Demotic script and grammar.</note>

*m.* This group cannot be read as *tm* or *too*. It must be connected
with the word for **"clay."**
l. 27. *hyn-w*, meaning **"some,"** suggests the meaning of
**"a pair"** in this context.
l. 28. Regarding the word *e(r)ash-t-she-p*, see the chapter on Grammar.
*e.re*: probably the Egyptian emphasizing particle <note>represented by the hieroglyphs of a **reed leaf** and a **mouth**</note>, compare to column 7, line 1.
The meaning of the phrase **"so you should do"** here is not merely habitual but
**instructional**, acting as a command equivalent to the old Egyptian verb form used to give directions in medical texts like the <term>Papyrus Ebers</term> <note>One of the oldest and most important medical papyri from ancient Egypt, dating to roughly 1550 BCE.</note>.

# COLUMN VI

1. An inquiry of the lamp: You should go to a dark niche <margin>A niche in a wall with special orientation for magic utensils occurs in the 19th Dynasty; see Naville, *Four Stelae*.</margin> that is clean and without light, and dig out a new hole in an east 
2. wall. Bring a white lamp <note>original: "hbs ht"</note> in which no red lead or gum-water has been applied. Its wick must be pure, and you shall fill it with genuine, clean oil. 
3. You shall recite the spells of **the Adoration of Ra** <note>Ra is the ancient Egyptian Sun God; these spells likely mirror the hymns used to greet the sun at dawn in the "Book of the Dead."</note> at dawn in his rising. Bring the lamp, facing the rising sun, and recite the spells written below over it four times. 
4. You shall take it into the clean niche with the **boy** <note>Young boys were frequently used in ancient divination because their youth was equated with spiritual "purity," making them better "vessels" for seeing gods.</note>. You shall recite the spells to the boy, while he does not look at the lamp, his eyes 
5. being closed, seven times. You shall put frankincense on the clean brazier. You shall put your finger on the head of the boy, his eyes being closed. 
6. When you wish, you shall have him open his eyes toward the lamp. He will then see the **shadow of the god** <note>The "shadow" or "shade" (*haybit*) was considered a manifest part of a divine or human soul.</note> around the lamp, and he will inquire for you 
7. regarding that which you desire. You should do it at midday in a place that has no light. If it happens that you are inquiring for a <term>dried spirit</term> <note>The meaning of "’yḫ sšre" is not quite clear. In the "Second Tale of Setne Khamwas," a similar term appears.</note>, use a wick of fine 
8. linen; that is what you should put in the lamp, and you should fill it with pure <term>syr-oil</term>. If it is for another business, use a pure wick with genuine, clean oil 
9. which you put in the lamp. If you are doing it to **bring a woman to a man**, put **rose ointment** in the lamp. You must seek the lamp <insert>when it is pure</insert>.

### COLUMN VI.

(1) **An inquiry of the lamp.** You go to a clean, dark cell without light and you dig a new hole in an eastern wall (2) and you take a white lamp in which no red lead <note>original: "minium"</note> or gum-water has been used. Its wick must be clean, and you shall fill it with clean, genuine **Oasis oil** <note>Oil from the Egyptian oases (like the Kharga or Dakhla oases) was highly prized for its clarity and quality in ritual use.</note>. (3) You shall recite the spells of **Praising Ra** at dawn as he rises, and you shall bring the lamp when lighted opposite the sun and recite the spells provided below to it four times. (4) Then, take it into the cell—you must be pure, and the boy also—and you pronounce the spells to the boy. He must not look at the lamp; his eyes should be (5) closed. You shall recite this seven times. You must put pure frankincense on the brazier. You put your finger on the boy’s head while his eyes remain closed. (6) When you have finished, you make him open his eyes toward the lamp; then he will see the shadow of the god around the lamp, and he will inquire for you (7) concerning that which you desire. You must perform this at midday in a place without light. 

If it is the case that you are inquiring for a **damned spirit** <note>Likely referring to a "ghost" or the spirit of the deceased used for lower forms of divination.</note>, a wick of sail-cloth (8) is what you put in the lamp and you fill it with clean butter. If it is for some other business, a clean wick with pure, genuine oil (9) is what you put in the lamp; if you wish to do it to bring <unclear>a person</unclear>...

10. ...on a new brick, and let the boy sit upon another brick. With his eyes closed, you shall recite down upon his head four times

"Indeed, you are the lamp of royal linen of <term>Osiris</term> <note>The god of the afterlife, whose myth of death and resurrection forms the core of Egyptian funerary religion.</note>, the <term>drowned god</term> <note>original: "p ḥsy". While this word usually means 'praised' or 'blessed,' in this context it refers to those who drowned in the Nile, who were considered divinely sanctified. Osiris is the archetype of the 'drowned' one whose body was lost in the water.</note>, spun by the hand of <term>Isis</term> <note>The sister-wife of Osiris, known for her powerful magic and her role in restoring his body.</note>, and woven by the hand of <term>Nephthys</term> <note>The sister of Isis who assisted in the mummification of Osiris.</note>."
13. "Indeed, you are the first bandage that was made for Osiris, Foremost of the West <note>A common title for Osiris, meaning he is the leader of the spirits in the land of the dead, which was located in the West.</note>. Indeed, you are the great wrapping that <term>Anubis</term> <note>The jackal-headed god of mummification who prepared Osiris's body for burial.</note> brought with his own hand to the body of Osiris, the Great God."
14. "I have brought you today, O wick, to make the boy see within you, and you shall give answer to every matter about which I am asking here today."
15. "Do not fail to do that which you should do, O wick! I have taken you from the hand of the Black Body; I have filled you from the hand"
16. "of the female body. The blood of the drowned one is what I put upon you. Through the oil, the fluid of Anubis is what is poured upon you by the spells"
17. "of the Great Magician, which I recite to you. May you bring to me the god in whose hand is the authority today, and let him give me answer to every matter about which I am asking"
18. "here today, in truth without falsehood. O <term>Nut</term> <note>The sky goddess, mother of the stars and the gods.</note>, mother of water! O <term>Ipet</term> <note>A hippopotamus goddess associated with childbirth, protection, and sometimes fire.</note>, mother of fire!"

---

<note>original: "p ḥsy" — compare to line 16 and earlier notes. While 'approved' or 'praised' might seem unexpected for Osiris, it is equivalent to 'justified' or 'deified.' The sense of 'drowned' is highly applicable, as Osiris’ body was submerged in the waters. This is supported by other texts, including a Greek papyrus mentioning "the bones of Esies... of Esies (the drowned one) who was carried in the current of the river for three days and three nights." Plutarch’s account in his book 'On Isis and Osiris' (chapter 13 onwards) also confirms this.</note>
<note>original: "t.t ʾS.t ... t.t Nb.t-ḥ.t" — In both instances, a strong 't' is written at the end of the word for 'hand,' likely an archaic dual form preserved in the ritual language.</note>
<note>original: "snb a.fy" — Refers to the specific linen wrappings used by Anubis when he prepared the mummy of Osiris.</note>

<summary>This page contains a powerful incantation where the magician ritualistically identifies the lamp's wick and oil with the sacred funerary wrappings of Osiris and the "fluid of Anubis." By doing so, the physical objects are transformed into divine tools intended to compel a god to appear and answer the magician's questions through a boy medium.</summary>

### COL. VI

...from a woman to a man, rose ointment is what you should put into the lamp. You must place the lamp (10) upon a new brick, and the boy must also sit upon another brick with his eyes closed. You shall shout down into his head four times. (11) These are the spells which you recite to the lamp and to the wick beforehand, before you recite over the boy:

**The Formula:**
"Are you the unique, great wick of the linen of <term>Thoth</term> <note>The Egyptian god of wisdom, writing, and magic, believed to be the author of sacred spells.</note>? (12) Are you the fine linen robe of <term>Osiris</term> <note>The god of the dead and the afterlife.</note>, the divine <term>Drowned One</term> <note>original: "p ḥsy". In ancient Egypt, those who drowned in the Nile were considered sacred and semi-divine, mirroring the myth of Osiris whose body was lost in the water.</note>, woven by the hand of <term>Isis</term> and spun by the hand of <term>Nephthys</term> <note>The sisters of Osiris who gathered his body and performed the first mummification.</note>? (13) Are you the original bandage that was made for Osiris, <term>Foremost of the West</term> <note>original: "Khentamente". A title for Osiris as the leader of the souls in the land of the dead, which was located in the West.</note>? Are you the great wrapping with which <term>Anubis</term> <note>The jackal-headed god of embalming.</note> stretched out his hand to the body of Osiris, the mighty god? (14) I have brought you today—O, you wick!—to make the boy look into you, so that you may give a reply to every matter I ask about here today. (15) Will you not do it? <note>original: "an tm-ir", literally "is not-doing-it what you will do?" This is a ritual command phrased as a rhetorical question to compel the object to obey.</note> O wick, I have placed you in the hand of the <term>black cow</term> <note>original: "h.t kme-t". Black animals were frequently specified in Egyptian and Greek magical papyri for their association with the potency of the underworld.</note>; I have lit you in the hand (16) of the <term>female cow</term> <note>The text emphasizes the female gender to ensure ritual precision, as the words for bull and cow sounded very similar.</note>. The blood of the <term>Drowned One</term> is what I have given you for oil; the hand of Anubis is what is laid upon you. The spells (17) of the <term>Great Sorcerer</term> are those which I recite to you. Bring me the god who holds the command for today <note>The Egyptians believed a different deity presided over each of the 365 days of the year.</note> and let him give me an answer regarding everything about which (18) I inquire here today, truly and without falsehood.

Ho! <term>Nut</term> <note>The goddess of the sky and mother of the gods.</note>, mother of water! Ho! <term>Apet</term> <note>A protective hippopotamus goddess associated with fire and childbirth.</note>, mother of fire!"

### COL. VI

19. "Open for me, <term>Nut</term> <note>The goddess of the sky, often depicted as a woman arched over the earth, whose body is filled with stars.</note>, mother of the waters, mother of the gods! Open for me, O Osiris! Open for me!" 
Then you must say it and you must shout with your voice very loudly.

20. You shall say also: "Eseks, the Great One," (others say "The Heart of the Great One") seven times. If it happens that <unclear>...</unclear> these are the things you should recite 

21. alone to the lamp. Then you should sleep <note>This refers to 'incubation,' a practice where the practitioner sleeps in a sacred space to receive a prophetic dream.</note> without speaking. If it happens that a vision occurs and you wake up, you should recite 

22. its invocation and its power: "I am the face of the young prince <note>original: "p hr n srw hwnw". The practitioner identifies themselves as a youthful god or hero to command authority over the spirits.</note>, my name having been born on the noble bed 

23. in <term>Abydos</term> <note>A sacred city in Upper Egypt, the primary cult center of Osiris and believed to be the location of his tomb.</note>. I am the soul of the Great Ram <note>A reference to the 'Ba' or soul of Osiris, often manifested as a ram-headed deity.</note> who is in Abydos; I am the guardian of the Great Corpse which is in the <term>Necropolis</term> <note>original: "ww-pq". A specific sacred district within Abydos where the tomb of Osiris was thought to be located.</note>. 

24. I am he whose eye is the eye of the hawk <note>The hawk or falcon is the symbol of Horus, the son of Osiris, who watches over his father's body.</note> who watches over Osiris in the night. I am <term>Foremost of his Mountain</term> <note>original: "tp tw-f". An ancient title for Anubis, the jackal god who protects the cemetery.</note> on the high ground of Abydos. 

25. I am he who watches over the Great Corpse which is in the <unclear>...</unclear>."
<margin>In the night</margin> <gloss>The Night <note>The scribe clarified the Demotic "n grh" with the Coptic word for night, ⲡⲓϫⲱⲣϩ.</note></gloss>
Regarding the writings which you should write upon the lamp: ***baxaxcixrx*** <note>These are 'voces magicae'—magical strings of syllables or names that were believed to have power because of their sound rather than their literal meaning.</note>

26. —whose name is hidden in my heart. "Soul of Souls" is his name! <note>A powerful epithet for a supreme creator god or the glorified Osiris.</note> (Say this) formula seven times. If it happens that <unclear>...</unclear>

27. These are the things you recite. If it is an inquiry through a boy <note>Mediumship; using a young child as a scryer to look into the lamp and describe what they see.</note> that you are performing, you should recite these things mentioned above to the lamp. 

28. But you should not shout down into the head of the boy. You should turn around and recite this other spell to the lamp again: "Hail, <term>Osiris</term>, the lamp! 

29. He gives vision to those who are there; he gives vision to their faces."

The inscriptions of which she is often identified with Nut. <term>ROCHEMENTEIX</term>, <italic>Works</italic>, pp. 261, 302.

l. 20. *e-f htn*, etc. The pronunciation of both groups was probably almost identical: <italic>hetōn</italic>.

l. 23. *ww-pq*, a sacred place at Abydos, cf. <term>BRUGSCH</term>, <italic>Geographical Dictionary</italic>, 226.

l. 24. *'hm*. Perhaps, according to its ancient significance, meaning one of the mummied hawk-figures placed watching at the corners of the coffin, but in Coptic the word ⲁϩⲱⲙ has acquired the meaning 'eagle.'

<margin>*n grh*:</margin> the gloss over the ***n*** is strange, as the group evidently corresponds to the Bohairic (dialect) word for "the night."

<margin>*tp tw-f*.</margin> The old title of Anubis.

'desert,' probably in the sense of 'necropolis.'

l. 25. The name written with three hieratic signs <note>These three characters consist of a symbol for "spirit" or "light," a scarab beetle (representing the god Khepera or the concept of "becoming"), and a sign for "great" or "official."</note> is quite uncertain. The first sign probably reads *'h*, 'spirit,' but might read *wyn*, 'light,' *wbn*, 'shine' or 'rise,' or possibly *šw*, 'light,' or the god 'Shu.' The scarab may read *hpr*, 'scarab,' 'become,' the god Khepera, but hardly *t*, 'land': and the last sign *wr*, 'great.'

### COL. VI

(19) "Come unto me, <term>Nut</term><note>The goddess of the sky, considered the mother of the celestial bodies.</note>, mother of water; come <term>Apet</term><note>A hippopotamus goddess associated with protection and fire.</note>, mother of fire; come unto me <term>Yaho</term><note>A magical name derived from the Hebrew name for God, Yahweh, frequently used in multicultural magical spells of this period.</note>." You are to say this while *drawling* <note>original: "sh-sh"</note> with your voice exceedingly. 

(20) You say again: "Esex, Poe, Ef-khe-ton," or otherwise said, "Khet-on," seven times. If it is a *direct inquiry* <note>A ritual where the practitioner seeks a vision for themselves, rather than using a child as a medium.</note>, these alone are the things that you recite 

(21) to the lamp, and you lie down without speaking. But if obduracy <note>The failure of the spirit or vision to appear.</note> takes place, you must rise and recite 

(22) his summons, which is his compulsion. **Formula:** "I am the Ram's face, Youth is my name; I was born under the venerable persea tree <note>A sacred fruit tree, often associated with the sun god and the recording of royal names.</note> 

(23) in <term>Abydos</term><note>One of the most sacred cities in Egypt, the cult center of the god Osiris.</note>; I am the soul of the great chief who is in Abydos; I am the guardian of the great corpse that is in <term>U-pek</term><note>original: "U-peq," the sacred district in Abydos believed to house the tomb of Osiris.</note>; 

(24) I am he whose eyes are as the eyes of <term>Akhom</term><note>A sacred falcon or hawk, representing divine watchfulness.</note> when he watches over Osiris by night; I am <term>Teptuf</term><note>An epithet for Anubis, the jackal-headed god of embalming, meaning "He who is upon his hill."</note> upon the desert of Abydos; 

(25) I am he who watches the great corpse which is in <term>Busiris</term><note>A major cult center of Osiris in the Nile Delta.</note>; I am he who watches for the Noble-Scarab-of-Light." 

<margin>The spells that you write on the lamp: "Bakhukhsikhukh" <note>A "barbarous" or magical name intended to resonate with spiritual power.</note> (along with drawn figures)</margin>

### COL. VI (continued)

(29) The spell: "O lamp, O lamp! <term>Amun</term><note>The 'King of the Gods' in the Egyptian pantheon, whose name means 'The Hidden One.'</note> is abiding within you. O lamp, O lamp! I am

(30) calling to you, so that you might come down beneath the head of the Great Sea, the Sea of Syria <note>original: "[ḥ]r," often referring to the Syrian coast, a location significant in the myth of Isis searching for Osiris.</note>, the Sea of Osiris again. I say

(31) to you once more: you should come, for I have sent you. O lamp, be truthful to yourself, since you found <term>Osiris</term> upon his boat of papyrus <note>original: "rms," a term used for a papyrus skiff, mentioned in Greek sources as "rops."</note> and faience <note>original: "tḥn," a glittering, glazed ceramic material highly prized in Egypt.</note>,

(32) with <term>Isis</term> at his head and <term>Nephthys</term><note>The sister of Isis and Osiris, often depicted as a protective mourner.</note> at his feet, and the male gods and the female gods <note>original: "n ntr·w ḥwt·w . . . s-ḥm·tw"</note> in his company. Isis said: 'Let it be said

(33) to Osiris regarding the matters that I am asking about, so as to let the god come in whose hand is the authority <note>original: "wḥ-sḥne," meaning a decree or divine command.</note>, and let him give me an answer to every matter about which I am asking

(34) here today.' Isis said: 'Let them call to me a god'; I have sent him, he being clever <note>original: "šq-f," meaning discreet, swift, or skilled.</note> regarding the matters for which he shall come, and let him finish them.

(35) They went and they brought him to her. And you are the lamp which they brought to her. The inspiration <note>original: "hyt," referring to a magical power, terror, or divine breath; equivalent to the Greek "exorcizo."</note> of <term>Sekhmet</term><note>The fierce lioness goddess, daughter of the sun god Ra.</note> your mother and <term>Heka</term><note>The god of magic personified, sometimes associated with the god Shu.</note> your father

(36) falls upon you! You shall not be filled with <note>In this context, being "filled" implies receiving the essence or sacrificial favor of the gods.</note> Osiris and Isis, you shall not be filled with <term>Anubis</term> unless you give me an answer to every matter I am asking

(37) about here today, in truth, without telling me a lie. If you do not do that which you must do, I will not give you oil;

->COL. VII.<-

(1) I will not give you oil, I will not give you fat. O lamp, if you do it, I will give you the head of a female <note>A ritualistic bribe, potentially referring to a specific offering or the wick itself.</note> and I will provide blood."

## COL. VII.

(1) "I will not give you oil, I will not give you fat, O lamp; truly <gloss>verily</gloss><note>original gloss: "epe."</note> I will give you the body of the female...

He <note>Referring to the deity being invoked</note> would be connected with the lion-headed goddess <term>Tefnut</term><note>The goddess of moisture, often paired with Shu; like Sekhmet, she takes a leonine form.</note>, here perhaps merged with the lion-goddess Sekhmet.

Line 37. The phrase "I will not" (original: *bn e-y a*) includes an extra character which is technically incorrect for the negative future tense in Demotic, but occurs commonly in this specific papyrus. See also column 7, line 1, etc.

->COL. VII.<-

Line 1. The word "verily" (original: *e'ry*) with the Coptic gloss <gloss>epe</gloss>—apparently corresponding to the Egyptian hieroglyphs for a reed leaf, a mouth sign, and a horizontal bar—as seen in column 5, line 28: used here to mean "verily," "truly," or "but."

### COL. VII

(2) ...of the field <note>A reference to the mythical "Field of Reeds" or the primordial battleground of the gods.</note> ... so that I take your hand at the ... of the enemy. <note>original: "hft." This likely refers to the "Contest of Horus and Set." In Egyptian myth, Horus and Set fought for the throne; during the struggle, Horus's eye (representing the light of the lamp) was injured. ### COL. VII 59

...cow and put the blood (2) of the bull into you, and place your hand upon the testicles <note>A mythological reference to the castration of Set, the "enemy of Horus," which symbolizes the magician's power over chaotic forces.</note> of the enemy of Horus. Open for me, O inhabitants of the underworld! I hold the box of myrrh in my hand; (3) receive me into your presence, O spirits of <term>Aker</term><note>Aker was an ancient earth deity, often depicted as two lions, who guarded the horizon where the sun rose and set.</note> who belong to Bi-wekem <note>A sacred region in the eastern desert associated with the gods.</note>, the box of frankincense with four corners. 

O dog <note>original: "p iwiw." An epithet for the jackal-headed god Anubis.</note> who (4) is called <term>Anubis</term> by name, who rests upon the box of myrrh and whose feet are set upon the box of frankincense: let the (5) ointment come to me for the "son of the lamp" <note>original: "p šre hbs." This could refer to the lamp's wick or, more likely, the young boy acting as the medium for the god’s voice.</note>, so that he may give me an answer regarding everything I ask here today, in truth and without lies. 

(6) Io, Tabao, Soukhamamou, Akhakhanbou, Sanauani, Ethie, Komto, (7) Kethos, Basaethori, Thmila, Akhkhou! <note>These are "voces magicae," or magic words. They were often strings of vowels and non-standard names intended to resonate with the divine frequency.</note> Give me an answer to everything I ask here today. (Recite seven times).

Reveal yourself to this boy who holds my vessel today, and let him give me a true answer (11) without falsehood. 

I will glorify you in <term>Abydos</term><note>One of the oldest and most sacred cities in Egypt, considered the burial place of the god Osiris.</note>; I will glorify you in heaven before <term>Phre</term><note>The sun god Ra (Pre).</note>; I will glorify you (12) before the moon; I will glorify you on earth; I will glorify you before him who sits upon the eternal throne, the one of great glory! (13) Peteri, Peteri, Pater <note>original: "Pater," likely the Greek word for "Father."</note>, Enphe, Enphe! The god who is above heaven, in..."

<note>Historical Note: The pair Shu and Tefnut (the gods of air and moisture) were often worshipped together and, in some contexts, were identified with the dual nature of Aker, the lion-god of the horizon.</note>
<note>The animal associated with Anubis was strictly a fox or jackal, though ritual texts often describe him as being colored black, the color of the fertile Nile silt and rebirth.</note>

### COL. IX. (Translator’s Annotations)

<note>Line 1: **Khons-m-Waset-Nefer-hotep** is the title of the principal form of Khons at Thebes, famously mentioned on the "Bekhten Stela." As a moon-god, he is often merged here with Horus (the sun) to show his universal power. The "noble youth rising from the lotus" is a classic image of the newborn sun god.</note>

<note>Line 2: **Silver** is the color of the moon in Egyptian symbolism, just as gold represents the sun. **The Ethiopian** refers to Amun of Meroe, who was seen as a powerful, ancient source of magic by this time.</note>

<note>Line 3: **The four vessels of eternity** likely refers to the four cardinal directions or the "four corners" of the world, emphasizing the god's reach across all space and time.</note>

7. <margin>
l. 7. The figure described may be compared with the ram-headed scarabs having hawk’s wings which are common on late coffins. The word *bs* refers to figures often holding two **Shen rings** <note>A circle with a line at a tangent, representing eternal protection.</note> or two scepters with **Maat feathers** <note>The feather of truth and cosmic order.</note>. The phrase "serpent of eternity" matches the Greek phrase "the everlasting serpent" <note>original Greek: *ton aeizōon ophin*</note> found in the Paris Magical Papyrus.
</margin>

<margin>
l. 9. The **lebis** <note>original: *lbs*</note> is a fish. In Greek it is called a **lebias** <note>original: *lebias*</note>. In modern Arabic, the *lebis* is the *Labeo Niloticus*, a fish related to the carp. It is dark olive brown, appearing almost black.
</margin>

### COL. IX

22. of man, the spirit of the **Underworld** <note>original: *ty-t* (Duat).</note>, the spirit of the tomb, the spirits of the transfigured dead, the spirits of the deceased. Let them <insert>make</insert> a declaration to me concerning the matter today about which I am inquiring, saying, "I [am]..."

---

The snake used as a word-sign has six loops here and in another passage (21/4), but only four loops in line 16. This matches the serpent symbol for **Shay** <note>The personification of Fate or Destiny.</note>, which usually has four loops, though it varies. Shay is said to dwell in the "Abyss" (Nun). Therefore, this is the snake of Fate. However, while the sign clearly means a sacred snake, it should likely be read as **Hefe** (serpent) rather than **Shay** (the divinity of fate itself). The term *Syt* (another word for snake) is derived from the Egyptian "Son of Earth," which is represented by a <note>hieroglyphic serpent determinative with multiple loops</note> in late period texts.

**Line 16.** *Youthful*: This word is related to the Coptic terms for "to swell," "to act proudly," or more likely "youth."
*Serpent*: This is the specific word for a serpent encountered earlier in the text. Both "youthful" and "serpent" may be acting as adjectives here, following a common Coptic grammatical structure.
*Rise up*: Written here with a non-standard spelling.

**Line 17.** *Child*: The scribe likely made a mistake, writing "sister" instead of "child."

**Line 20.** *A serpent, son of Atum*: Since Atum is a form of the Sun God, Ra, this refers to the protective solar serpent.

### COL. IX

...before every god and every man that has come forth from the stone of **Ptah** <note>The creator god of Memphis, associated with craftsmen and the physical creation of the world.</note>. For I am the serpent that came forth from the **Abyss** <note>original: *Nun*; the primordial waters from which the sun god emerged at the beginning of time.</note>. I am a (16) proud **Kushite** <note>original: *Ethiopian*; in Egyptian magic, southern lands were often associated with exotic and potent power.</note>, a rearing serpent of real gold, with honey upon my lips; that which I shall say comes to pass at once. Ho! ... (17) mighty one, for I am **Anubis**, the infant; I am **Isis** and I will bind him, I am **Osiris** and I will bind him, I am **Anubis** [and I will bind] him. You will save me from every... (18) and every place of confusion. **Lasmatnout, Lesmatot** <note>Magical names of power, often called *voces magicae*, intended to compel spiritual forces.</note>, protect me, heal me, give me love, praise, and reverence in my vessel (19), my protection here today. 

Come to me, **Isis**, mistress of magic, the great sorceress of all the gods. **Horus** is before me, **Isis** behind me, **Nephthys** is my diadem <note>The practitioner surrounds themselves with the protective family of gods to ensure safety during the ritual.</note>, (20) a snake of the sons of **Atum** <note>The sun god as the creator and finisher of the universe.</note> is that which... a **Uraeus-diadem** <note>The rearing cobra worn on the foreheads of gods and kings as a protective weapon.</note> at my head; for he that shall strike me shall strike King **Mont** <note>A falcon-headed god of war from the Theban region.</note> here today... 

(21) **Mihos** <note>A lion-headed god, son of the goddess Bastet.</note>, the mighty one, shall send out a lion of the sons of **Mihos** under compulsion to fetch them to me—fetch them to me!—the souls of gods, the souls (22) of men, the souls of the **Underworld**, the souls of the horizon, the spirits, and the dead, so that they tell me the truth today concerning this matter.

---

<note>Translator's Commentary:</note>

This section refers to the poisonous serpent formed by **Isis** from the spittle of **Ra**, used to bite him and force him to reveal his secret name (a well-known myth from New Kingdom texts). For the grammatical construction, compare line 21 "a lion of the sons of Mihos" with other technical phrases in columns 10/13 and 10/10.

**Line 21:** **Mihos** is a lion-god, the son of **Bubastis** <note>The Greek name for the city of the goddess Bastet.</note>. Regarding the pronunciation of the name, see the scholar **Spiegelberg**, *Eigennamen* <note>original Latin: "Personal Names"</note>, page 4.

The phrase "fetch them to me" is repeated twice. This should be read as an imperative command; see the parallel in line 35. It is possible the repetition is a scribal error.

The word for "souls" <gloss>original: *nhar*</gloss> often omits the plural sign in writing because the word itself did not change form in the plural.

**Line 22:** The term for "the dead" uses a prefixed "n" which likely represents a doubling of the "m" sound. 

This list of spirits corresponds to the phrase "the dead and the demons" <note>original Greek: *nekues kai oi daimones*</note> found in the Paris Magical Papyrus <note>original: *Pap. Bibl. Nat. 1453*</note>.

The spell concludes by calling upon the lion-god Mihos to compel the souls of gods, men, and the dead to appear and provide truthful answers during a vessel-scrying ritual.</summary>

35. and her child... bring them to me (twice): the souls of the gods, the souls of men, the souls of the underworld <note>original: *ty·t*</note>, and the souls of the mound <note>original: *ʾhy·t*; likely referring to the primeval mound of creation or a burial mound.</note>.

## COL. X.

1. Oh spirits and the dead, let them tell me the truth today regarding the matter about which I am asking. For I am **Artemi** <note>original: *ʾrtemy*; likely a syncretic form of Artemis, perhaps viewed here as a solar or creative force</note>... in the womb, rising in the east.
2. Come to me, **Anubis**, with your beautiful face! I have come to worship you... (twice)... [South, North,] West, and East.
3. All you who dwell in the West <note>A traditional Egyptian euphemism for the realm of the dead.</note>, let them be hidden and very secret; let them be established and set right; let them be enchanted according to the command [of the Great One] of Glory. For I am:
4. **Iae, Iae-o, Iae, Iae-o, Sab-o-th, A-t-ne** <note>original: *yʿe yʿ-ʿo yʿe yʿ-ʿo sʿb-ʿo-th ʿ-t-ne*; These are "Voces Magicae" or magical names. "Sab-o-th" likely derives from the Hebrew "Sabaoth" (Hosts), commonly used in Graeco-Egyptian magic.</note>. For I cast [a spell] upon you: **Tsye Galate**,
5. **Arkhe I-o-o, Phalekmy, Iae-o, Makha-kha-y, Iee Kh-o... Kh-o-khrekhy, Ee-o-th,**
6. **Sarbyaqo, Ygra, Psibyeg, M-o-mo, Monekh, Stsyth-o, S-o-th-o-n, Na-o-n, Kharmay.** <note>These phonetic strings are intended to compel the spirits through the vibration of sacred sounds.</note>
7. By the command of all these gods whose names I have spoken here today: awaken them for me (twice)—the **Drowned** <note>original: *ḥsy·w*; those who died in the Nile and were considered sacred.</note> and the dead. Let your souls and your forms <note>original: *sšt*; the manifest image or secret shape of a spirit.</note> appear to me
8. at the mouths of my **lamp**, my **wick**, and my **oil** <note>original: *ḥbs... swt... zʿlʿ*; the physical components of the scrying ritual.</note>. Let it <gloss>the spirit</gloss> give me an answer concerning every matter that I am asking [about] here today, in truth, without [falsehood].

### COLUMN X

9. Say: "Command them! Now, now! Quickly, quickly!" <note>original: *ys sp-sn tkr sp-sn*; these are the Demotic equivalents of the Greek magical command *ede ede tachu tachu* ("now, now, quickly, quickly").</note> Let his moon-spell proceed for you
to a dark chamber <note>original: *pr n kke*; a room without light, essential for seeing the "inner light" during divination.</note> whose entrance opens to the South or the East,

10. in a clean place. You should spread it with clean sand from the Great River <note>The Nile.</note>. You should bring a new pottery vessel or
11. a new bowl of glazed ware. You should put one **lok**-measure <term>lok: an ancient liquid measure, roughly equivalent to a pint or a Hebrew 'log'.</term> of water that has stood overnight <note>original: *mw n str*; water left out to settle, often thought to absorb celestial energy.</note> or pure water into the cup, along with one **lok** of genuine oil

12. that is pure; or use oil alone without adding water. 13. at the bottom of the vessel. You should arrange three new bricks in a circle around the vessel.

14. You should place seven clean loaves of bread upon the bricks surrounding the vessel. Then, bring a clean boy, one who is "tested" <note>This suggests the boy has been ritually purified or has previously shown a faculty for seeing visions.</note>

15. in his ears <note>meaning he is sensitive to the whispers of the spirits.</note> to speak before the vessel. You should make him sit upon a new brick, and you shall sit yourself

17. and the formula over his head seven times. When you finish, you lift your hand from his eyes and <unclear>look at</unclear> the vessel. You should put your hand

### COLUMN X

20. If you desire to perform this ritual using a vessel alone <note>original: *hne w'et-k*; referring to a solo divination without the aid of a child medium.</note>, you should fill your eyes with this eye-salve <note>referring to the mixture described in previous instructions.</note>. You should [sit over the vessel?] according to what is written above, while your eyes

22. from the fourth day of the lunar month until the fifteenth day <note>original: *'h mh wz·t*, literally "the moon completing the Wedjat-eye," meaning the full moon.</note>. [This is the inquiry] using a vessel alone to see

23. the bark of Ra <note>The sun-boat in which the god Ra travels across the sky.</note>. **The Spell:** "[O]pen to me, O Heaven! O Mother of the Gods! Let [me see the bark] of Ra as it goes down and up <note>original: *hty-hnt*; this refers to the sun-boat sinking toward the horizon and rising toward the zenith.</note>

24. within it! For I am Geb, Hereditary Prince <term>Hereditary Prince: *'rpe ntr·w*; an ancient title of the earth-god Geb as the leader of the gods.</term> of the Gods. This is the prayer that I perform before Ra, my father, [concerning] the matters that have proceeded from my hand.

27. Who illuminates the soul... O lion... O darkness, O those of the East!

28. O Great Abyss! O soul of the ram! O soul of the West! O [soul of souls]! O darkness of darknesses!"

<margin>l. 22. *'h mh wz·t*, i.e. at full moon.</margin>

<margin>l. 22. *šn hne w'et* = <note>original Greek: "σκεψη δια λεκανης αυτοπτου" and "αυθο-πτικη λεκανομαντεια"</note> "inquiry by means of a vessel for seeing it oneself" or "bowl-divination." This phrase is found in various Greek magical papyri (Pap. Bibl. Nat. and B. M. Pap. XLVI).</margin>

<margin>l. 23. *hty-hnt*. The parallel text in Column 27 gives this in hieratic. *ht* means 'to float downstream' (north), and *hnt* means 'to sail upstream' (south). Together, *hty-hnt* is a compound expression meaning 'to go down and up,' or 'to move to and fro,' referring to the movement of the sun-boat through the sky and the underworld.</margin>

<margin>l. 24. *'rpe ntr·w* is a very ancient title of the god Geb.</margin>

<margin>l. 26. The number seven was considered sacred in Egypt, possibly due to Babylonian influence.</margin>

<note>Translator's note on footnotes:</note>
Compare with references 117 and following; see Lepsius, *The Book of the Dead, Preface*, page 6; Ebers Papyrus, Column 54, line 19 (18th Dynasty); the Seven Hathors <note>Goddesses who decreed the fate of newborns.</note> (19th Dynasty, Orbiney Papyrus, 9, 8; Brugsch, *Thesaurus*, 800); and the 42 Assessors in the *Book of the Dead*, Chapter 125. The appearance of seven spirits in the very ancient Chapter 17 of the *Book of the Dead* is of particular significance.

The "seven kings" are not mentioned elsewhere in known literature. The phrase "four Montus in their cities" appears in the text "May my name flourish" <note>original Latin/French ref: LIEBLEIN, x. 3</note>, and the number seven is frequently associated with the city of Hermonthis <note>The cult center of Montu.</note>, (Brugsch, *Religion*, 164; 7 Hathors and 7 Horuses).

Line 27: The reading of the name <image-desc>Egyptian hieroglyphic sequence featuring a lion-headed figure, an eye, a water ripple (n), a reed mat (p), a water ripple (n), a vertical stroke, and a seated deity figure.</image-desc> is uncertain. *Rw* is an obscure lion-god.

-> **Notes on the Text** <-

**Line 29.** "Son of Nut" might be either Osiris or Set, probably here the former: "soul (*ba*) of Nut" is a possible reading.
**Line 30.** Anepo may be "great Anubis" or "elder Anubis." The gloss <gloss>king</gloss> <note>original: *rou*</note> may be incomplete.
**Line 32.** *p-tgs-Ast*, pronounced perhaps *pteksese*. This refers to the fruit of the cornel-tree.
**Line 33.** "Wood of satisfaction" is apparently the name of an object made of wood, likely the kohl-stick. *Any* is likely the Egyptian name for "juniper." Its juice is frequently used as a writing ink in magical papyri.

### NOTES ON COLUMN XI.

Line 1. **Giving favor:** <note>original: *t hse-t*</note> This refers to a specific type of spell designed to make the practitioner liked or respected by others.

Line 2. **Form of an ape:** <note>original: *šme*</note> While the text is slightly unclear, it likely refers to the practitioner taking on the divine persona of Thoth's sacred animal to gain his power.

Line 3. **Save me from all things evil:** This is a common protective formula found in ancient Egyptian funerary and magical texts.

Line 4. **Evil spell:** <note>original: *r bn*</note> This can be translated as slander or a malicious curse cast by an enemy.

### Translator's Notes on Column XI

**Line 5:** The word *mt(?)* is written differently from the usual word for "middle" (ⲙⲏⲧⲉ) and is likely a masculine word meaning "depth." It is unlikely to be the word *mep*. The transcription suggests a hieroglyphic group <image-desc>Hieroglyphic group showing a seated deity, a bird (sr), and phonetic markers (r, i, t).</image-desc> representing a "standard" or "holy bird." The reference to the Nile (*Hapi*) suggests the inundation that brings fertility.

**Line 7:** *Pr-Re* (House of Re) is the traditional Egyptian name for Heliopolis.

**Line 8:** *nb ḥrwy*. The scribe appears to have made a pun or substitution between "hostility" and "testicles." In Egyptian mythology, the "testicles of Set" or "eyes of Horus" are often mentioned in magical protections. The name *Srpt-my-sr* is part of the "barbarous names" common in Greco-Egyptian magic.

**Line 11:** *mst-s* is a specific Demotic relative form meaning "which I hate" or "which is hated."

<summary>This page concludes a "spell for gaining favor" where the practitioner identifies themselves with a series of powerful deities like Re and Sekhmet to compel others to provide food and show respect. It utilizes "voces magicae" and mythological puns to establish the magician's cosmic authority.</summary>

Grant me praise, love, [and awe before So-and-so, son of] So-and-so today. Let him give me all good things; (10) let him provide me with sustenance and delicacies. [But as for my enemies,] the sun shall block their hearts and blind (12) their eyes; it shall cause darkness to be upon their faces. For I am *Birai... rai*—depart!—*Rai*. I am the son of Sekhmet <note>Sekhmet is the lioness goddess of war; her son is usually identified as the lion-god Mihos or the lotus-god Nefertem.</note>. (13) I am *Bikt*, the Bull of *Lat*. I am *Gat*, the son of *Gat*, whose [power is in] the Underworld, who dwells in the depths of the Great Mansion in Heliopolis <note>The Great Mansion was the name of the central temple of the Sun at Heliopolis.</note>. (14) I am the son of Heknet, the Lady of the Protective Binding <note>Likely referring to the linen bandages used in mummification or protective amulets.</note>, who ties with leather straps... [I am the] phallus which the great and mighty Powers guard.

***

**Translator’s Notes on the Text:**
*   **Blindness curse:** The threat to "blind their eyes" (line 11) mirrors ancient Egyptian and early Christian protective curses intended to neutralize enemies.
*   **The Lion Gods:** The mention of being the "Son of Sekhmet" (line 12) invokes the fierce protective power of lion deities like Nefertem or Mihos.
*   **Magical Names:** Names like *Birai* and *Gat* are examples of *voces magicae*—words that had no everyday meaning but were believed to carry immense power when spoken by a magician.

---

**Commentary Note:**
Regarding the "shrew-mouse" <note>Greek: *μύγαλος*</note> mentioned in line 15: This animal is prescribed several times in ancient magical papyri <note>specifically in the National Library of Paris collection</note> for "attraction spells." While the Coptic word for shrew-mouse is usually *alili*, this text uses the term *amem*, which is similar to a Sahidic Coptic word <note>Sahidic: *amem*</note> that the ancient Greek translation of the Old Testament <note>the Septuagint</note> used to mean "wasps" <note>Greek: *σφῆκες*</note>. However, the context here clearly links the creature to Letopolis and the god Horus.

18. the desert and its animals. Let him do everything which I shall say to him, together with [every person who shall see] me, or to whom I shall speak, or those who speak
19. to me—among every man, every woman, every child, every old man, every person [or animal or thing in the] whole land [who] shall see me in these moments today.

An oxyrhynchus fish <note>A sacred fish with a long, downturned snout, often associated with the myths of Osiris or Set.</note>—you place it (22) in first-quality lily oil, or else cinnamon-laurel oil <note>original: *tesheps-oil*</note>, or moringa oil which [has been... and you add liquid] styrax <note>A fragrant resin used as incense.</note> to it, with first-quality frank[incense]...

***

<note>Line 17: *shfet*. In historical records of a conspiracy against Pharaoh Rameses III, a similar spell was used to grant "valor and respect."</note>

<note>Line 20: The final words may be *khef neb*, meaning "every enemy." The word for "every" was written above the line as a correction. The meaning of this passage remains quite difficult to determine.</note>

<note>Line 21: *'h*. This is a standard direction for speed found at the end of many magical invocations. The fish mentioned here is treated as a masculine noun, unlike in previous sections (12/31).</note>

<note>Line 22: The word for "lotus" (*seshen*) may be a mistake for "lily oil," which the Greek physician Dioscorides (Book 1, chapter 62) calls *sousinon*, made from lilies. The cinnamon-laurel oil (*tesheps*) is derived from the *Laurus cinnamomum* tree. Moringa oil (*beq*) and styrax (*nenehe*) are also common ingredients in ancient perfumery.</note>

## COL. XII.

1. [Another way(?) to make] a woman love a man: Opobalsamum <note>original: *hepwbˁlsˁmw*; the resinous juice of the balsam tree.</note>, 1 <term>stater</term> <note>A Greek unit of weight, roughly 14 grams.</note>; Malabathrum(?) <note>original: *ḥbʾryr(?)*; likely dried cinnamon or cassia leaves.</note>, 1 <term>stater</term>;
2. Costus <note>original: *qwšt*; an aromatic root used in incense and perfumes.</note>, 1 <term>stater</term>; ...of scent, 1 <term>stater</term>; Myrrh, 1 <term>stater</term>; "True Oil" <note>Probably high-quality olive or sesame oil.</note>, 2 <term>lek</term>-measures <note>A small liquid measure.</note>. You should grind these [medicines?].
3. You should put them into a [vessel] which is clean and put the oil on top of them on the first day of the lunar month for one day, while the lunar [month]
4. begins. You should bring a <unclear>reed/stalk</unclear>... [black?] which is 9 fingers in length—others say 7—of height, while its eye is <unclear>painted?</unclear> with the color of [the one who...]

<margin>Line 26: The prefix *pa* corresponds to the Coptic **ⲡⲁ-**. The term for "Pharaoh" (*p pr-ˁo*) appears in other papyri, such as the British Museum Papyrus XLVI, line 113: "I am the messenger of the Pharaoh Osoronnophris," <note>original Greek: *εγω ειμι αγγελος του φαπρω οσοροννωφρις*</note> where the phrase *phapro* matches our *pa p pr-o*. It is clear that the initial sound of "Pharaoh" had already been lost in speech, merging with the article. This double-naming is similar to "Amenophis, son of Hapis" <note>original: *Ἀμενωφις του Πααπιος*</note> in the writings of Josephus, *Against Apion* <note>original Latin: *Contra Ap.*</note> i. 26.</margin>

<margin>...recite. The only kings whose names end in the "sh" sound are Darius, Xerxes, Artaxerxes, and the native Egyptian king Khebbesh. Of these, Darius is almost certainly the name to be restored here. See the historian Diodorus Siculus i. 95; for his reputation as a "teacher of magic" <note>original Greek: *μαγικων διδασκαλος*</note> specifically, see Porphyry's *On Abstinence* <note>original Latin: *de Abstin.*</note>, iv. 16. That kings and those seeking the throne used these arts is suggested by a spell of "Rameses III" given to the herdsman Penhuyban to grant him "valor and respect."</margin>

*malabathrum*: <note>original: *μαλαϐαθρου*</note> meaning the leaf of the *Laurus Cassia* tree; see Sigismund.

<margin>The fish mentioned in line 9 refers to the *Latus* fish. According to scholars <note>original: MM. Lortet and Hugounenq</note>, these are found mummified in all sizes at Latopolis. In Arabic, it is called *qishr*.</margin>

<note>Regarding line 6: The original text may contain a scribal error, written as "if the two days pass" rather than "until the two days pass."</note>

<note>Regarding line 8: The sequence of 'Lotus Lion-Ram' or 'Lion-Ram Lotus' is a common ritual motif; see the notes for column 1, line 12 for the symbolic significance of these composite deities.</note>

<note>Regarding line 9: The fish is treated as feminine in the grammar here, suggesting it was sacred to a specific goddess, despite the word for fish usually being masculine.</note>

### COL. XIII.

1. The method for causing a separation <note>original: *prz*, corresponding to the Greek term *diakopos*, a ritual intended to create discord or "cut" a relationship.</note> between a man and a woman, or a woman and her husband.
2. "Away! Away! Fall! Fall! Geb <note>The ancient Egyptian god of the Earth.</note> has coupled with his... his mother Tefnut <note>The goddess of moisture; the spell uses a shocking mythological inversion—incest between Earth and Moisture—to create ritual chaos or discord.</note> again . . .
3. . . . the heart of his father; his face is the flame of the one whose breath is fire, his body being of stone and he . . .
4. Fill the land with fire, and let the mountains [shake/burn(?)]; the flame of every god and every goddess, the living, the great, the <unclear>resplendent</unclear>.
5. [Magical names: *Bareshak, Bel-kash*] . . . Cast away [Name] son of [Mother's Name] from [Name] daughter of [Mother's Name]. <note>In Egyptian magic, individuals are identified by their mother's name to ensure the spell reaches the correct person.</note>

**Translator's Commentary on the Notes:**

*Line 29:* The scribe often added omitted words above the line. The plant *sisymbrium* was well-known to ancient botanists like Dioscorides. One variety grew on land and the other in water (possibly watercress). Its association with Venus/Aphrodite explains its use in a ritual for sexual success.

*Line 31:* The "fish" mentioned here lacks a specific identifying marker in the original script but likely refers to a ritual object that must be physically buried to activate the spell's influence over a household.

*Col. XIII, Line 1:* This separation ritual mirrors similar spells found in the Leyden Greek Papyri, used to break apart couples.

*Col. XIII, Line 2:* The language here is "archaistic," meaning the scribe used deliberately old-fashioned grammar to give the spell the weight of ancient authority.
