->### COL. XIII.<-

(1) The method for separating a man from a woman and a woman from her husband. (2) "Woe! Woe! Flame! Flame! <term>Geb</term> <note>The ancient Egyptian god of the Earth.</note> assumed his form of a bull and coupled with [the daughter of(?)] his mother <term>Tefnut</term> <note>original: *coivit [cum filia ?] matris suae Tefnet*. (3) because the heart of his father cursed his face; the fury of him whose soul is as flame, while his body is like a pillar <note>The scribe uses the spelling *he-f* for "body" here, which is a variation of *het-f*.</note>, so that he . . . . . . . . . . ---

<note>The scribe employs archaic Egyptian terms here, including the obsolete word for heart (*'b*) and specific hieroglyphs—such as a couchant lion or a tall jar—to represent sounds within the Demotic script.</note>

<note>The exclamation "Woe! Woe!" (*wy sp-sn*) is a standard formula for ritual distress, appearing in other magical manuscripts like those in the Louvre.</note>

<note><term>Geb</term> is here identified with the Greek god <term>Kronos</term>. In Egyptian astronomy, the planet Saturn (Kronos) was called "Horus the Bull." <term>Nut</term> (the sky), the daughter of <term>Tefnut</term>, appears as the heavenly cow in the text known as *The Destruction of Mankind*. The restoration regarding the "mother" is uncertain but aligns with the Greek myths of Kronos and the accounts in Plutarch’s *On Isis and Osiris*.</note>

<note>The term *'n* might refer to the city of Heliopolis (On), though the symbol used by the scribe usually indicates a stone rather than a city.</note>

<note>Line 5: The phrase "is cast upon" is followed by a gap of about an inch and a half where the text is illegible, possibly erased by the original scribe.</note>

You take its **heart** <note>original: *het* (Coptic).</note> into a gold <term>seal-ring</term> <note>original: *htm*. A ring used for sealing documents, here serving as a magical amulet.</note>.

(21) You should put it on your hand and walk to any place; it will bring you [favor, love, and majesty]. You should perform a **ritual** <note>original: *heb* (Coptic). Can mean a festival or a specific ritual act.</note> of a drowned one and

(22) some wine, and you let the person drink it; it acts upon the **womb** <note>original: *metre*</note>. You take the **bone** <note>original: *kashe*</note> of [a ... lizard(?)] of the garden for any item of food.

(23) It acts upon the **womb** <note>original: *metre*</note>. You take a **butterfly** <note>original: *phaphala* (Coptic).</note>, two of them(?), into some oil. [You should cook] it and you anoint the person with it.

(24) If you wish to cause a **separation** <note>original: *paeshe*. A "division" spell intended to break a relationship.</note> over a person so that it never leaves them: a **lizard(?)** <note>original: *hantous*. (25) and you bathe the person in them. If you wish to make him **numb** <note>original: *torp*. To stun, paralyze, or render someone senseless.</note>, you should do... it makes him **numb**. You put some beer...

(26) into his eyes; it causes him **blindness** <note>original: *chope*. To be seized by blindness or a physical affliction.</note>.

(8) *Masstysinks*, **Anubis**, the greatest <note>original: "mekyste". From the Greek "megiste," meaning "greatest."</note>, *Arian*, he who is great, *Arian*, this... *Arian*

(9) he who is outside. Hail, *Phryks, Enxibroks, Ambroks, Ebroks, Xon* <note>These are "voces magicae" or words of power—strings of syllables believed to command spiritual forces through their sound alone.</note>

(10) *Nbrokhre*... the **Great Child**, **Anubis**. Say: "I am the one who is just... Masph-o-nege."

(11) "Hail! Let every word I speak happen today! For, hail! You are *Tham, Thamtham, Thamtham...*"

(12) "*Thamthamtham Thamthamotsy*, **Amon, Amon**! <note>original: "’Mn sp-sn". Amon was the king of the Egyptian gods. The notation "sp-sn" means "twice," indicating the name should be repeated for emphasis.</note> Your name is Truth, and those who speak your name say *Th-o-m*..."

(13) "I am *Th-o-m*. You are... *Thotsy* is your name. *Sythom, Anythom, Aps...* sacrifice of a black ram." <note>original: "šᶜtn-sr-km". In Egyptian magic, the color of a sacrificial animal was highly significant; black was often associated with the underworld or the regenerative power of the Nile silt.</note>

(14) "Open to me the mouths of my vessel <note>original: "hne". This refers to the divination bowl or "vessel" used to hold the liquid for scrying.</note> today! Come to me at the mouths of my vessel, my savior! Let..."

(15) "my cup be opposite the sky. Let the dogs of the whirlpool <note>original: "hwlot". A term for a specific mythological location or group of guardians associated with the deep waters of the Abyss.</note> give me that which is true in the Abyss <note>original: "p nwn". (16) "that which I am asking about today, truly, truly, without any falsehood." **a, e, ee, i, o, u, oo** <note>original: "α·ε·η·ι·ο·υ·ω". The seven Greek vowels were used in magic to represent the seven planets and the cosmic harmony of the heavens.</note> Great Spirit! <note>original: "mᶜkh-o-pnewmᶜ". Derived from the Greek "mega pneuma."</note>

(17) **Instructions:** You bring a bowl made of copper. You engrave a figure of **Anubis** inside it. You fill it with water from a spring <note>original: "mw n str". Literally "water of drawing," meaning water freshly taken from a well or spring before it has been used for other purposes.</note>...

(18) ...that the Sun god has not seen. <note>original: "b-ar p ra gmt-f". A common ritual requirement for "virgin water" or "night water," which must be kept in the dark to maintain its magical potency.</note> You pour genuine oil on top of it. You place it on a new brick which is spread...

(19) ...with sand. You place four other bricks for the **boy** <note>original: "hm-hl". Egyptian divination often employed a young boy as a medium, as their youth was equated with spiritual purity and a better connection to the gods.</note>. You make the boy lie on his belly...

(20) ...and make him place his chin on the brick of the vessel. You make him gaze into the oil while a cloth is spread over his head.

### COL. XIV

21. There being a lamp <note>original: "ḥbs"</note> at your right and a bowl <note>original: "ᶜb"</note> of fire at your . . . . then you put a sprig of the herb of

22. **Anubis** <note>original: "sym n 'Np"; the commentary identifies this as the 'Anubiada' plant, possibly a type of nightshade or nettle</note> upon the lamp. The incense which you are to put

23. on the fire: frankincense <note>original: "'lbwnt"</note>, myrrh <note>original: "mrḥe"</note>, ammoniac <note>original: "'mwnyᶜk"; the commentary notes this refers to the 'Ammoniacal herb' which produces the incense resin</note>, *trymym-t-s*, and dates; these are to be mixed with wine and you should make them into

24. small balls. You put them on the fire when you wish. You should make the child <note>original: "ḥm-hl"; a young boy acting as the medium</note> open his eyes and you ask him, saying: "Has the god come in?" If he

25. says: "The god has come in," then you recite before him the formula <note>original: "z-mt·t"</note>: "Your highness, great **Anubis**, this . . . this power,

30. the lamp away from the child, you take the vessel which contains the water, and you take the cloth [from] off him. You can perform it again

31. for a vessel-inquiry by yourself. Excellent, excellent. It has been tested nine times. The herb of **Anubis** grows in many places.

## COL. XV.

1. A drink which you prepare: Bring a little of the brain from the head of a man who has been murdered <note>original: "ḥtb·t"; referring to someone who died a violent death, a common ingredient in ancient magic believed to hold potent, unspent life force</note>
2. with seven grains of buried barley and a bone of a man who has died; you should grind them with ten [measures of] <unclear>apple-juice</unclear>.
3. Another says: nine seeds of an apple, and you take the blood of a leech <note>original: "hᶜmᶜtᶜ"</note> of a black ass and put it upon them, along with a little
4. blood from your second finger, the <term>sapien-finger<note>Likely the middle finger, which was believed in ancient medicine to have a vein leading directly to the heart or to be spiritually significant.</note></term> of your left hand, and your [semen], and you
5. mix them together at once and you put them into a cup of wine; and you say three "offerings" over it into the
6. froth of the wine, without your having tasted it and without a libation having been made from it; and you recite this spell over it seven times:
7. You cause the woman to drink it. You also bind the skin of the hyena mentioned above in a strip of royal linen
8. and bind it to your left arm. Its spell: "I am he of Abydos <note>original: "ʾBt"; the cult center of Osiris</note> in truth,
9. who completes the birth in the name of **Isis**, she of many ... the daughter of the prayer-place of Fate.
10. I am this image of **Ra**; *Setem-Asir* <note>original: "s-tᶜme-sr"; likely meaning "The one who hears Osiris"</note> is my name. I am this image of the Commander of the Army..."

---

*Horehound* <note>original: "marrubium"</note>, the latter (through a misunderstanding of the Greek word for *leek/horehound* <note>original: "πράσιον"</note>) with the leek. Here we have it compared to the *enke* <term>ⲉⲛⲕ</term> : (<term>ⲉⲛⲟⲩⲕ</term>?) *fleabane* <note>original: "κόνυζα"</note>, Bsciai, Rec. trav., vii. 25; LORET, Flora of the Pharaohs, 2nd ed., p. 68. Curiously enough, amongst the synonyms of *fleabane* in Dioscorides (III. 126), the name *anubias* occurs. Evidently there is some confusion here with the plant *stachys*.
*by wbh* probably means "tends toward whiteness."

---

## COL. XV.

l. 1. *ḥtb·t*: this might represent an Achmimic <note>A regional dialect of Coptic</note> infinitive ending in **e**, but in the *History of the Romans* no. 245 there is a feminine noun *ḥtby*.
l. 2. *a man who is dead* <note>original: "rm e-f mw·t"</note>, no doubt again a man slain or murdered. Parts of the body of those who had suffered a violent death were considered peculiarly efficacious for magical purposes. Cf. P. S. B. A. xiii. 169-70.
l. 3. *seeds* <note>original: "pr·w"</note>, a genitive "of" <note>original: "n"</note> has probably been omitted by mistake.

It also includes philological notes on plant names like horehound and fleabane used in these rituals.</summary>

(32) Its leaf is like the leaf of a Syrian plant; it turns white; its flower is like the flower of <term>fleecebane<note>original: "conyza"; a plant used in ancient medicine and ritual, often associated with driving away spirits or insects.</note></term>.

### COL. XV.

(1) A potion. You take a little shaving from the head of a man who has died a violent death <note>original: "ḥtb"; ancient practitioners believed the spirits of those who died prematurely or violently remained near their bodies and could be compelled to perform tasks</note>,
(2) together with seven grains of barley that have been buried in a grave; you pound them with ten <term>oipe-measures<note>A standard Egyptian unit of dry or liquid volume.</note></term>,
(3) or otherwise nine, of apple-seeds; you add the blood of a worm <note>possibly a leech; original: "h-t-w"</note> of a black dog to them, along with a little
(4) blood of your second finger—the one associated with the heart <note>The "physician's finger," traditionally believed to possess a vein running directly to the heart</note>—of your left hand, and with your semen, and you
(5) pound them together and put them into a cup of wine and add three <term>uteh-measures<note>A ritual pouring or a small unit of measure.</note></term> to it of
(6) the first-fruits of the vintage, taken before you have tasted it and before they have poured out any libation from it; and you pronounce this invocation to it seven times
(7) and you make the woman drink it; and you tie the skin of the <term>parasite<note>original: "syb" or "helmêt"; the text refers back to a specific creature, possibly a worm or insect, used in the previous column's preparations.</note></term> aforesaid with a band of <term>fine linen<note>original: "byssus"; high-quality linen used for sacred or royal purposes.</note></term>
(8) and tie it to your left arm. Its invocation and formula: "I am he of Abydos <note>referring to Osiris, whose cult center was at Abydos</note> in truth,
(9) by formation and birth in the name of **Isis** the bringer of fire, she of the throne of the <term>Good Spirit<note>original: "Agathodaemon"; a Greek term for a protective spirit or deity of fortune, often depicted as a serpent.</note></term>.
(10) I am this figure of the sun, *Sitamesro* <note>original: "s-t-me-sr"; likely meaning "The one who hears Osiris"</note> is my name. I am this...

---

<margin>The term "worm" (original: *helmêt*) mentioned in line 7 is referred to elsewhere as *syb*. These terms are somewhat vague in the original language, covering parasitic worms, insects, or even certain types of sores.</margin>

<margin>The phrase "by nature and birth" (line 9) is a difficult construction in the original Demotic.</margin>

<margin>The name of Isis is linked here to the "name of Abydos," suggesting the city itself is personified as the goddess, or the practitioner is invoking her specifically in her role within the Osiris mysteries.</margin>

13. the great offering-place of <term>Abydos<note>An ancient city, the primary cult center of Osiris and a place of pilgrimage for those seeking eternal life.</note></term>, where the blood of Osiris is justified, which Isis brought and poured into

19. [Name] daughter of [Mother] feel it, she being in love with him, she being mad after him, she burning for him, she seeking him 
20. in every place, there being a flame of fire in her heart in her hour of not seeing him."

22. from your finger <note>the "heart-finger" mentioned previously</note> which is written above again; you take the apple [seeds], you add blood to them, and you put them into the cup of wine,
23. and you recite this over it seven times, and you make the woman drink it at the designated time.

### Notes on the Transcription

l. 12. *ḥsy* (the drowned/sanctified one): <note>original: "ḥsy"</note> <gloss>a small hook-shaped sign inserted after the 'ḥ' seems to be a kind of determinative</gloss>. The reference here is, of course, to Osiris. 

*to the harbor* : the geographical significance of this is difficult to grasp, but compare with Column IX, line 23.

l. 13. *pour into*: used specifically for ritual liquids.

*who is* <note>original: "nt a"</note> seems to be the relative form used in this papyrus instead of the older form. 

l. 14. *Give it in it*: <gloss>above this is a letter resembling 'a' erased by two lines</gloss>; the following words are very difficult to understand. The word for "gave" <note>original: "te-f"</note> may be intended as a past relative form, "which he gave."

1. Another book: <term>Thew ye o-e o-n yᶜ wᶜ<note>Magical vocalizations. These strings of vowels were believed to resonate with the celestial spheres.</note></term>. Another book: <term>Elon<note>original: "nfr sp-sn"; literally 'very good' or 'perfect.'</note></term>.

4. Into the light of this lamp, and appear to this boy <note>Ancient Egyptian divination often used a young child as a medium to look into the flame of a lamp and describe the visions they saw.</note> and answer me regarding everything I ask about.

### COLUMN XVI.

(1, 2) The words spoken to the lamp: "Both, <term>Theou<note>Greek for 'God'.</note></term>, Ie, Oue, O-oe, Ia, Oua—otherwise, Theou, Ie, Oe, Oon, Ia, Oua—<term>Phthakh<note>The god Ptah, creator of the world.</note></term>, <term>Eloe<note>A Greek transcription of the Hebrew 'Elohim' (God).</note></term>—otherwise, <term>Elon<note>Likely 'Elyon', the 'Most High'.</note></term>, excellent, excellent—(3) Iath, Eon, <term>Puriphae<note>Greek for 'Fire-shining'.</note></term>, Ieou, Ia, Io, Ia, Ioue, come down (4) to the light of this lamp and appear to this boy..."

<margin>
**Line 27 Notes:** 
<term>Tastai</term>: an unknown name. This phrase is not translated in the Demotic section.
<term>Alchas</term>: the letter 'l' is recognizable in the facsimile; see the note on 1/16. The 's' of Coptos is likewise seen in the facsimile.
</margin>

**Scholarly Commentary:**
Regarding the phrase "If to me...", <term>WÜNSCH</term>, in the supplement on magical tablets, quotes an inscription from the Alaoui Museum (also published by <term>MASPERO</term>). It is a Latin love spell from the 2nd century AD found at <term>Hadrumetum<note>Modern Sousse, Tunisia.</note></term> but written in Greek characters: 

"If [the request is not granted], I will descend into the inner sanctuaries of Osiris and I will break apart the <term>burial<note>original: "τὴν ταφήν" (taphēn); the mummy.</note></term> and send it to be carried away by the river. For I am the great official of the great god <term>Achrammachalala<note>A frequent magical name of power in Greek papyri.</note></term>." 

As Wünsch explains, the practitioner threatens to snatch the chest of Osiris from his tomb and throw it into the Nile—exactly what the villainous god <term>Typhon<note>The Greek name for the god Set.</note></term> once did.

### COLUMN XVI. LINGUISTIC NOTES.

<margin>
**Line 1:** This is only a <gloss>gloss</gloss> (explanatory note) on the second line.
**Line 2:** The apparent 'n' at the beginning is likely a scribe's error.
**Line 4:** The word <term>šn</term> is often used in this papyrus to mean "answer" or "be inquired of" rather than just "inquire."
</margin>

## COLUMN XVI

...and inquire for me about that which I ask (5) here today: <term>Iao<note>A magical name for the divine, often associated with the Hebrew Yahweh.</note></term>, Iaolo, Therentho, Psikhimeakelo, (6) Blakhanspla, Iae, Quebai, Barbaraithou, Ieou, Arponknouph, (7) Brintatenophri, Hea, Karrhe, Balmenthre, Menebareiakhukh, Ia, (8) Khukh, Brinskulma, Arouzarba, Mesekhriph, Niptoumikh, (9) Maorkharam. Ho! Laankhukh, Omph, Brimbainouioth, (10) Segenbai, Khooukhe, Laikham, Armioouth.’ 

You say it, (11) it <note>The lamp must be ritually pure.</note> being pure, in this manner: ‘O god that liveth, O lamp that is lighted, Takrtat, he of eternity, bring in (12) <term>Boel<note>A powerful divine intermediary or spirit summoned to provide visions.</note></term>!’—three times—‘Arbeth-abi, Outhio, O great great god, bring Boel (13) in, Tat’—twice—‘bring Boel in!’—Three times. ‘Takrtat, he of Eternity, bring (14) Boel in!’—Three times. ‘Barouthi, O great god, bring Boel in!’—Three times.

(15) The invocation which you pronounce before <term>Phre<note>The Egyptian Sun god, Ra.</note></term> in the morning before reciting to the boy <note>The "boy" refers to the young, uncorrupted medium used to view the visions in the lamp.</note>, in order that that which thou doest may succeed: 

(16) ‘O great god, Tabao, Basoukham, Amo, Akhakharkhan-krabounzanouni-(17)edikomto, Kethou-basa-thouri-thmila-alo.’—Seven times.

(18) Another method of it again. You rise in the morning from your bed early in the day on which you will do it, or any day, (19) in order that everything which you will do shall prosper in your hand, you being pure from every abomination. You pronounce this invocation before Phre three times or seven times: 

(20) ‘Io, Tabao, Sokhom-moa, Okh-okh-khan-bouzanau, An-(21)iesi, Ekomphtho, Ketho, Sethouri, Thmila, Alouapokhri.'

---

Line 7. <note>original: "κάρρε"</note> *Karrhe*: the breathing or "h" sound on the second "r" is a notable phonetic detail. 
Line 11. <note>original: "e-f wb"</note> *it being pure*: the lamp must be physically and ritually cleansed as described in the following lines.
<note>original: "a·wy"</note> *Bring*: This word is a command to bring the spirit into the flame.
Line 15. <note>original: "z a·r"</note> *Succeed*: This term refers to the final outcome or success of the spell.

## COLUMN XVII

22. What I shall undertake <note>original: "$\theta$y t.t a"</note> today, let its performance succeed.

26. his eyes and you say to him again: "Do you see the light?" If he says to you, "I see the light and the flame of the lamp," you recite this immediately, saying:
27. "Heve! Nove!" <note>original Greek: "ηευε νουε". These are "voces magicae" or magical cries intended to catalyze the vision.</note> nine times. You ask him about that which you wish.

## COLUMN XVII

(22) May everything that I set my hand to here today come to pass.
**The Method:**
Take a new lamp that has never contained <term>red lead<note>original: "minium" or "prš". A red pigment used in antiquity; its absence suggests a requirement for ritual purity.</note></term> and (23) place a clean wick in it. Fill it with pure, genuine oil and set it in a concealed place that has been purified with <term>natron-water<note>A solution of naturally occurring salt used by the Egyptians for ritual cleansing.</note></term>. (24) Place the lamp upon a new brick. 

Bring a boy and seat him upon another new brick, his face (25) being turned toward the lamp. Close his eyes and recite the words written above down into the boy's head seven times. Then, make him (26) open his eyes. Say to him: "Do you see the light?" 

When he says to you, "I see the light in the flame of the lamp," you must cry out at that moment, saying (27) "Heve!" <note>original: "Heoue". A magical invocation or "vox magica."</note> nine times. You may then ask him about anything you wish, provided you have already recited the invocation to <term>Phre<note>The Egyptian Sun god, Ra.</note></term> in the morning. (28) 

Perform this in a place where the entrance opens to the East. Position the face of the lamp toward the *(blank)*. Position the face of the boy (29) toward the *(blank)*, facing the lamp, while you stand at his left hand. Cry down into his head and strike his head with your second finger <note>likely the middle finger</note> (30) of your right hand.

### COLUMN XVII (Continued)

(1) **Another version of the method, very effective, for the lamp:**
Recite: "Boel!" (three times). "I! I! I! A! A! A! Tat! Tat! Tat! <note>Boel and Tat are names of power; Tat is a form of the god Thoth.</note> You are the first attendant of the Great God, he who gives light exceedingly, (2) the companion of the flame, in whose mouth is the <term>unquenchable flame<note>original: "asbestous lychnos".</note></term>."

->COLUMN XVII NOTES<-

**Line 2:** The "flame." The markings in the original manuscript serve to separate the text from later interlinear additions.
**Line 27:** The term for "unquenchable" appears here and in other Greek magical papyri to describe a lamp that never goes out.

And they will surely succeed. However, if you do not maintain ritual purity <note>This likely refers to dietary restrictions, sexual abstinence, or clean clothing prior to the ritual.</note>, it will not succeed; the most vital element of this work is purity.

12. upon the wing of the goddess Triphis <note>original: "t rpy.t"; Triphis was a local goddess of the Panopolis region, but the term also refers to the constellation Virgo in astrological magic.</note> of the East. I am Great (twice)! "Great" is my name, my true name. I am . . . .

13. "Great-is-my-name" is my true name. I am L-ao-t-mu <note>A phonetic ritual name of power.</note>, I am the one who has exercised power (twice), he whose

14. power and flame belong to this golden crown which is upon his head. Thay-yt (twice), T (twice)!

15. Hatra (twice), the face of Ae (twice). O Anubis <note>Anubis is invoked here as the "king of the underworld" and the keeper of secrets who reveals the light.</note>, king of the underworld! Let the darkness depart!

16. Bring the light in to me, into my vessel-inquiry <term>vessel-inquiry: a divination ritual where a practitioner or medium stares into a bowl of liquid or a lamp (lecanomancy) to receive visions from the spirit world.</term>. For I am Horus, son of Osiris, whom Isis bore.

17. The noble child whom Isis loves, who seeks after his father Osiris-Onnophris <note>original: "Wsr wn-nfr"; a title meaning "Osiris the Perfect/Continually Good Being."</note>. O Anubis,

18. king of the underworld! Let the darkness depart! Bring the light in to me, into my vessel-inquiry.

19. My lot <note>or "my fate"</note> is here today! Let me succeed! Let him whose face is bent down <note>Referring to the young boy who must lean over the vessel to see the gods.</note> succeed at this vessel here today,

20. until the gods come in and they tell me a truthful answer concerning my inquiry which I am making

21. about it here today, in truth, without any falsehood. Arise, O Anubis!

22. The son of the ... walk forth at this moment. Bring the gods of this city to me, along with

23. the god who gives the answer today, so that he may tell me [the answer to] my inquiry which I am making about it today. <note>Recite this nine times.</note>

24. You shall open your eyes—or the boy shall—and you shall see the light. You shall call out to the light, saying, "It is open!"

### ->COL. XVIII.<-

...light, for I am (10) Fair-face <note>original: "hr-nfr"; a title often associated with the solar deity or the rejuvenated Osiris.</note>—another roll says, ‘I am the face of Nun <note>original: "Nun"; the personification of the primordial watery abyss from which all life emerged.</note>—in the morning, Halaho at midday, I am (11) Glad-of-face in the evening. They-yt (twice), To (twice), (15) Hatra (twice), the Dog-face <note>original: "hwn n p p-hwr"; likely referring to the jackal-head of Anubis.</note> (twice).

**[Instruction:]** Say this nine times.

(24) You open your eyes or those of the boy <note>Divination often involved a young, prepubescent boy serving as a "pure" medium to see the gods.</note> and you see the light. You invoke the light, saying, ‘Hail,

<margin>
**Translator's Notes on the Text:**
*   **oy oy:** The hieratic symbols <note>Hieratic: a cursive form of Egyptian hieroglyphs used for everyday documents and religious papyri.</note> below these characters are of uncertain meaning.
*   **Line 13:** The word *p'r-y* is difficult to interpret. It may be a copyist's error; if corrected, it translates to the power/prevailing mentioned above.
*   **Line 13 (cont):** The phrase *g-m-g-m* corresponds to a known magical vocalization found in other papyri.
*   **Line 17:** *nte mr-s* is a specific grammatical construction indicating a relative clause.
*   **Line 23:** The phrase *nt θ wh n p-hw* likely refers to the god who "governs" or "has authority" over the current day.
</margin>

### Notes on Column XIX

l. 7. *Isis the Savior* is figured on a stela of Horus and the crocodiles <note>A "cippus" or protective tablet showing Horus taming dangerous beasts.</note> in the British Museum, <term>BUDGE</term>, Mummy, p. 359; <term>WILKINSON</term>, Ancient Egypt, 3rd ed., vol. iii. Pl. 33. For *saving* see note to l. 4.
l. 8. **\*Leek\*** is prescribed for *animal bites*, <term>DIOSCORIDES</term>, ii. 178.
**\*Smyr\***: compare *kmw*, <term>BRUGSCH</term>, Wtb. Suppl., 1245; or *qmḥ*, *kmḥ*, ib., Suppl., 1249; Wtb., 1455, 1495 = קמח, ‘fine meal.’
l. 9. *tm·t*, ‘wound’; the word is best known in the Egyptian *ḥr dm·t* = person stung, bitten, or wounded, written out in the Metternich stela <note>A famous healing monument covered in spells against snake bites and scorpion stings.</note>, l. 32.

1. 38. This seems to imply an original chaos of burning.
1. 39. *p* seems distinct, *stm* (to hear) is extremely doubtful.
*lšlš* (?) : the reading is from <term>REUVENS'</term> copy <note>Conradus Leemans published the work of Reuvens, a pioneer in Demotic studies.</note>. *Tsibo* (purify), 'purify,' is determined by <image-desc>Egyptian hieroglyph of a seated deity or king holding a flail</image-desc> in 5/3, and 'cleanse' fits the sense here.

->COL. XX.<-

1. 1. *plege* = *plēgē* (wound), as noted by <term>MAX MÜLLER</term> <note>Wilhelm Max Müller, a 19th-century Egyptologist.</note>, *Records of the Past*, viii. 174. *plēgē* is used especially of the sting of a scorpion, British Museum Greek Papyrus 121, line 193, etc., but also of bites and stings of venomous animals in general, *Dioscorides*, *On Venomous Animals* 19, and of wounds in general. Except that it bleeds (line 14) there is little here to show what is meant by *plege* so long as lines 7-8 remain unintelligible.
1. 2. According to this, Anubis was the eldest son of Osiris (line 5), and his mother was Sekhemt-Isis, called in line 9 Isis. The liaison of Osiris with Nephthys (compare <term>PLUTARCH</term> <note>The Greek historian Plutarch recorded the most famous version of the Osiris myth, though Egyptian versions often varied.</note>) is referred to in the Old Coptic of the Paris National Library Papyrus.
*nte-s(?)-'y* is a possible reading, *a·r-s* is not possible.
1. 3. *wm-rm* (man-eaters) : there were Anthropophagi (man-eaters) and Cynamolgi (dog-milkers) with dog heads (compare 21/7) associated in Africa, apparently on the Upper Nile, as recorded by Pliny.

---

**Notes on the transcription:**
The scribe appears to have struggled with archaic styles in line 10, adding an unnecessary letter to "tongue." In line 12, the word for "tail" is noted for its rarity, appearing in later Gnostic texts like the *Pistis Sophia* regarding dragon anatomy. The negative command used in line 12 is a specific dialectal form (Achmimic) that would have been current when this manuscript was written.

# COL. XXI

## COL. XXI.

1. The inquiry of Osiris <note>original: "p šn-hne n Wsr." An "inquiry" (shen-hene) refers to a ritual used to obtain an oracle or vision from a deity.</note>.
2. His praise: "Osiris, the King of the underworld <note>original: "p pr-ʻo n t ty·t." Literally "the Pharaoh of the Duat (underworld)."</note>, the lord of the burial, he whose head is in This <margin>This (Tny). An ancient city near Abydos, the traditional burial place of the head of Osiris.</margin> while his feet are in Thebes <margin>Thebes (N). The southern capital; Egyptian myth often divided the body of Osiris among different cities.</margin>, he who receives <margin>receives wind <note>original: "θ wh." Corresponding to the Coptic word for "taking" or "receiving" the breath of life.</note></margin> the wind in Abydos <note>original: "'Bt." The primary cult center of Osiris.</note>.</note>
3. Whose chapel <note>original: "'whe·t." Likely a wooden shrine or sacred structure.</note> is Jerusalem <note>original: "pr-šylem." A striking syncretism where the Egyptian god is associated with Jerusalem or possibly the Pool of Siloam.</note>, he who is upon the sycamore of Meroe <note>A region in ancient Nubia (modern Sudan) known for its distinct culture and Egyptian-influenced religion.</note>, who is upon the mountain of Pharaoh-Ramses, who is upon the house of eternity."
4. "The house of Netbew forever; he whose face is like the face of a falcon of royal linen <note>original: "š-stn." Fine, high-quality linen used for royal garments and sacred mummification.</note>, while his tail is like the tail of a serpent."
5. "Whose limbs are limbs of [might?], whose hands are the hands of a person who is [armored?] like this crocodile on the side, and this staff of command is in his hand."
6. "His praise: Yahweh, Sabaoth-the-Great <note>original: "yʻhw sʻbʻh-ʻo." These are Hebrew divine titles—Yahweh and Sabaoth (Lord of Hosts)—repurposed here as magical words of power.</note> ... Michael, Sabael <note>Names of angelic beings common in the magical traditions of the Mediterranean.</note>."
7. "His praise: Anubis, in the district of the [divine faces?]; he who [belongs to] this land, who carries a staff under his one foot."
8. "Open the darkness of the matter; bring the light to me and enter to me; tell me the answer to that which I am asking about here today," [say] nine times.
9. "Until the god comes and the light appears. You should do it according to the method of the [ritual] described above again, while the face [of the boy] is toward the east, and your own face is toward the west, while you call out above his head."

---

## Col. XXI.

1. <note>Scholars such as Erman and Griffith have noted that the opening lines of this column are closely related to "Old Coptic" texts found in Paris. Old Coptic refers to the Egyptian language written using the Greek alphabet with some additional signs, representing a transitional phase between the ancient scripts and later Coptic.</note>
2. <note>The Old Coptic parallel to line 2 translates as: "His praise is Osiris, the King of the underworld, the lord of the burial, he who is in the presence of... his feet are in the south... his glory is in Jerusalem." This shows how these magical traditions were shared across different scripts and languages in Roman-era Egypt.</note>

<margin>*This.* The Edfu geographical list states that the head of Osiris was preserved at Abydos (in the district of This) and a foot was kept at Thebes.</margin>

<margin>*Receives wind.* Parallel to the Old Coptic "chiotou."</margin>

3. <note>The term for "chapel" used here has a wood determinative, suggesting a portable shrine. The mention of Jerusalem (or Siloam) highlights the multicultural religious landscape of the time.</note>

It provides specific instructions for a ritual involving a boy medium, light, and directional orientation to receive answers from the divine.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the sequence of magical rituals from the previous column. While the previous page focused on physical ailments like stings and throat obstructions, Column XXI moves into the realm of high ritual magic and divination. It introduces a "vessel-inquiry" (scrying) ritual, invoking Osiris and Anubis through complex cosmic descriptions that blend traditional Egyptian mythology with Hebrew and Greek mystical names.</meta>

# COL. XXI

->## COL. XXI.<-

(1) The vessel-inquiry of Osiris <note>original: "p šn-hne n Wsr." A ritual where a medium—usually a young boy—gazes into a bowl of liquid to see visions or receive oracles from the god.</note>. (2) "Hail to him! Osiris, King of the Underworld, lord of burial, whose head is in This <margin>This (Tjenu): An ancient city near Abydos, the legendary resting place of the head of Osiris.</margin>, and his feet are in Thebes <margin>Thebes (Waset): The great southern capital of Egypt.</margin>, he who gives the answer <note>original: "θ wh." Literally "takes the wind" or "gives the breath," a technical term for providing an oracular response.</note> in Abydos, (3) whose chapel is in Jerusalem <note>original: "Pa-sholom." A fascinating example of religious blending, identifying the realm of Osiris with Jerusalem or perhaps the Pool of Siloam.</note>, he who is beneath the *nubs*-tree <term>nubs-tree: A sacred tree, likely a sycamore or jujube, associated with divinity in both Egypt and Nubia.</term> in Meroë <margin>Meroë: The capital of the Kingdom of Kush, located in modern-day Sudan.</margin>, who is on the mountain of Poranos <note>original: "P-co-rnws." This likely refers to the Greek *Ouranos* (Heaven) or Mount Olympus.</note>, who is over my house for eternity, (4) the house of Netbeou <note>original: "Ne-tbeu." Spirits of retribution or divine justice often mentioned in magical texts.</note> forever; he whose countenance is like the face of a falcon wrapped in linen, a mighty one whose tail is the tail of a serpent, (5) whose back is the back of a crocodile <note>original: "mnt." A creature described as having a spiny back; interpreted as a crocodile or a guardian dragon.</note>, whose hand is a man's, who is girded with this bandage-girdle, in whose hand is this wand of command." (6) "Hail to him: Iaho, Sabaho, Atonai <note>original: "Iaho, Sabaho, Atonai." These are magical transcriptions of the Hebrew names for God: Yahweh, Sabaoth (Lord of Hosts), and Adonai (Lord).</note>, Mistemu, Iauiu; hail to him: Michael, Sabael <note>Michael and Sabael: Archangels common in the syncretic "magic" of the Greco-Roman period that merged Jewish, Greek, and Egyptian traditions.</note>." (7) "Hail to him: Anubis <note>The jackal-headed god who guided souls and presided over embalming.</note> in the district of the dog-faces <margin>Dog-faces (Cynocephali): A mythical race of dog-headed humans believed to live at the edges of the world.</margin>, he to whom this earth belongs, who carries a wound on one foot." (8) "Hide the darkness in the center, bring in the light for me, come in to me, tell me the answer to that about which I am inquiring here today." —Speak this nine times, (9) until the god comes and the light appears. You must perform it in the same manner as the instructions provided above; the boy's face being toward the East and your own face toward the West; you call down into his head <note>The practitioner "calls down" the divine power into the boy's head to trigger the visionary state.</note>.

*the nubs-tree of Meroë*: see the note by <term>Maspero</term> <note>Gaston Maspero (1846–1916) was a famous French Egyptologist.</note> in the *Proceedings of the Society of Biblical Archaeology*, xiii. 496. Compare with "the house of the trees" in line 36.
*Poranos*: perhaps the Greek *Ouranos* (Heaven), in the sense of Mount Olympus.
line 4. *Netbeu*: see the previous note on column 2, line 9.
*hf*: a "snake's" head or tail is regularly described this way in Egyptian texts.
line 5. *mnt*: some monster or reptile with a spiny back, perhaps a crocodile, to judge by the symbol used in the original script. It may refer to a guardian dragon <note>original Coptic: "mnot".</note>.
*bandage-girdle*: very obscure, though the writing is clear.
line 7. *dog-faces*: compare with the term *honoror* and see the notes to column 20, line 3. This refers to the "Cynocephali" mentioned by the Greek historian Herodotus.
*one foot*: the term <gloss>ou-rete</gloss> is used here for "one foot" as opposed to the usual word for a pair of feet.

# COL. XXI

10. [The ritual] of the beetle of the wine-jar to make a woman love a man <note>original: "a t s-ḥm-t mr ḥwt." This is a standard heading for an aphrodisiac or attraction spell.</note>. You should find a beetle <note>The text includes a small drawing of a beetle followed by a star symbol, indicating a specific celestial or marked species.</note> that is small, which has no horn (?), and which has three shield-like marks <term>shield-like marks (ikym): Derived from the Egyptian word "ikm," meaning shield, referring to the protective plates on the beetle’s head.</term>

11. on the front of its head. You will find its face going outward... or the one that has two horns as well. You should find it in the "House of the Sun" <note>likely an east-facing window or a sun-drenched spot</note> when you want it. [Wrap it] in a cloth on your day of...

12. and you should bind it to your face with a strip of palm-fiber with the beetle on the palm of your hand. You shall speak over it toward the sun when it rises, seven times. You shall finish and then let it drown in "noble water" <note>original: "ḥsy." This refers to water considered sacred because a being has been ritually drowned in it, effectively "deifying" the specimen.</note>

13. and some Egyptian milk. You should put [the beetle] on its head in a [vessel] of olive oil. You should leave it until evening with the milk. When evening comes, you shall

14. take it out. Spread it on a cloth and put a linen bandage (?) over it for four days. You should burn frankincense before it. When the four days have passed and it is dry,

15. you should take it in your hands with a cloth spread under it. Divide it with a bronze knife. Take its right half and your own nail clippings from your right hand and your right foot.

16. You should grind them on a new fragment of a grinding stone in grape-juice (?). Grind them with nine seeds of an apple and your [semen] <note>original: "mt.t." Literally "word" or "matter," but in medical/magical contexts, it often serves as a euphemism for biological fluids like semen or spittle.</note> or your spittle without oil

17. in the [bedroom]. Make it into a pill and put it into the wine. Speak over it seven times. You should make the woman drink it. Then you take the other half [of the beetle] and your nail clippings from your [left] hand

18. and your [left] foot again, and you should wrap them in a strip of royal linen <note>original: "š-stn." High-quality fine linen used for divine statues or royalty.</note>...

***

**Notes on translation and terminology:**

Line 10: *To make a woman love* - The scribe omitted the verb "to make" (*ire*), which is standard in these formulas. 
"Fish-faced (?)" - The commentary suggests this beetle might be a weevil, characterized by a long snout similar to the Oxyrhynchus fish. In the writings of Horapollo <note>Horapollo was an author who wrote about Egyptian hieroglyphs in late antiquity.</note>, such a creature is described as <term>one-horned and uniquely shaped</term> <note>original Greek: "μονόκερως καὶ ἰδιό-μορφος"</note>.

Line 11: *The one with two horns* - Likely refers to a stag-beetle. Other magical papyri describe <term>the bull-shaped beetle</term> <note>original Greek: "κανθαρον τον ταυρομορφον"</note>. Horapollo further classifies these as <term>two-horned and bull-like</term> <note>original Greek: "δίκερως καὶ ταυροειδής"</note>.

Line 12: *Bind* - While the word can mean "wrap," in this context of speaking toward the sun, it suggests a ritual positioning. 
*Head (ḥ·t)* - This is likely a scribal error for "belly" (*ẖ·t*), a common phonetic confusion in Demotic between the "h" and "kh" sounds.

(33) of true lapis lazuli <term>lapis lazuli (ḥstb): A deep blue stone representing the heavens.</term>. You are the support of Shu <note>Shu was the god of air who held up the sky.</note>; you are the *salapin* <note>A magical or technical term of uncertain meaning, possibly a title or anatomical part.</note> of Osiris. You are this black bull, the fury that came forth from the Nun <note>The Nun was the primeval watery abyss from which the universe was born.</note>,

(34) while the beauty of Isis is with you. You are *Raks Raparaks*! You are the blood of this male donkey which was brought from the land of Syria to Egypt, [poured] out into the wine."

---

Aside from the main text, it should be noted that in lines 21/27 and 21/41, the letter *š* is written over *ne*, almost as an afterthought. This suggests that *ne*, which is spelled identically to the Coptic future auxiliary <note>original: "ⲛⲁ"</note>, must be the verb "to go" <note>original: "ⲛⲁ," related to the idea of "becoming" or "future being"</note>, as opposed to the word for "coming" <note>original: "ⲛⲏⲟⲩ," which describes the state of having arrived</note>.
The verb *ne* <note>original: "ⲛⲁ"</note> is practically the state of "going" <note>original: "ϣⲉ"</note>. The correction of *ne* to *š* in both passages is quite remarkable. Although some grammarians (like Steindorff) suggest this form is allowed in certain sentence structures, it seems the scribe found it difficult here. The correction changes the meaning from "until she be going" (a state) to "until she go" (the action). It appears the scribe was confused by the word *ne*, leading to these mistakes. The following table comparing the verbs "come" and "go" may be helpful for understanding these nuances:

| Meaning | Language | Infinitive (Action) | Infinitive (Action) | Qualitative (State) | Sdm-f (Verb Form) |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| ‘go’ | Coptic | to go | to go / future | going | he goes |
| | Demotic | *š* | *ne* / *ne-* | *ne* (?) | *ne-f* |
| ‘come’ | Coptic | to come | — | coming | he brought |
| | Demotic | *'y* | — | *'n-’w* | *'w-f* |

### Philological Notes

**Line 28.** *p-t o-t*. It is very rarely that the Ennead is written without the addition of the word "gods." The double Ennead of eighteen gods is frequently mentioned from the earliest times onward.

**Line 29.** **πο** <gloss>Coptic gloss</gloss>. This gloss may represent a specific grammatical form of the word for "becoming" or "shining."
*n nw mhrr* for *n we mhrr*: "a scarab."
*hm-t*: Perhaps "trade" or "art."

**Line 30.** *hyt*. Despite its unusual spelling, this word is certainly "fury," given its common association with the verb "to cast" (*hwy*). We may translate the phrase as the "fury of Phalam," etc.

**Line 31.** *mws*. From the ancient Egyptian *mis-t*, likely referring to the liver or kidneys. The "small intestine" (*p mht hm*) is a term also found in the Rhind Mathematical Papyrus.

### COL. XXIII.

(1) A spell to induce a trance <note>The text uses a term for "falling," likely referring to catalepsy or a sudden loss of consciousness</note>. Ritual Instructions: (2) Take the head of a donkey and place it between your feet, facing the sun in the morning when it is about to rise. (3) Face it again in the evening when it goes to its setting. Anoint your right foot with "set-stone" <note>likely a mineral like alum or a specific Syrian stone</note> (4) of Syria, and your left foot with clay—the soles of your feet as well. Place your right hand (5) in front and your left hand behind you, with the donkey's head held between them. Anoint both of your hands with donkey’s blood, (6) as well as the corners of your mouth <note>original: "fnz," which may refer to either the nostrils or the edges of the lips</note>. Utter these charms toward the sun in the morning and evening for four days; then (7) the subject will sleep. If you wish to make him die, perform this for seven days. To perform this magic <term>taking a pledge <gloss>original: "θ-we-t," a term for gaining power over a god or man</gloss></term>, you must bind a thread of palm-fibre (8) to your hand, and a mat of wild palm-fibre to your phallus and your head; this is very excellent. This is the invocation which you utter before the sun: (9) "I invoke you, who are in the empty air, terrible, invisible, (10) almighty, god of gods, dealing destruction and making desolate, O you..." <note>This invocation is very similar to the "severing spells" <gloss>Greek original: "diakopai"</gloss> found in other ancient magical papyri, such as those held in the Leiden collection.</note>

### COL. XXIII.

11. ...a stable house. Just as you were cast out from Egypt and outside
12. the country, you were named "The One Who Breaks All and is Not Defeated." <note>This refers to the myth of Seth (Typhon) being expelled from Egypt after his conflict with Horus and Osiris.</note>

14. for I call upon you by your own authentic name, which you are not able
15. to ignore: Io Erbeth, Iopakerbeth, Iobolchoseth, Iopatathnax, <note>These are "voces magicae"—powerful, untranslatable magical names used to compel the deity.</note>
16. Iosoro, Ioneboutosoualeth, Aktiophi, Ereshkigal <note>The Sumerian goddess of the underworld, here invoked alongside the Egyptian Seth</note>, Neboposoaleth,

20. presence—or she has done so. Because of this, I do these things. <term>Common formula <gloss>original: "koina," indicating the practitioner should add the standard conclusion to the spell</gloss></term>.

21. An inquiry before the Moon: You should perform it for an inquiry through a vessel or through a young boy. <note>Young boys were often used as mediums in ancient divination rituals.</note>

### COL. XXIII

(11) ...who hates a well-established household. When you were cast out of Egypt and out of (12) the country, you were titled, “The One Who Destroys All and is Unconquered.” <note>This refers to the mythological expulsion of Seth (Typhon) by the followers of Osiris and Horus.</note> (13) I invoke you, Typhon Seth; I perform your ceremonies of divination, (14) for I invoke you by your powerful name in words which you cannot (15) refuse to hear: Io Erbeth, Iopakerbeth, Iobolkhoseth, Iopatathnax, <note>These are "voces magicae"—mystical names used to compel spiritual forces.</note> (16) Iosoro, Ioneboutosoualeth, Aktiophi, Ereshkigal <note>The Sumerian goddess of the underworld, here syncretized with Egyptian magic.</note>, Neboposoaleth, (17) Aberamenthoou, Lerthexanax, Ethreluoth, Nemareba, Aemina, (18) come to me completely and approach, and strike down [Name] <note>The original likely used a placeholder for the target's name.</note> with shivering and (19) fever; he has wronged me, and has poured out the blood of Typhon beside him (20) or her: therefore I do these things.’

->Common formula.<- <note>original: "koina," a shorthand instruction for the practitioner to recite the standard closing of a magical spell.</note>

(21) To obtain a divination before the moon: You perform it by a vessel-inquiry alone or with a child medium. If it is you who will inquire, you fill your eye (22) with green eye-paint and stibium <term>stibium <gloss>a black mineral powder, also known as kohl, used for both cosmetic and ritual protection</gloss></term>. You stand on a high place, such as the roof of your house, and address the moon when it fills (23) the *uzat* <term>uzat <gloss>the Eye of Horus, symbolizing the full moon and restoration</gloss></term> on the 15th day, you having remained pure for three days. You pronounce this invocation to the moon seven or nine times until he appears to you (24) and speaks to you: ‘Hail! Sax, Amun, Sax, Abrasax; for you are the moon, (25) the chief of the stars, the one who formed them. Listen to the things that I have said, follow the words of my mouth, and reveal yourself to me, Than, (26) Thana, Thanatha, otherwise Thei—this is my correct name.’ Recite this nine times until she <note>The scribe shifts the gender here, likely influenced by the Greek word for moon, *Selene*, which is feminine, whereas the Egyptian *Iah* is masculine.</note> reveals herself to you.

---

Line 24: The use of the first sound of the hieroglyphic group representing a bolt of cloth, a loaf of bread, and a papyrus roll (*st*) within the spelling of the name Isis (*ast*) is noteworthy.
Line 26: The hieroglyphs consisting of a house, a mouth, and a vertical stroke are read first as **ea** and then as **ehi**.
After the numeral IX (nine), the word for "times" (*sep*) should probably be understood.
*Regarding the phrase "until she reveals herself":* It seems as if the feminine pronoun refers to the Greek word for the moon, *Selene*, whereas the Egyptian moon deity is usually masculine.

### COL. XXIV

1 ounce, (9) **henbane** <note>original: "hyoscyamus," a plant in the nightshade family used as a powerful sedative and anesthetic.</note>, 1 ounce, (10) **ivy**, 1 ounce;

(17) A medicament for making a man sleep; very good:—(18) **pips of apple**, 1 stater, 1 **drachma** <term>drachma <gloss>a unit of weight</gloss></term>, **mandragora root** <note>the root of the mandrake plant, highly valued in antiquity for its narcotic and hallucinogenic effects.</note>, 4 drachmas, (19) **ivy**, 4 drachmas; pound together; you put fifteen (20) **uteh** of wine to it; you put it into a glass **glyt** <term>glyt <gloss>a glass vessel or jar</gloss></term>; (21) you keep it. If you wish to give it, you put a little into a cup of wine, (22) you give it to the man.

**Ivy**: it grows in gardens; (23) its leaf is like the leaf of **shekam** <note>likely referring to a sycamore or fig-type leaf.</note>, being divided into three lobes (24) like a vine-leaf; it (the leaf) is one palm <note>a measurement based on the width of the hand</note> in measurement; its blossom (25) is like silver—another says gold.

Another: **gall of an Alexandrian weasel**, (26) you add it to any food.

Another: a **two-tailed lizard** <note>abnormal animals were often prized as potent ingredients in magical medicine.</note>.

(27) A medicament for **catalepsy** <note>original: "catalepsy," a state of physical rigidity and lack of response to stimuli.</note>: **gall of a horned viper** <note>original: "cerastes," the Saharan horned viper.</note>, **pips** of western apples, **herb of klo** <note>original: "klo," a plant known in Coptic traditions as a source for vegetable arrow poison.</note>, (28) pound them together; make into a pill, put (it) into the food.

<note>The scribe uses the term "one by one" (Coptic: oua oua). In line 14, the instruction suggests "letting them absorb it" or "letting the patient drink it." The weight "half-kite" mentioned in lines 18-19 refers to the Coptic "sikite," which represents half a drachma.</note>

### Preparation for Gathering Spirits
To gather the spirits, take the blood of a wild goose <note>original: "smune-goose."</note>, the blood of a hoopoe, the blood of a nightjar, (10) the *ankh-amu* plant, the *senepe* plant, the "Great-of-Amun" plant, the "stone of life" <note>original: "qes-ankh."</note>, genuine lapis-lazuli, myrrh, and the "Footprint-of-Isis" plant. Grind these and form them into a ball. Paint (11) your eyes with this mixture, combined with the tear of a goat, using a stylus <note>original: "pleasure-wood."</note> made of ebony. Tie yourself at your side (12) with a cord made of male-palm fiber.

### The Vessel-Inquiry of the Lamp
(13) This is the method for performing the <term>vessel-inquiry: A form of divination (scrying) where a practitioner looks into a liquid or a flame to receive visions.</term> of the lamp. Take a clean, bright lamp. Do not put red lead <note>original: "minium."</note> or gum-water into it. Its wick must be made of fine linen. Fill it with genuine oil (14) or "oil of dew." Tie it with four threads of linen that have not been boiled. Hang the lamp on a wall facing East (15) using a peg made of laurel wood. 

Bring a young boy and have him stand before the lamp. He must be ritually pure and have never been with a woman. Cover his eyes with your hand. (16) Light the lamp and recite the incantation down into the top of his head seven times. Then, have him open [his eyes]...

# COL. XXIX

Look, here is another version of it again. You take the boy to a high, elevated (21) room and make him stand in a place where there is a large window in front of him, its opening facing the East where the rising sun (22) shines into it. Paint the boy's eyes with the eye-paint that is prescribed for it; recite to him [...] times, or perhaps seven times. Stand over him and make him (23) gaze toward the sun when it fills the <term>Sound Eye</term> <note>original: "uzat." This refers to the sun reaching its full strength, likely at the summer solstice or high noon.</note>. He must stand upright on a new brick, with a new linen robe behind him, and his eyes should be closed. (24) Recite down into the crown of his head; strike his head with your finger (the one described above, likely the ring finger); offer **Frankincense** <note>The text likely refers to the burning of resin as a purification offering.</note> before him. [The ointment] which you apply to the boy's eyes when he performs any <term>Solar Divination</term> <note>original: "vessel-inquiry of the sun." (26) Take two <unclear>fish</unclear> <note>original: "šʿe" or "šme," likely a species of river fish.</note> from the river while they are still alive; burn one of them using vine-wood before the sun, and add the blood of the other to it. (27) Grind it together with myrrh and form the mixture into a pill the length of one finger. [...] apply this to his eyes. Take a **Kohl-pot** <note>A small jar used for holding eye cosmetics.</note> made of [...] and a **Kohl-stick** <note>An applicator used to apply eye paint.</note> made of (28) <term>Lel-wood</term> <note>original: "lel," an unidentified plant or wood used for ritual tools.</note>. Keep your eyes open toward it, and then the god will appear to you and give you an answer (30) regarding everything. The most important requirement is **Purity**. This ritual is effective for a boy medium, and it is just as effective for you if you are acting alone.

<meta>This page serves as a botanical appendix to the preceding ritual instructions. Having described the solar divination and the preparation of eye ointments, the manuscript now provides a glossary of medicinal plants and their synonyms in both Demotic and Greek, likely to ensure the practitioner selects the correct ingredients.</meta>

# VERSO

## VERSO COL. I.

1. Eyebrow of the Sun <note>original: "’nḥ n rᶜ" (Demotic); "ὀφρὺς ἡλίου" (Greek). This is likely a type of botanical rush.</note>
2. Eyebrow of the Moon <note>original: "’nḥ n ᶜḥ" (Demotic); "ὀφρὺς (σελήνης)" (Greek).</note>
3. These are various plants.
4. <term>Sungenon</term> <note>original: "ἡλιογονον." Literally "sun-born," likely referring to a species of thistle.</note>
5. <term>Moongenon</term> <note>original: "σεληνογονον." Literally "moon-born," identified by ancient authors as the Peony.</note>
6. These are various plants.
7. <term>Spurge</term> <note>original: "θιθυμαλος" (Tithymalus). A plant known for its milky, caustic sap.</note>
8. It is this small plant that is found on the riverbanks,
9. which releases a milky sap.
10. If you put its milk upon a person’s skin,
11. it causes it to blister.

## VERSO COL. II.

1. <term>Chamomile</term> <note>original: "χαμεμελον."</note>—"Pure-Mountain" is its name.
2. <term>White-flower</term> <note>original: "λευκανθεμον." Likely the ox-eye daisy.</note>—"Horse-fringe" is its name.
3. <term>Lily-flower</term> <note>original: "κριναθεμον."</note>—"Nothing is better than me" is its name.
4. <term>Golden-flower</term> <note>original: "χρυσανθεμον." Likely the corn marigold.</note>—"Fair-faced" is its name; others say: "The Golden Flower."

---

## COMMENTARY ON COLUMN I

**Line 1:** The name <term>Eyebrow of the Sun</term> <note>original: "ὀφρὺς ἡλίου"</note> is a synonym for the <term>Olive-rush</term> <note>original: "σχοῖνος ἐλεία"</note> mentioned in Dioscorides’ *On Medical Materials*, book 4, chapter 52.

**Line 4:** <term>Sungenon</term> <note>original: "ἡλιογονον"</note>: This is cited as a synonym for <term>Sous</term> in older lexicons alongside <term>Safflower</term> <note>original: "κνίκος," Carthamus tinctorius</note> and the <term>Distaff Thistle</term> <note>original: "ἀτρακτυλίς"</note>. It appears to be a variety of thistle.

**Line 5:** <term>Moongenon</term> <note>original: "σεληνογονον"</note>: This was the name given by the <term>Prophets</term> <note>High-ranking Egyptian priests or specialized ritualists.</note> to the <term>Peony</term> <note>original: "παιονία"</note>. Plant names used by these "Prophets" were frequently linked to deities and celestial bodies. While some scholars identify these "Prophets" specifically as Egyptian, the term may more broadly refer to various mystical traditions.

**Line 9:** The grammatical structure here is unusual; the writer uses a specific relative form but inserts a marker of habit <note>original: "ḥr"</note>, which is a rare linguistic anomaly in this dialect.

**Line 10:** The ancient physician Galen, in his work *On Simple Medicines* (book 8, 19/7), makes the same observation regarding the juice of the <term>Spurge</term> <note>original: "τιθύμαλλος"</note>: if it touches the skin, it burns and irritates it.

<summary>This page functions as a bilingual botanical glossary, equating Demotic plant names with Greek technical terms. It specifically identifies plants like spurge, chamomile, and peonies, noting their physical properties and ritual associations.</summary>
<keywords>botany, pharmacology, Spurge, Chamomile, Peony, Sungenon, Moongenon, Egyptian Prophets, Dioscorides, Galen</keywords>

(3) <term>Lily-flower</term> <note>original: "Crinanthemon"</note>. "None is better than I" is its name.

---

## COMMENTARY ON COLUMN II.

**Line 1.** <term>Chamomile</term> <note>original Greek: "χαμαίμηλον"</note>; chamomile is a synonym of <term>Anthemis</term> <note>original: "ἀνθεμίς"</note> in Dioscorides’ *On Medical Materials*, book 3, chapter 144, and of <term>Parthenium</term> <note>original: "παρθένιον"</note>, in chapter 145.
The name *thw-w'b* <note>Coptic: "ⲧⲱϩ+ⲟⲩⲏⲃ"</note> translates to **"clean hay,"** probably named for its sweet scent. According to the writer Apuleius (chapter 24), *thaboris* <note>manuscript variation: "tuoris"</note> was the Egyptian name for the chamomile plant <note>reference: Wiedemann, *Ancient Egyptian Words Transcribed by Classical Authors*, p. 22</note>.

**Line 2.** <term>White-flower</term> <note>original Greek: "λευκανθεμον"</note>: this is a synonym of <term>Anthyllis</term> <note>original: "ἀνθυλλίς"</note>, <term>Anthemis</term>, and <term>Parthenium</term> in Dioscorides (book 3, chapters 143–145), but none of these plants seem to perfectly match the Egyptian name *šq ḥtr*, which translates to **"prick(?)-horse."** Compare the word *šq* to the Coptic word for "to dig" <note>original: "ϣⲱⲕ"</note> and the Arabic for "prick" <note>original: "شاك"</note>.

**Line 3.** <term>Lily-flower</term> <note>original Greek: "κρινάνθεμον"</note> is said by some to be the **houseleek**; perhaps its appearance in Dioscorides (book 3, chapter 127) as a synonym of the <term>Day-lily</term> <note>original: "ἡμεροκαλλίς"</note> provides a better explanation.

**Line 4.** <term>Golden-flower</term> <note>original Greek: "χρυσάνθεμον"</note>, described in Dioscorides (book 4, chapter 58) as the <term>Crown Daisy</term> <note>Latin: "Chrysanthemum coronarium"</note>. The name also appears as a synonym for <term>Artemisia</term> <note>original: "ἀρτεμισία"</note>, <term>Goldy-locks</term> <note>original: "χρυσοκόμη"</note>, <term>Helichrysum</term> <note>original: "ἑλίχρυσον"</note>, and the <term>Great Everliving</term> <note>original: "ἀείζωον τὸ μέγα"</note> (Dioscorides, book 4, chapters 55, 57, 88).
For the phrase "the gold flower" <note>original: "t ḥrr.t nb"</note>, compare scholarly works by Lemm and the plant specimens found in ancient Egyptian funeral wreaths from Hawara <note>reference: Petrie, *Hawara*, p. 53</note>.

<summary>This page contains a bilingual (Demotic and Greek) botanical glossary that identifies medicinal plants like spurge and chamomile, providing their descriptive Egyptian names and cross-referencing them with classical herbalists like Dioscorides.</summary>
<keywords>botany, herbal medicine, chamomile, spurge, Dioscorides, Egyptian plant names, pharmacological synonyms, Hawara wreaths</keywords>

### COMMENTARY ON COLUMN III.

**Line 7.** <term>Magnesia</term> <note>original: "mᶜknesyᶜ"</note> is <term>magnetic iron ore</term>. This substance is described by several ancient authorities, including the Greek physician **Dioscorides** (book 5, chapter 147) and the Roman naturalist **Pliny** in his *Natural History* (book 36, chapter 25).

**Line 10.** **"It being black"** <note>original: "e-f km"</note>: In this context, the scribe describes the color of the mineral after it has been processed or ground.

**Line 11.** **"Living magnet"** <note>original: "mᶜknes nt ᶜnḥ"; Greek: "µαγνης ζῶν"</note>. This term refers to naturally magnetized magnetite. It is frequently mentioned in the medical prescriptions of **Alexander of Tralles**, a famous 6th-century physician. The phrase **"it is imported"** <note>original: "ḥr 'n-w-f"</note> indicates that this high-quality magnet was not sourced locally.

**Line 13.** **"Human magnes"** <note>original: "mᶜnes n rm"</note>: This likely refers to <term>bloodstone</term> or <term>haematite</term>. The name "human" may stem from the belief that it had properties similar to human blood. **Pliny** (Natural History, 36. 25) describes a "haematite magnes" found in Zimiris, Ethiopia, which **"yields a blood-like substance if rubbed"** <note>original Latin: "sanguinem reddens si teratur"</note>.

<summary>This page lists minerals and stones used in ancient medicine, specifically focusing on different types of Magnesia and magnetic ores, including the "living magnet" and "human magnes" (bloodstone).</summary>
<keywords>Magnesia, magnet, magnetite, bloodstone, haematite, ancient medicine, mineralogy, Pliny, Dioscorides, Alexander of Tralles</keywords>

### VERSO COL. III.

(1) Medicament [for a <unclear>seizure</unclear>. Gall of a <term>Horned Viper</term>] <note>original: "cerastes"</note>, (2) seeds of Western apples, and the herb of <term>Klo</term>. (3) Grind them together, make into a ball, put it into wine, and drink it.

---

### SCHOLARLY COMMENTARY

The text discusses the **Male Magnet** and **Female Magnet**, where the former is strongly magnetic and reddish in color; scholars suggest the term "of man" used here translates the Greek idea of "andrios" or "masculine" minerals. 

The name for **India** appears here in a Coptic-influenced form. 

The **Apshe-beetle** mentioned in the recipe is likely the same beetle referred to in Chapter 36 of the *Book of the Dead*, used here in a ritual or medicinal context alongside apple and styrax.

The term **Lees of wine** refers to "salt of tartar," which was described by early researchers as a white stony substance. 

**Galbanum** was a well-known medicinal resin in antiquity, frequently cited by the Greek physician Dioscorides for its healing properties.

<summary>This page catalogs various types of magnetic minerals (magnesia) and their physical properties, alongside a medicinal recipe using viper gall and apple seeds. It reflects the intersection of Egyptian, Greek, and Indian mineralogical knowledge in the late antique period.</summary>

<keywords>magnesia, magnet, hematite, India, kohl, horned viper, medicinal wine, galbanum, beetle, styrax</keywords>

## VERSO COL. IV.

(7) Regarding <term>Quick-lime</term> <note>original: "sgewe"; possibly from the Greek word for "equipment" or "preparation," here used to refer to a specific mineral substance like lime</note>, the method of knowing (8) it, to see if it is genuine: you grind a little (9) in water and you rub it on the skin (10) of a man; in a short moment (11) it <unclear>burns</unclear> the skin.

(12) Its name in the Greek language is <term>Aphroselenon</term> <note>original: "αφροσεληνον"; literally "froth of the moon," referring to selenite or foliated sulphate of lime</note>. (13) <term>Moon-stone</term> <note>original: "zḥ n ʿḥ"; literally "writing of the moon"</note>: it is a stone that is white.

(14) A <term>Potion</term> <note>original: "phr.t"; a medical or magical preparation</note> to make a woman love a man: <term>Acacia</term> pods <note>original: "θθ.t n šnt.t"</note> (15) which are in honey; rub his <unclear>phallus</unclear> with it (16) and you sleep with the woman.

(17) <term>Moon-stone</term>: it is a stone that is white; it is like (18) glass when it is ground very fine, like <term>Orpiment</term> <note>original: "ʾrsenygwn"; from the Greek "ἀρσενικόν," a yellow arsenic sulfide mineral used in pigments and medicine</note>.

<column-break/>

## VERSO COL. IV. (Continued)

(1) A remedy for an <term>Ear</term> <note>original: "msze"</note> that is discharging fluid: (2) a little gall with good wine (3) and you apply it to it; it will not draw out <unclear>pus</unclear> from the inside. (4) And you grind a little gall with wine (5) and you apply it to it for 4 days.

(6) <term>Salamander</term> <note>original: "σαλαματρα"</note>: (7) a small lizard (8) which is of the color of <unclear>Keurine</unclear> <note>perhaps a saffron-like yellow</note> (9) which has no feet.

(10) <term>Head of a sheep</term> <note>original: "tp n sr"; Greek: "κεφαλεκη" (kephalike)</note> is its name: (11) a plant which is like a cluster of <term>Wild Fennel</term>.

### NOTES ON COLUMN IV

Line 1: The cross mark (x) found at the beginning of sections in this and the following column appears to be a visual aid intended to help the reader navigate the crowded handwriting of the original manuscript.

**Discharge from the ears:** Similar treatments are found in the <term>Papyrus Ebers</term> <note>one of the most famous medical documents from ancient Egypt, dating to roughly 1550 BCE</note>, column 91, line 3.

Line 7: The sign in the original manuscript resembles the word for "bronze," rather than "variety."

Line 8: The term for <term>chrysolite</term> <note>original: "kalaine"</note> refers to a greenish-yellow stone, as discussed in historical linguistic essays.

Line 10: <term>Ram’s Horn</term> <note>original: "tp n sr"</note> is likely the <term>Chickpea</term> <note>original: "krios"; Latin: "Cicer arietinum"</note> as mentioned by the ancient writers Dioscorides and Pliny.

Line 11: <term>Wild Fennel</term> <note>original: "shmar hwt"</note>. This term is linguistically significant as it shows a Semitic influence on the vocabulary used in the text.

->VERSO COL. V.<-

(1) A medicine to stop bleeding: <term>Great Nile</term> water <note>original: "mw n hapy-aa"; this likely refers to the juice of a specific succulent plant named for the river</note> (2) in beer. You have the woman drink it in the morning (3) before she has eaten, and it will stop.

(4) The way to know if a woman is pregnant: you have (5) the woman pass her water <note>urine</note> upon this same plant mentioned above again (6) in the evening or the morning. Then you examine the plant: (7) if it is withered, she is not pregnant; if you find it (8) flourishing, she is pregnant. <note>This "wheat and barley" style pregnancy test is a famous feature of Egyptian medicine, appearing in papyri dating back to the Middle Kingdom.</note>

(9) A medicine to stop bleeding: leaf of <term>shysha</term> <note>an unidentified plant</note>; (10) leaf of "fly-copper" plant <note>original: "hmt-af"; possibly a literal name for a plant used to treat insect bites or one that resembles a fly</note> which is found (11) with you. ### VERSO COL. V.

->VERSO COL. V.<-

(1) A medicine to stop bleeding: juice of the <term>Great Nile</term> plant <note>original: "mw n hapy-aa." This likely refers to a specific succulent or water plant named after the personification of the Nile's fertility.</note> (2) together with beer. You have the woman drink it in the morning (3) before she has eaten; then the bleeding stops.

(4) The way to know if a woman is <term>pregnant</term> <note>original: "enceinte." The Demotic uses a specific grammatical formula common in medical texts to introduce a diagnostic test.</note>: You have the woman (5) pass her water <note>urinate</note> upon this herb (the one mentioned above) (6) in the evening. When morning comes, if you find the plant (7) <term>withered</term> <note>original: "scorched."</note>, she will not conceive; if you find it (8) flourishing, she will conceive. <note>Testing urine on plants was a hallmark of Egyptian medical science for millennia.</note>

(9) A medicine to stop bleeding: leaf of <term>sheisha</term> <note>an unidentified medicinal plant.</note>, (10) fresh leaf of the <term>Fly-Bronze</term> plant <note>original: "hmt-af." This may be a literal translation of a foreign plant name, possibly the "corn-bluebottle," noted for its metallic sheen.</note>. Pound these, apply (11) them to yourself, and you lie with the woman. Another: myrrh, (12) garlic, and the gall of a gazelle. Pound these with (13) <term>old scented wine</term> <note>original: "rp s n sty," equivalent to the Greek "oinos euodes." Aged, fragrant wine was frequently used as a solvent for potent ingredients.</note>. Apply it to yourself and lie with her.

(14) <term>Asphodel</term> <note>original: "Asphodelos."</note>, (15) otherwise called <term>wild onion</term> <note>original: "chelkebe," a bulbous plant similar to a leek.</note>.

### VERSO COL. VI.

1. A medicine to stop the flow of fluid from a woman. The first of the medicines: oil which is <unclear>applied</unclear> to her for two days.
2. Aside from the second day: a second treatment. <term>White lead</term> <note>original: "psymytsy," from the Greek "psimythion." Lead carbonate was frequently used in antiquity as a drying agent for wounds and skin conditions.</note> which you should grind with a little <term>red lead</term> <note>original: "’nzyr," from the Greek "sandarake," a red arsenic or lead mineral.</note> from the oil-seller.

4. or a bandage of linen that is thin. You soak it in this medicine so it moistens the <term>vulva</term> <note>original: "s·t-eywe·t," literally "the place of washing," a common Egyptian euphemism for the female genitalia.</note> after she has
5. washed with fine wine. You put the medicine-soaked cloth inside her and you insert it from the outside in
6. into her <term>womb</term> <note>original: "ꜥte·t."</note> for a moment, in the manner of the <unclear>organ</unclear> of a man, until the medicine
7. penetrates. You bring it out and leave her until evening. When evening comes, you should prepare a <term>honey-plug</term> <note>original: "qlme·t n ’by," a medicated suppository or pessary made with honey used to carry active ingredients.</note>
8. of real honey, and you put it inside her until morning, until the third day—another saying: the fourth day.

### COL. VI.

1. 1. The last signs must be *hw II*, ‘two days’ : cf. l. 2. The group before this is unusual. The first sign may be **T**, the uterus, reading *k·t* or *ḥm·t(?)* (Kahun Papyrus V. 2 note), and the last two might stand for *n-s*, ‘to her,’ or for *n mn*, ‘daily.’

### VERSO COL. VI.

(1) A remedy to cure fluid in a woman. The first remedy: salt and oil; pound them; apply to the <term>vulva</term> <note>original: "s·t-eywe·t," literally "the place of washing," a common Egyptian euphemism.</note> daily for two days.
(2) After the two days, the second remedy: <term>white lead</term> <note>original: "psymytsy," from the Greek "psimythion." Lead carbonate was used as a drying agent and to treat inflammation.</note>. You should pound it with a little <term>pigment</term> <note>original: "’nzyr," likely red lead or sandarake, a mineral pigment.</note> from an oil-dealer (3) very carefully. Add true oil of fine quality to it, together with an egg, and pound them together. You take a strip (4) of fine-spun linen cloth; you dip it in this medicine. She must bathe in the bathhouse, and she must (5) wash with good wine. You put the medicated strip on her. You draw it in and (6) out of her vulva for a short time, in the manner of the <term>phallus</term> of a man, until the medicine (7) spreads. You remove it and leave her until evening. When evening comes, you dip a <term>bandage</term> in genuine honey, (8) and you put it on her until morning. Do this for three, or otherwise said four, days.

### VERSO COL. VII.

(1) Another [remedy] to follow it: juice of a <term>cucumber</term> which has been rubbed down, one <unclear>ladleful</unclear>. Water from the "ears" of a <term>kle-animal</term> <note>original: "ql-ꜥ." This likely refers to a puppy or a small mammal; the "water of the ears" may be a specific secretion or a metaphorical name for a plant extract.</note>, one <unclear>ladleful</unclear>, like the ladle (2) of a wine-cup. You add a <term>uteh-measure</term> <note>An ancient Egyptian unit of volume.</note> of good wine to them. She drinks it at midday, before she has (3) eaten anything whatever, after bathing in the bathhouse as she has done before. When evening comes, you put the <term>cloth</term> (4) with honey on her as described above for seven days.

---

<note>Historical Note: As the scholar Revillout <note>Eugene Revillout, a 19th-century specialist in Demotic law and texts.</note> pointed out, the word "psymytsy" is the Greek "psimythion," or white lead. This identification is supported by Zoega <note>Georg Zoega, a pioneering scholar of Coptic and Egyptian antiquities.</note> on page 626 of his works, and by the ancient Greek physician Dioscorides <note>Author of "De Materia Medica," the primary pharmacological text of antiquity.</note> in book 5, chapter 103.</note>

### VERSO COL. VIII.

(1) **Treatment for Gout** <note>original: "ποδακραν" (podagran). A painful form of inflammatory arthritis, often affecting the base of the big toe.</note>
(2) You cause the man to sit and you apply a medicinal plaster to the foot of the man. (3) You should recite [the spell?] ... after it upon his foot and his limb. You ask (4) the man, saying to him: "Has it been heard?" <note>In ancient medical contexts, "hearing" often meant the patient feeling the medicine's effect or the pain subsiding.</note> Do this for 3 days. After this, you bring <term>burr-reed</term> <note>original: "qpqp." Likely an astringent plant material.</note>. (5) You boil it in <term>henna oil</term> <note>original: "nhe n qwpre," from the Greek "elaion kyprinon." See the translator's note below.</note>. You anoint his foot (6) with it. You should stop. You bring <term>Alexandrian figs</term> <note>original: "qnt'e R'qt." Alexandria was a major center for trade and specialized produce.</note>, dried raisins, (7) and <term>hand-plant</term> <note>original: "sym n gyz," glossed in Greek as "pentadaktulos" (five-fingered). This is the Cinquefoil (Potentilla).</note>. You grind them with wine. You smear it on the outside (8) of these [parts] and you blow upon it with your mouth.

### VERSO COL. IX.

(1) Another [recipe]:
(2) **Euphorbia** <note>original: "ευφορβιου." A potent plant resin used as a purgative.</note>: 1 <term>kite</term>.
(3) **Pepper**: ½ <term>kite</term>.
(4) **Pyrethrum** <note>original: "περηθου." Likely Pellitory, used to stimulate saliva or treat toothache.</note>: 1 <term>stater</term> <note>original: "sttr·t." A Greek unit of weight.</note>.
(5) **Autarches** <note>original: "αυταρχες." An unidentified botanical or mineral ingredient.</note>: 1 <term>stater</term>.

(7) With 6 <term>staters</term> of wine.

->COL. VIII.<-

line 5. *nhe n qwpre* = <term>henna oil</term> <note>original: "ἔλαιον κύπρινον."</note>, as mentioned by the ancient scholars Dioscorides and Pliny. It is made from the seeds or leaves of the henna plant (*Lawsonia inermis*). While the red dye from the leaves is a common cosmetic today, the oil was a frequent ingredient in ancient medical prescriptions.

line 7. *sym n gyz*: literally "hand-plant," with a <gloss>Greek annotation</gloss> <note>original: "πητακταλος."</note> which stands for "five-fingered" (pentadaktulos), referring to the **Cinquefoil** (*Potentilla*). This plant is still known by both names in Greece.

lines 7-8. *n p bl ny*: A specific phrasing regarding the external application of the mixture.

->COL. IX.<-

line 3. *πεπτερεως* for *πεπέρεως*, meaning **pepper**.
