<header>VERSO COL. IX</header>

Another treatment to follow: you take a new dish; you put (5) ten <term>uteh-measures</term> <note>An ancient Egyptian liquid measure.</note> of old sweet wine on it; you put a half-<term>kite</term> <note>A unit of weight roughly equal to 9 grams.</note> of fresh <term>rue</term> <note>A bitter herb used in ancient medicine to stimulate menstruation or treat digestive issues.</note> on it from (6) dawn till midday; let her wash herself in the bath, and come out and drink it. When it is evening (7) you put honey on her as described above again for seven days.

### VERSO COL. VIII.

(1) **Treatment for Gout** <note>original: "ποδακραν" (podagran). A painful form of arthritis; in antiquity, it often referred generally to foot pain.</note>. (2) You make the man sit down; you place <term>clay</term> <note>Used as a cooling poultice.</note> under the feet of the man; (3) you apply [the medicine] to it, his feet resting on it; you ask (4) the man, saying, "Has it been heard?" <note>A common idiom in these texts asking if the medicine has "hearkened" to the spell or if the patient feels the relief taking effect.</note> for three days. Thereafter you take an <term>ant</term> (?) <note>The transcription is uncertain; this may refer to a specific insect used in ointments or a misspelled plant name.</note>, (5) you cook it in <term>oil of henna</term> <note>Derived from the Lawsonia inermis plant, valued for its fragrance and medicinal properties.</note>; you anoint his feet (6) with it. When you have finished, you take <term>Alexandrian figs</term> <note>Alexandria was the primary port for luxury produce and medicinal exports.</note> and raisins (7) and <term>potentilla</term> <note>Also known as "Cinquefoil" or "five-finger grass" because of its five-lobed leaves.</note>; you pound them with wine; you anoint him in addition to (8) these; and you blow on him with your breath.

### VERSO COL. IX.

(1) Another recipe: (2) 1 <term>kite</term> of <term>Euphorbia</term> <note>Spurge; the milky sap was used as a powerful, often irritating, purgative or topical treatment.</note>, (3) 1/2 <term>kite</term> of pepper, (4) 1 <term>stater</term> (?) <note>A Greek weight unit.</note> of <term>pyrethrum</term> <note>Pellitory-of-Spain; a root that causes a tingling sensation and was used to treat toothaches and joint pain.</note>, (5) 1 <term>stater</term> (?) of <term>adarces</term> <note>A salty, foam-like substance found on reeds in salt marshes.</note>, (6) native <term>sulphur</term> <note>Sulphur that has not been processed by fire, often used in skin treatments.</note>, 1 <term>stater</term> (?), (7) any wine, 6 [measures].

---

<note>Dioscorides, Book 2, chapter 188; and for its use as a magico-medical ingredient, see Wessely, *New Greek Magical Papyri*, page 25; and below Column V. 14/3; Sigismund, *Aromata*, page 41.</note>
<note>Line 4. "Perethou" is a variation of "pyrethrum," apparently a plant from the parsley family that is hot to the taste: compare Dioscorides, Book 3, chapter 78.</note>
<note>Line 5. "Antarkes" refers to "adarce," as seen in Dioscorides, Book 5, chapter 136, which is a salt-like crust found on plants in marshes. It is noteworthy that these four ingredients—spurge, pepper, pyrethrum, and adarce—are all found among many others in a prescription for gout given by the physician Alexander of Tralles, Book 11, page 628.</note>
<note>Line 6. "Dionaperon" is a Greek term for "native sulphur" or "unfired sulphur" (literally "without fire"). For its use, compare British Museum Greek Papyrus 121, line 168; Zoega, 626; the term "onaperon" in Kircher 203 referring to "red sulphur" is likely a corruption of this word.</note>

### COLUMN X. NOTES

1. 1. *Gouty man*: The word used is a direct borrowing from the Greek *podagron*.
1. 2. A similar method of dealing with gout—using inscribed metal and skins—is found in the writings of Alexander of Tralles, a 6th-century physician. These charms were as common in the ancient world as they are in some modern folk traditions.
1. 3. Tin is frequently used for magical purposes in Greek and Demotic papyri.
1. 5. *With the two feet*: This refers to using the feet of the deer-skin to tie the amulet.
1. 12. The knife-like symbol is a known shorthand for the Zodiac sign of Leo in the Stobart tables and other Egyptian astrological texts.

### COLUMN XI. NOTES

1. 10. *Twne* is perhaps the reading of the damaged group of signs.

### VERSO COL. XIII.

1. O gods who are in **Egypt** <note>original: "Kmy"</note>, fill your hands with **fire** and **flame**, cast it into the heart of [Name] born of [Name];
2. burn her, or a spirit will . . . of her sleep, making the woman of the West(?) . . . let the house . . .

---

### COMMENTARY ON COL. XII.

l. 1. A brief note or variation on line 2.
l. 2. *Qwow*: Identified with the modern city of **Qau** (Antaeopolis). While related to the Coptic word for "mountain," it specifically designates a cult center here.
l. 3. *The plumpness* (*p 't*): Note that in the ancient East, obesity was a sign of health, wealth, and beauty, particularly associated with the grace of the goddess Hathor.
l. 5. *goes after*: The text uses various forms of the verb "to come" or "to go" (*'y-f*), comparing the compulsion of the lover to the natural instincts of animals like wolves and dogs.

<summary>This page contains a powerful attraction spell (Erotikon) that invokes the goddess Hathor and various Greco-Egyptian and Jewish magical names to compel a person's love. It uses vivid animal metaphors—wolves and dogs—to describe the irresistible nature of the desired attraction.</summary>
<keywords>Hathor, Shai, Abrasax, Iao Sabaoth, Qau, Antaeopolis, love spell, Egyptian magic, Pantocrator, binding spell</keywords>

## VERSO COL. XV.

1. The names of the gods that you are seeking <note>original: "whe-k-s"; possibly "you are worshipping"</note> when you would bring a vessel <note>original: "ᶜze"; a jar or bowl used in divination</note> to [a vase-inquiry?]:
2. **Maskelli Maskello Phnougentabaoth** <note>these are "voces magicae" or magical names, frequently found in Greek and Demotic papyri to invoke chthonic powers</note>
3. **Hreksygth-o Perygthe-o-n Perypegneks**
4. **Re-o-basagra Ke-zm O-basagra**
5. This name, you speak it over the head of a boy <note>Egyptian divination often used an innocent youth as a medium to "see" into the vessel</note> who is [motionless?] like a falcon because of the names
6. of the **Dioscuri** <note>original: "Dioskoros"; the divine twins Castor and Pollux, often invoked as protectors</note> who are in the vessel; and it succeeds when you recite them to the [glory?] of **Adonai** <note>a Hebrew name for God, "Lord," used here as a word of power</note> which is written
7. outside, while it performs a great manifestation <note>original: "bkᶜy·i"</note>, bringing a vessel to [the ritual].

## VERSO COL. XVI.

1. **Army-o-wt** (another version: **Areioue**) **Sythany Wthany**
2. **Aryamosy S-o-br-tt Byrbat Mysyrythat**
3. **A-ms-tharmythat**; bring **N. daughter of N.** <note>original: "mn a·ms mn"; a formulaic placeholder for the target's name and her mother's name</note> to **N. son of N.** <note>the practitioner's name</note> out from her houses
4. in which she may be, into any house where **N. daughter of N.** may be, she loving him, she being mad after him,
5. she providing the [desire?] of his heart at every time. You write these names of [burning?] <note>likely "fiery names" intended to inflame passion</note> on a strip
6. of fine linen that is clean, and you put it on a new lamp that is clean, which is filled with pure [genuine] oil,
7. in your house from evening until morning. You find the hair of the woman to put it in the wick [to make it burn well].

---

## COLUMN XV. NOTES

1. 1. The scholar **Groff** <note>William Groff (1857–1901), an American Egyptologist</note> has written an elaborate study on this column in the *Memoirs of the Egyptian Institute* (iii. 377); many of his readings are incorrect, but it remains very difficult to read and interpret.
*Seeking (?)*: the second sign is imperfect; it might read as *worshipping*.
*Motionless (?)*: Compare with line 5.
*Vessel*: A jar or bowl, often used for scrying.
*Vase-questioning (?)*: Can this be a trace of the word for "inquiry," implying a ritual of "vase-questioning."
Line 2. For a similar list of names, see the *Leiden Greek Papyrus V*, column 9, line 10.
Line 5. Can the word "because of" have the meaning "instead of"?
Line 6. The **Dioscuri** were the patron gods of sailors and were often invoked in magic involving water or vessels.

### VERSO COLUMN XVII.

1. A spell to bring [a woman?] to send a dream; another version for sending a dream again:
2. . . . . . . . . . . .
3. You should write these [names] on a leaf of a bay-tree <note>original: "gbe·t n ʾqyr"; the bay or laurel was commonly used in Greco-Egyptian magic for its association with Apollo and prophetic powers</note> and you place it under your head when you lie down.
4. It produces a dream and it sends a dream. If it happens that you want to do it to send a dream, you put it to his <note>the target's</note> mouth in a bone <note>original: "qs"; likely a human bone, often used in "binding" or aggressive magic to establish a link to the underworld</note>.
5. It brings a woman again. You write this name on the leaf of the bay-tree with the blood of a warbler (?) <note>original: "pa"</note> or a hoopoe <note>original: "korkoret"</note>.
6. And you put the hair of the woman inside the leaf and you put it to his mouth in the bone; and you write on the ground this name: "Release
7. [Name] daughter of [Name] to the house of the place of sleeping where [Name] son of [Name] is."
8. It is also an attraction spell <note>original: "agogimon" (Greek: ἀγώγιμον); a technical term for a spell intended to "lead" or "drag" a person to the magician, often involving restless spirits or gods of the underworld</note>.

->COL. XVII.<-

1. 8. attraction spell = *agogimon* (ἀγώγιμον). <term>REUVENS</term> (1793–1835), a pioneering scholar of Egyptian papyri, discusses this term in his *Letters*, citing similar examples in <term>British Museum Greek Papyrus CXXI</term> (a famous magical handbook).

These rituals utilize sympathetic magic—such as using a target's hair or writing on bay leaves with bird's blood—to influence the dreams of others or compel them to leave their homes, as well as a ritual to ensure truthful divine revelations.</summary>

<meta>This page continues a sequence of magical recipes from the "verso" or back side of the papyrus. While the previous page introduced rituals for inducing dreams and attracting a partner using botanical elements like bay leaves, this page elaborates on those methods using more intense components like mummy remains and specific animal blood. It also shifts into a ritual for divine revelation, requiring the practitioner to apply a specialized ointment to their eyes to see and speak with a deity.</meta>

### VERSO COLUMN XVII.

(1) A spell to attract a woman to a man and to send dreams; or alternatively, to experience dreams yourself.
(2) (A sequence of secret magical symbols or characters.)
(3) You should write these names on a rush-leaf and place it under your head; then go to sleep.
(4) It produces dreams and it sends dreams. If you intend to use it specifically to send a dream to another person, you must place the leaf on the mouth of a mummy <note>The use of a mummy’s mouth acts as a "portal" to the underworld, allowing the magician to command spirits to deliver messages to the living.</note>.
(5) It also serves to attract a woman; write this name on the rush-leaf using the blood of a warbler (?) or a hoopoe <note>original: "korkoret"; the hoopoe was a bird frequently associated with magical secrets in Egyptian and Greek traditions</note>.
(6) Place the woman’s hair inside the leaf, and place it on the mouth of the mummy. You must also write on the ground with this name, saying: "Bring
(7) [Name] daughter of [Name] to the house, to the sleeping-place where [Name] son of [Name] is."
(8) This is also a <term>lead-spell</term> <note>original: "agogimon" (Greek: ἀγώγιμον); a technical term for an attraction spell designed to forcibly "lead" or "drag" a person to the practitioner</note>.

### VERSO COLUMN XVIII.

(1) **Erotbithou**
(2) **Ektoula**
(3) **Errepheai** <note>These are "voces magicae" or "words of power"—nonsense strings of sounds believed to be the true names of spirits or gods.</note>
(4) "Reveal yourself to me, God [Name],
(5) and speak to me regarding that which I shall ask you,
(6) truthfully, without telling me
(7) any falsehood."
**The Recipe:**
(7) Saffron: 2 measures
(8) <term>Stibium of Koptos</term> <note>original: "stimmi koptikon"; a black mineral powder used as eyeliner, specifically sourced from the city of Koptos, a major center for trade and magic</note>: 2 measures
(9) Pound these together with the blood of a lizard.
(10) Form it into a small ball and dissolve it in the <term>milk of a woman who has borne a male child</term> <note>original: "rte n ms-hwt"; this specific ingredient appears frequently in Egyptian medical and magical texts, believed to possess unique vitalizing properties</note>.
(11) Apply this to his <note>the practitioner's</note> right eye. You should perform the invocation
(12) in front of a lamp or facing the "Shoulder" constellation <note>This refers to the "Foreleg" or "Shoulder" of the sky, known to us as the Big Dipper or Ursa Major</note> in the evening.

### VERSO COLUMN XIX.

(1) A spell for bringing a woman out of her house.

---

### NOTES ON COLUMN XVIII.

Line 8. **Koptos Stibium**: This refers to *stimmi koptikon*. This specific Egyptian export was highly prized in the ancient world for both cosmetic and medicinal eye treatments.
Line 10. **Milk of a woman who bore a male**: A very common ingredient in Old Egyptian prescriptions. The Greek equivalent is *gala arrenotokou gynaikos* (milk of a male-bearing woman), which was often used in conjunction with stibium to treat eye ailments or prepare for visions.

<meta>The notes below were provided by the original transcriber to clarify difficult readings in the Demotic text.</meta>

## COLUMN XIX.

(1) 2. *qbḥ*: The reading of the first sign is doubtful. The determinative (a classifying symbol in Egyptian writing) would lead one to expect *bḥ*, "tooth," but it is difficult to read it that way. *qbḥ* might be *koḥe*, meaning "tendon," or it may be the name of an animal intended to be drowned.

## COLUMN XX.

(1) 1. *yr·t*, followed by the symbol for "evil." This "bad eye" could refer to medical eye disease or the "evil eye." The prescription's use of oil and salt suggests a medical treatment for an infection.
(1) 2. Amon was the principal deity of Meroe (the capital of the Kingdom of Kush). This spell highlights the cross-cultural exchange between Egyptian and Nubian magic.

He finds my son Horus moving as fast as his feet can carry him, and he struck him (3) in his head with three spells in the <term>Ethiopian language</term> <note>These are 'voces magicae'—mystical words from a foreign tongue intended to command supernatural forces</note>. He finds [Name] son of [Name] and carries him as fast as his feet move, (4) and strikes his head with three spells in the Ethiopian language: Gentini, Tentina, (5) Kwkwby, Akke, Akha." (6) (Say this) over a little oil: add salt and <term>nasturtium seed</term> <note>original: 'hlyn'; a plant also known as garden cress, frequently used in ancient pharmacology</note> to it, and you anoint the man who has the eye-disease with it. <note>
This section identifies a plant ingredient as *Vicia cracca* (the common vetch), which was a common crop in Egypt. The scholar notes that this identification is supported by several ancient Greek papyri, including those found at Oxyrhynchus and Tebtunis, as well as Coptic manuscripts where the plant is called **araki** or **araka**.
</note>

<note>
Regarding line 7: The phrase "Egyptian grapes" may specifically refer to "black grapes." This is supported by similar magical texts where black grapes are used for their symbolic color.
</note>

### Verso Col. XXV.

7. anoint your phallus with it
8. and you lie with the woman. Very good. <note>The phrase "nfr sp-sn" is a traditional Egyptian scribal notation meaning "twice good" or "excellent."</note>

### Verso Col. XXVI.

1. If you [wish to make] the gods of the vessel(?) speak with you,
2. [to make] the gods come in, you say this name [to them] nine times:
3. "Ya-o, Iphe, E-o-e, Gynntathor, Nepher, <note>original: "yʿ-ʿo yph e-ʿo-e gynntʾthwr nephʿr." These are <term>voces magicae</term>, secret names intended to compel the divine.</note>

6. they inquire for you in truth: "Ng-o-ngetsyks, Mantou,
7. N-o-b-o-e, G-o-ghyr, Hor-o-n-t-r, Nt-o-ntr-o-ma,

### COL. XXVIII. <note>Translator's note on the manuscript's physical state.</note>

1. 1. This short column appears to be the only part of the papyrus written in a different hand from the rest. It is very obscure, and the words seem much abbreviated. The group elsewhere reading "property" or "thing" is conspicuous, but is without the usual identifying marks, and perhaps has another meaning here.
1. 2. Compare to Verso Column 10, line 12. The zodiacal sign <note>hieroglyphic scorpion symbol followed by a star</note> stands for Scorpio. For the reading of the term "Scorpio," refer to the scholarly research by Brugsch.

It also includes a brief, obscure astronomical reference to the constellation Scorpio written in a distinct hand.</summary>

### VERSO COL. XXXI.

1. Cicirwort <note>original: "cicirwort"; a magical name of power, likely used to summon or compel a spirit.</note>

### VERSO COL. XXXII.

1. To cause a [woman] to feel **lust** <note>original: "λιβε" (libe); from the Greek for 'frenzy' or 'passionate desire.'</note> after a man:
2. You bring a **donkey** <note>original: "εωωε" (eōōe).</note> while it is alive;
3. and you take its <term>testicle</term> <note>original: "cege" (sege).</note> out and you place it in a [certain] place;
4. and you take its <term>phallus</term> <note>original: "qeτ" (qet).</note> and you place it in another place. You
5. take all its hair, and you grind it very, very well.
6. When it is dry, you take a little of that which you have ground, together with a
7. little blood from your second finger—the "scorpion-slayer"— <note>The index finger was traditionally called the 'scorpion-slayer' in Egyptian medical and magical texts.</note>
8. of your left hand, and you put it into a cup of wine;
9. and you make the woman drink it; then she feels **lust** after you.
10. If you put its <term>testicle</term> into a cup of wine for the **donkey** [owner?]
11. instantly, or put it into meat or something to eat [it has the same effect].
12. If you put its <term>phallus</term> into a gold ring and you put it
13. on your hand, it gives you great favor, love, and respect.

### VERSO COL. XXXIII.

1. Concerning Horus... [as] he was walking up onto the mountain of the desert at the hour of midday,
on a white horse [and] a black horse...

all the gods sitting at the place of judgement,
3. eating [of the produce] of the Nile. My great lord said to them: "Horus, come and
eat!" Horus said: "No, I will not eat!" He said: "Rise up against me..."

### VERSO COL. XXXI.

(1) Sisihoout <note>original: "Sisihoout"; a magical name of power used to address the spirit of the lamp.</note> (2) otherwise known as Armiouth, (3) the god who lives, the lamp which is (4) lit: come inside (5) before me, and give me a true answer (6) concerning that which I am asking about here (7) today.

### VERSO COL. XXXII.

(1) To make [a woman] rave <note>The text likely refers to the "frenzy" of love or lust.</note> for a man. (2) You take a live **shrew-mouse** <note>The identification of the animal is slightly uncertain but it is traditionally translated as a shrew-mouse.</note>, (3) and take out its gall and put it in one place, (4) and take its heart and put it in another place. (5) You take its whole body and pound it very much; (6) when it is dry, you take a little of the pounded material with a (7) little blood from your second finger—the "heart finger"— (8) of your left hand, and put it in a cup of wine (9) and you make the woman drink it. Then she will have a **passion** for you.
(10) If you put its gall into a cup of wine, then she dies (11) instantly; or you can put it in meat or some other food.
(12) If you put its heart in a ring of gold and wear it (13) on your hand, then it brings you great **praise**, **love**, and **respect**.

### VERSO COL. XXXIII.

(1) Horus... <unclear>text lost</unclear>... he was going up a hill at midday in the season of verdure <note>The season of "Shemu" or summer/harvest.</note>, mounted on a white horse... <unclear>text lost</unclear>... on a black horse, (2) the papyrus rolls [of...] being upon him, and those of the **Great of Five** <note>A prestigious title for high priests in the city of Hermopolis, the city of Thoth.</note> were in his bosom. He found all the gods seated at the place of judgement (3) eating [of the produce?] of the Nile, my Chief.

---

->**Translator’s Philological Notes**<-

The phrase "he goes..." refers to Old Coptic parallels in the *Journal of Egyptian Archaeology* (1883 and 1900) which read: "He who is on the mountain at midday in summer."
For the depiction of the god Horus on horseback, see <term>Plutarch</term> in his work <italic>On Isis and Osiris</italic>, chapter 19.
Line 2. The word for "seated" (*hms.t*) is likely related to the Coptic word <term>smast</term> (used here as an infinitive) rather than the word *semsnout*.
Line 3. The word *hcp* is found in connection with the rituals of embalming in the works of the scholar <term>Brugsch</term>, in his *Thesaurus of Egyptian Inscriptions* (pages 893, 895).

<meta>This page continues the "Verso" or reverse side of the papyrus. Following the previous column's depiction of a mythic Horus riding a horse through the landscape, the text transitions into a potent healing incantation intended to cure a headache or fever, invoking the protection of Isis, Nephthys, and the 365 gods of the year.</meta>

### VERSO COL. XXXIII

(4) "[Is there] another who is with me?" says he who eats, while my head hurts and my body hurts... <note>This likely reflects the patient or a mythological figure speaking in distress.</note> to a mountain in the south... I stood.
(5) Isis will **heal** it as she comes; Nephthys will **heal** it as she saves... the sixteen Spirits of Vengeance <note>original: "Ne-tbew·w"; these were feared supernatural beings, sometimes associated with the 16 cubits of the Nile's rise or the fragments of Osiris’s body.</note>, the one of power.
(6) The [3]65 gods <note>One deity for every day of the Egyptian year.</note> who sit on high to eat at the edge of the field of the **Nile** <note>term: "Ḥᶜp" (Hapy); the personification of the Nile's life-giving floods.</note>, my Great One <note>original: "pe wr"; a title of high status or divine intimacy.</note>, while they are protected from the fire. 
(7) [at his head,] the son of Isis at his head, so-and-so <note>Placeholder for the patient's name.</note> born of so-and-so, from the fires of the night and the fires of the day, this pain of the head, this inflammation,
(8) this heat [of the fires]... at his feet. Remove it from the head of so-and-so born of so-and-so. As the sun truly rises, [it shall be healed].
(9) [Repeat] seven times. [Then you should] anoint his hand, his body, and his feet, and you should recite the spell over him.

***

The word used here for the **Nile**, which includes a divine symbol, may represent *Hapy*. This same grouping of characters is found in the *Papyrus Insinger* <note>A famous collection of ancient Egyptian wisdom sayings.</note>.

The phrase *my Great One* is difficult to interpret; it could mean "belonging to the Great," "son of the Great," or a personal address to a protector.

For the term *apaεi*: refer to the note for Column 1, line 20.

**Line 5.** The word *ne* is likely a future negative.

Regarding the **sixteen Spirits of Vengeance**: this may refer to the "sixteen giants" mentioned in the *Berlin Papyrus*. In Egyptian tradition, there were sixteen cubits associated with the ideal height of the Nile flood. Alternatively, some accounts state the body of the god Osiris was torn into sixteen pieces (though other sources, like the writer Plutarch, suggest fourteen or seventeen).

**Line 6.** The **365 gods** represent one for each day of the year.

<summary>This page contains a healing spell against fever and headache, utilizing a mythological framework where Isis and Nephthys intervene to protect the sufferer. It references the 365 daily deities and the symbolic number sixteen, linked to the Nile and the myth of Osiris.</summary>

<keywords>healing spell, Isis, Nephthys, Nile, Hapy, 365 gods, Osiris, headache cure, Egyptian magic, fever</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Verso" (reverse) side of the papyrus, specifically Column 33. It builds upon the previous spells which invoked Horus to provide a specific remedy for fever and migraines through the agency of the divine family—Isis and Nephthys—and the collective power of the gods of the year.</meta>

### VERSO COL. XXXIII

They said, "Horus, come, are you eating? Horus, come, will you eat?" He said, "Take yourselves away from me; (4) there is no [desire] in me for eating. I am ill in my head; I am ill in my body; a fever has taken hold of me, a South wind <note>Ancient Egyptian medicine often attributed illnesses to specific winds, which were thought to carry breath-borne demons or divine influences.</note> has seized me."

(5) "Does Isis [cease] to make magic? Does Nephthys <note>The sister of Isis, often associated with protection and the mourning of the dead.</note> cease to give health? Are the sixteen <term>Netbeou</term> <note>original: "n-tbw·w"; these are 'Spirits of Vengeance' or divine punishers, sometimes linked to the sixteen cubits of the Nile's ideal flood height.</note>, is the one Power (6) of God, are the 365 gods <note>One deity for each day of the Egyptian year, representing a complete cycle of divine protection.</note> seated to eat the produce of the fields of the **Nile** <note>original: "Hapy"; the personification of the Nile's life-giving inundation.</note>, my Chief, until they remove the fever (7) from the head of the son of Isis and from the head of so-and-so born of so-and-so <note>A placeholder where the practitioner would speak the name of the patient and their mother.</note>?"

"The fevers by night, the fevers by day, the headache, this burning, (8) this heat of the fevers... of his feet, remove it from the head of so-and-so born of so-and-so."

(Say it) over genuine oil (9) seven times <note>The number seven was considered a powerful number in magical timing and repetition.</note>, and anoint his hand, his body, and his feet, and pronounce the words to him.

---

The names of the great god mentioned here are also found in the <note>original: "Leyd. Pap. Gr. V. 4, 32"; referring to the Leiden Greek Magical Papyri, a collection of spells from the same period.</note> and the 365 gods in the same collection.

*In the field*: This phrase was erased in the original manuscript.
Line 7. *srrf*: probably an alternative spelling for *srf*, meaning heat or inflammation.
Line 8. *ne 'r* <note>followed by an Egyptian hieroglyph of a seated figure, possibly a deity or personified illness</note>.
*šte*: a word functioning as a participle to pick up the instruction of "removing" or "withdrawing" the fever after the long list of gods.

<summary>This page contains a magical incantation to cure fever and headaches by invoking Horus's own illness and his subsequent healing by Isis, Nephthys, and the 365 gods of the year. The ritual involves reciting the spell seven times over oil and anointing the patient's body.</summary>

## ERRATUM <note>Latin for "error"; a formal list of corrections for a printed text.</note>

->Page 40, note to line 2, for **kote** <note>original: "котε"; a word fragment in Coptic ending in the letter epsilon.</note> read **kots** <note>original: "котс"; a word fragment ending in the letter sigma. This correction likely reflects a specific grammatical or dialectical nuance in the Egyptian language.</note><-

<term>erratum</term> <note>a correction of an error in a previous piece of writing</note>, <term>philology</term> <note>the branch of knowledge that deals with the structure, historical development, and relationships of a language or languages</note>, <term>Coptic</term> <note>the latest stage of the Egyptian language, written primarily with the Greek alphabet and used largely by early Egyptian Christians</note>

->It has been found necessary to make some changes in the numbering of<-
->the lines in Leiden I-V, XVII, and Back Side Leiden III, VIII, XXII-XXVI.<-

## CORRECTION <note>original: "ERRATUM"; a formal correction of an error found in a published text.</note>

->Page 40, note to line 2, for **kote** <note>original: "котε"; a word fragment in Coptic (the final stage of the Egyptian language) ending in the letter epsilon.</note> read **kots** <note>original: "котс"; a word fragment ending in the letter sigma. This correction ensures the proper grammatical ending is recorded for this specific spell or instruction.</note><-

<term>Recto</term> <note>The "front" of the papyrus, where the script usually flows with the grain of the plant fibers.</note>, <term>Verso</term> <note>The "back" of the papyrus, often used for supplementary material or lists.</note>, <term>Concordance</term> <note>An alphabetical list or table used to compare different numbering systems or locations of a text.</note>
